Leif Ericson found land after making a daring voyage across the ocean in his small Viking ship.

OUR AMERICA 

LEIF ERICSON 

"He found a strange, beautiful land." 

Did you ever find something for which you were not looking? Well, that is the way America was found.

A long time ago, the rest of the world did not know there was an America. The people who lived in America knew nothing about Europe or Asia. 

Then one year some Norsemen sailed from Europe and found a strange, beautiful land. The story of Leif Ericson tells how it happened. 

Eric the Red stood up. He put his hand over his eyes and looked out into the west. The waves tossed his small wooden ship up and down. The wind blew through his long, red hair and beard. saw some mountains covered with ice. come sight. 

Far away he

It was a wel- He knew now that the tiresome trip across the ocean was almost over. He looked again, slowly turning his head. He wanted to make sure that land was near before he told the others on his ship. 1

OUR AMERICA 

At last he said, "There is Greenland. There is the new country where we are going to live." 

Before Eric the Red had finished speaking, a tall, strong boy jumped to his feet. He was Leif Ericson, son of Eric the Red. With him on the ship were his mother, his brother, Thorstein, and his sister, Freydis, and a number of other persons. 

They were all Norsemen, or Vikings. Many days before they had left their old homes in Iceland. They had taken some of their cows and sheep, and perhaps a few pigs. They had also taken their household goods and other supplies on to the boat with them. 

Then they had started westward across the stormy 

Atlantic Ocean for Greenland. About thirty other small ships had started with them. 

Brown Bros.

The Norsemen come to Greenland

LEIF ERICSON 

3 Waves as high as small hills had tossed their ships. 

Strong winds had blown many of them away from the rest. They had passed great, white icebergs floating on the sea. Some of the ships had been wrecked. 

Others had turned back to Iceland. Only fourteen ships had reached Greenland, the new country which now lay before them. The year was 985, almost one thousand years ago.

For a long time Leif lived in Greenland. He helped his father, Eric the Red, build houses and barns out of stone and sod. There was almost no wood in Greenland. The trees were small and thin. He learned to sail the ships and to steer by the sun in the daytime and the stars at night. He hunted and fished and learned as much as he could about Greenland. He grew to be a fine man.

From time to time other Norsemen from Iceland came to live in Greenland. They brought news of their old homes and friends in Iceland. Sometimes they brought news from Norway, which was the real home of the Norsemen. Leif's father and some of the others had been born in Norway. 

One day Leif decided he would go to Norway. He wanted to visit King Olaf and live in his court. At that time it was the custom for the sons of good Norse families to be educated in Norway. So he took some men and a ship and sailed for Norway. 

He brought the king gifts of walrus tusks and polar bear skins. He told King Olaf many stories

OUR AMERICA 

about Greenland. The king was glad to see Leif and to hear these stories. He asked a great many questions about Leif's far-away home. 

Leif lived at the king's court all winter, but in the spring he began to get restless. He planned to go back to Greenland. Finally his ship was ready. All of the presents given him by the king were safely aboard. 

Then Leif set sail. 

Leif was young and brave and a good sailor. So he decided to try something which had never been done. 

He started out straight across the ocean for Greenland instead of first going to Iceland and then sailing from Iceland to Greenland. It was a daring plan. 

For many days Leif and his men sailed westward. 

Their small ship was tossed by the waves and blown by the wind until they did not know where they were. 

It was a very hard voyage for the men, but one day they saw land to the west. Leif decided that they would go ashore.

This was not Greenland. It was some strange, beautiful land, perhaps the most beautiful Leif and his men had ever seen. There were green grass and wild grain and large trees. Wild birds and fish and game were plentiful. Although the Norsemen did not know it, the beautiful country which they had found was part of North America. They had discovered a new continent much larger than Europe. This was in the year 1000. It was almost five hundred years before Columbus discovered America.

LEIF ERICSON 

5 

Brown Bros.

Landing of Leif Ericson in America. 

When Leif finally returned to Greenland everyone was glad to see him. Even Eric the Red had begun to fear that Leif had been lost at sea. The people listened eagerly to his stories of the beautiful land. 

They wondered at his daring voyage straight across the ocean. Soon they began to call him Leif the 

Lucky. They were all proud of him.

Leif had brought samples of grass and grain and wood from the new land. The Norsemen in Greenland liked to look at these things. Some of them began to think of exploring the new country. Perhaps it might even prove to be a good place to live. Then, too, the very thought of adventure was pleasant to these fearless Norsemen.

OUR AMERICA 

A trip was planned. Thorstein, Leif's brother, was chosen as the leader. Eric the Red also went along. 

They were not so lucky as Leif. Unfavorable winds blew them about and kept them from going in the right direction. All summer they tried. They were blown back and forth. When fall came they gave up and returned to Greenland.

Several years passed, but the new country had not been forgotten. At last, Leif decided to find the beautiful land again. After sailing for several days he saw land. Leif and his men went ashore. The country near the ocean was flat, hard stone. Off in the distance the hills were covered with snow and ice. 

This was not the beautiful land which Leif had seen. 

Leif named the country "Hellaland," the land of flat stones. It may have been the country which is now called Labrador.

Leif was not easily discouraged. Once more his ship headed south. After a few days Leif and his men again came to land. When they went ashore they could plainly see that this was more like the beautiful land. 

The beach was covered with soft, white sand. Farther on there were many large trees. To this place Leif gave the name "Markland," which means forest land. 

Leif was not yet satisfied. He decided to look still farther. He headed his ship out to sea and sailed away. Several days later they saw an island on which they landed. From this island they went west until they reached the mainland. Then they sailed up

LEIF ERICSON 

Brown Bros. 

A statue of Leif Ericson a river which ran into the sea. They had found a truly beautiful land this time.

It was so pleasant that Leif and his men decided to spend the winter. Soon they had built a large house. 

Then they began to explore the near-by country. The men were divided into two groups. They were to take turns exploring. While one group guarded the house

OUR AMERICA 

and the ship, the other was to see what could be found in the new land.

On one of these trips great bunches of wild grapes were found growing on vines. There were so many of them that one day Leif said, "We shall call this country we have found Vinland." This means wine land. 

Many people think that the place Leif called Vinland was a part of the United States. Later, when 

Leif returned to Greenland with new stories of the beautiful land, this name was changed to Vinland the Good.

It was, indeed, a good land for the Norsemen that winter. They had been used to snow and ice and biting cold winds, and long nights and short days. In 

Vinland the grass stayed green until late in the fall. 

It was seldom cold. The river did not freeze over. The days and nights were more nearly of the same length. 

With the coming of spring Leif was busy preparing for his return to Greenland. The ship was loaded with some of the wild grapes and wild grain which had been saved. Some large logs which had been cut during the winter were also put on the ship. Once 

Leif and his men set sail it was not long before they arrived safely in Greenland. What exciting tales they told about their adventures in Vinland!

This was Leif's last voyage to the new land which he had discovered. Many of his friends, who had never been there, were eager to go. Among them was 

Thorwald, Leif's younger brother who had been born

LEIF ERICSON 

in Greenland. Perhaps he wished to see, as well as to hear about, the beauties of Vinland. Perhaps he and some of the other young men wanted to have adventures. Then they, too, could tell stories during the 

Ewing Galloway

This Viking ship in a museum in Norway is over a thousand years old. It is about one hundred feet long. long winter nights of Greenland. There were also many who wanted to make a place to live in the new land. The grapes and the grain and the forests seemed very good to them.

The old Norse stories which tell us of these things are called sagas. They say that the next spring over

OUR AMERICA 

one hundred persons left Greenland to go to Vinland.

Some of the men took their wives along. Freydis, Leif's sister, went with her husband. So did Gudrid, whose baby boy was born soon after they had found a likely looking place to settle in the new land. The saga says that he was named Snorri. 

The little colony of Norsemen soon began to have a difficult time. The winter was very cold. They ran short of food. For a while they could not even catch fish or hunt wild game. They had to kill some of the cattle which had been brought with them. Before long many of them began to quarrel among themselves. There was no strong leader whom all would obey.

Somehow they managed to get through the winter. 

No one was very well satisfied with the country where they had landed. Some wished to go farther south. They wanted to search for a better land such as Leif had described. Others wanted to go farther north so that they would be nearer to Greenland. At last the little colony split up. One small group took a ship and turned toward the north. The rest went farther south.

Days and weeks slipped by rapidly. The Norsemen who had turned south soon found a good place. 

There was plenty of sweet, green grass for their cattle. Wild grain could be had for the taking. Great numbers of fish lived in the streams. Grapevines grew everywhere in the shady places. Large trees

LEIF ERICSON 

11 were all about them. At last, they had reached a country like the Vinland about which Leif had told them. But they also found something else which Leif had not seen. 

One day the Norsemen were greatly surprised to see some strange-looking people paddling toward them in small boats. Soon the strangers stopped.

They had brown skins and black hair. Their cheek bones were high. Their dark eyes seemed to see everything. They were almost as tall as the Norsemen but not so heavy.

For a little while the Norsemen and the strangers watched each other. Neither quite knew what to make of the other. Soon the strangers paddled off in their little boats as mysteriously as they had appeared.

The Norsemen called the strangers Skraelings, but they were probably Indians. Not until the next spring did the Norsemen see the Skraelings again. 

Then they came to the little settlement with some skins and furs. They traded these to the Norsemen for little pieces of bright red cloth.

The Skraelings wanted to trade for some of the swords and spears they saw. The Norsemen would not allow this. The trading continued for some time. 

The pieces of red cloth kept getting smaller and smaller. Neither could understand the other's language so they had to use sign language. Before long the Norsemen and the Skraelings both began to be

OUR AMERICA 

afraid they were getting the worst of the trading. 

One day while they were trading, the Skraelings were badly frightened. A bull, which belonged to the 

Norsemen, came running out of the woods straight toward the traders. The bull was bellowing loudly. 

Never before had the Skraelings seen or heard such an animal. They turned and ran to their boats and paddled away as fast as they could.

About a month later the Skraelings came again, but not to trade. This time they began to attack the 

Norsemen. A fierce fight followed, but the Skraelings were driven away. A little later the Norsemen were attacked again. This time Thorwald was killed. 

This trouble with the Skraelings again upset the little colony of Norsemen. Once more they began to quarrel among themselves. Some of them wanted to remain. Others wanted to leave this dangerous place and return to Greenland.

At last they began to fight among themselves, and many were killed. Those who were left, loaded their ships and sailed for Greenland. One of the ships was so full of worm holes that it sank on the way. Only a few of those who had started out so bravely got back to Greenland.

It was a sad ending for the first settlement ever to be made in America. Never again did the Norsemen try to build homes in Vinland. For a while they continued to visit the new land whenever they needed a load of logs. Soon these trips were no longer made.

LEIF ERICSON 

13 Then as the years went by, one by one, the Norsemen visited the beautiful land only when some old, old story-teller took them back on a long winter evening with a stirring tale of Leif the Lucky and Vinland the Good.

The sailors wanted to turn back but Columbus was not afraid to sail on.

II 

CHRISTOPHER COLUMBUS 

"He wanted to find the east by sailing west."

After the trips of the Norsemen to the strange, beautiful land, almost five hundred years passed before it was found again. This time, too, America was found by an accident. 

When Christopher Columbus sailed from Spain in 1492, he was looking for a new way from Europe to Asia. What he really found was something quite different. "Are you very tired, Diego, my son?" asked the man. "Only a little, father," replied the boy bravely. "Is it much farther?" "I do not know how far it is to the next town. Perhaps on the other side of that hill is a place where we can spend the night. We must go on yet a little farther."

The man and the boy stopped to rest for a moment. 

They had walked many miles since morning. The sun with its fierce heat had beaten down upon them all day. There had been many hills to climb. In 15

OUR AMERICA 

places the dust had been more than an inch deep. 

The man looked wearily at the long hill ahead of them. Then he took the boy by the hand. They began to walk on, each footstep raising a little cloud of dust.

And so it was that they came to La Rabida. This was a monastery in Spain not far from the seaport town of Palos. Here they stopped and asked for a bit of food and a place to rest. The monks who lived in the monastery of La Rabida were kind and wise. 

They lived simply, serving God and helping men whenever they could.

The monks looked carefully at the man and boy and thought, "Surely these two are not common beggars." So they asked them to come in and spend the night.

That evening the man began to talk with one of the monks. "My name is Christopher Columbus," he said. "I was born in Genoa in Italy. My father was a weaver of wool. When I was a boy I liked nothing better than to see the ships come in and to hear the sailors tell their stories. When I was fourteen years old I became a sailor. But I did not want always to be a sailor. "I learned to sail a ship by the sun and stars. I learned how to read the compass and also how to read and make maps. I studied everything I could find about the oceans and the winds. At last I learned navigation, but even that was not enough. "It is good to know how to read and write and fig- 1

CHRISTOPHER COLUMBUS 

17 ure, but it is better to know more. So I began to talk with learned men. They taught me that the earth was round and not flat as so many foolish people believe. I studied their maps and charts and books. I read the accounts of great travelers such as Marco Polo, who spent so many years in China and Asia. I asked questions of the sea captains and sailors. My brother, who is a map-maker, helped me. My wife's father gave me many maps and charts and books. "The more I studied the surer I became of one thing. The great sea which lies east of Asia is the same ocean that washes our own shores. If this is true, and the world is round, why cannot a ship reach the east by sailing straight west across the ocean? 

That is what I believe. "Think how easy it would be for the merchants to trade with the Indies and China if this could only be done. No longer would it be so difficult to get silk and spices and rich jewels from the east. Yes, I am sure a ship can get to the east by sailing west. But there are not many who believe in my idea. "For many years now, I have talked about my plan. 

I have been laughed at and made fun of. Many people have called me crazy. If I were rich, I would buy a ship and hire a crew. Then I would prove that I am right. "I went to see King John of Portugal. I thought perhaps he would help me. I thought he might give me ships with which to make the voyage. But the

OUR AMERICA 

king's advisers laughed at me. I was disappointed, but I was not discouraged. "I went to Spain, and got King Ferdinand and Queen Isabella to give me a hearing. They were polite and kind. They seemed to be interested, but a Columbus before Queen Isabella committee of their learned men advised them against the trip. I then tried to get others to help me. Some of them were members of the king's court. A few of them believed that my plan was a good one, but something always happened to keep me from getting help. "For many years I have been trying to get aid in Spain. Now, I am going to see the king of France. 

Perhaps he would like to help me find a new route to the Indies by sailing west."

CHRISTOPHER COLUMBUS 

19 The monk, who was himself a learned man, was very much interested in what Columbus said. He begged Columbus to wait a while before going to 

France. "I know Queen Isabella," he said. "I once served at her court. I shall ask her to listen to your plan again. Spain must not lose this chance to find a new sea route to the rich cities of China and India.”

Before long a message came to La Rabida. Columbus was recalled to the court of King Ferdinand and Queen Isabella. This time he succeeded in making them believe that his plan was worth trying. Queen 

Isabella especially was interested. She persuaded the king to give Columbus the money he needed. 

It seemed almost too good to be true. The many long years of discouragement had been hard on Columbus. Although he was only forty-one years old, his hair had long since turned white. At no time had he quit trying. Now, at last, he was to get his chance. 

It was indeed a fitting reward for this man who would not give up and who had such great faith in himself.

Columbus' troubles, however, were not yet over. 

He had to get ships and load them with supplies. 

Then he must have sailors who were not afraid to go with him. At that time sailors knew very little about the broad Atlantic Ocean. Many of them believed that dreadful monsters lived far out in the ocean. 

Others were afraid they might never get back if they went too far from land.

OUR AMERICA 

00000 From an old print Many sailors believed that dreadful monsters lived far out in the ocean.

With the help of the queen and three brothers, named Martin, Francisco, and Vincente Pinzon, who lived in the seaport town of Palos, Columbus at last got three ships. Their names were the Santa Maria, the Pinta, and the Niña. The Santa Maria, the flagship, was the largest. It was only ninety feet long. 

Large ocean ships today are ten times that long. The 

Niña was the smallest ship. 

Columbus was captain of the Santa Maria. Martin Pinzon was in charge of the Pinta. Vincente Pinzon was captain of the Niña. Altogether there were about a hundred sailors on the three ships. One of the men was a silversmith. Columbus wanted him to test the precious metals he expected to find. Another man was a doctor. Still another was a man who

CHRISTOPHER COLUMBUS 

21 

The Santa Maria, the Pinta, and the Niña could speak many different languages. Columbus thought he would be useful in talking with the people who lived in the Indies.

Early one morning in August, 1492, the sailors made the ships ready. Soon the wind was filling the white sails, and the ships began to move out of the harbor at Palos. Many people stood on the shore watching the little fleet leave. They were wondering if it would ever be seen again.

Most of the men on board the ships were looking back. Many of them were thinking that perhaps this would be their last look at Spain. But Columbus did not look back. This man who wanted to find the east by sailing west was at last on his way. His face was

OUR AMERICA 

turned toward the west and the great unknown ocean before him.

A short time later they had to stop at some islands to fix the Pinta, so that it could be steered in the right direction. Again they went on. Each day Columbus kept a record of how far they went and of the things which happened. It is from these records that people were able to learn about what took place on the voyage.

For nearly three weeks the wind blew steadily from the east. Farther and farther west sailed Columbus. Some of the sailors began to fear that on this part of the ocean the winds never blew from the west, and therefore they could never get back home. 

Then one day a storm came up from the west. The sailors felt better. When this happened Columbus was very thankful. He thought it was a sign that 

God was helping him on this voyage. 

As the ships got farther and farther from home, the sailors again became afraid. Many of them wanted to turn back, but Columbus would not listen. 

Some of them talked of throwing Columbus overboard and taking the ships back themselves. Most of them were afraid to do this because no one but Columbus knew how to find the way back to Spain. So day after day Columbus wrote in his records, "This day we sailed on!" 

Everyone was eagerly watching for signs of land.

Once they saw some birds which Columbus said were

CHRISTOPHER COLUMBUS 

23 land birds. Several days later they fished up a floating branch with some flowers on it. Then they knew they were near land. The sailors were no longer frightened. That night a tiny point of fire was seen. 

Early the next morning, October 12, 1492, land was before them. How happy they must have been! 

Landing of Columbus

Columbus put on his finest uniform, a scarlet velvet suit. It was the uniform of a Spanish admiral. 

The crew took small boats and rowed ashore. Columbus gave the island the name of San Salvador. Today it is called Watling Island. It is south and east of 

Florida. The men knelt on the soft sand of the beach and gave thanks to God for their safe voyage. Then

OUR AMERICA 

Columbus claimed the land he had discovered for the king and queen of Spain.

Even though he found no silks and spices and jewels, he felt sure that he had reached one of the little islands near the coast of India. He did not know that he had discovered America. No one had any idea that a great, new continent had been found. No one knew how much farther west it really was to the rich cities of the east.

The people who came down to the beach to meet Columbus looked upon him with great wonder. They had brown skins and broad faces. Their hair was black and coarse. They wore little clothing. At first they were frightened, and remained hidden among the trees. When they saw Columbus and his men would not harm them they came out. They were very pleased with the colored beads and red caps which Columbus gave them. Then they gave Columbus some little gold ornaments. Because he thought this island was near India he gave these friendly people the name "Indians."

The ships with their white sails looked to the 

Indians like great birds from heaven. They thought the white men with their fine clothes and shining armor and waving flags were messengers from the gods. Never before had they seen such wonderful things.

The next few months were spent in sailing among near-by islands. One of these was Cuba, which is

CHRISTOPHER COLUMBUS 

25 southeast of Florida.

Columbus did not find the mainland where he believed the rich cities of India to be. The Santa Maria ran aground on one of these islands. Columbus had to leave the ship. On this island they built a fort. When Columbus set sail for 

Spain he left a colony of forty men there, but on his second voyage none of them could be found.

On the way back to Spain the two little ships were separated by a fierce storm. For a while it looked as if no one would get back with the great news. Then the storm passed. The ocean became quiet. Each ship sailed on, thinking surely the other had been lost. 

Columbus, in the Niña, was the first to get back. 

When a little later the Pinta came into the harbor, there was great rejoicing.

A message was sent at once to the king and queen. 

Then Columbus began the long trip to the king's court. First, he stopped for a little visit with his good friends, the monks at La Rabida.

Columbus was now a famous man. There were great dinners and parties given for him. There were long parades and cheering people. They stared with surprise at the brown-skinned Indians and the bright-colored parrots which he had brought back with him. The king and queen asked him to live at their court. Every honor was paid the man who had found a new route to the east by sailing west.

Three more times Columbus visited the new world. 

Each time he hoped to find the rich cities of the

OUR AMERICA 

Indies, but each time he failed. The people of Spain began to lose faith in him. One time he was sent back in irons. Another time he returned to find his friend, Queen Isabella, dead.

Many of those who had praised him now began to make fun of him. Where were the riches everyone had expected to see? What good was this land he had found with its poor Indians? Perhaps it was not even 

India, but some strange land.

Columbus never knew what a really great discovery he had made. He died in 1506, still believing that he had found India by sailing west. If he had only known how large and rich a land he had found, perhaps his last days would not have been so unhappy.

Never again was America to be forgotten, as it was for almost five hundred years after Lief the Lucky had found it. Before long Spain had sent dozens of ships and thousands of men to the new country. Settlements were started in many places, and trade began to grow.

Other countries in Europe, such as England and France, also began to explore the New World. Soon they, too, were making settlements and carrying on a rich trade. No, never again would America be forgotten.

Neither was Columbus to be forgotten, even though the New World which he had found was named after someone else. He is remembered as the

CHRISTOPHER COLUMBUS 

27 man who discovered America. Yes, but even more than this, he is remembered as the man who would not give up until he had done what he started to do. 

Never will the great faith and courage of Columbus be forgotten. How truly was it said by Joaquin Miller, a famous poet, that— "He gained a world; he gave that world 

Its grandest lesson: 'On! Sail on!'"

MAURICE ZUSMEN 

Governor William Bradford asked Massasoit and his Indian tribe to come to the first Thanksgiving Day celebration.

III WILLIAM BRADFORD AND THE PILGRIMS 

"They helped to start a new country.'

After Columbus had found the way, explorers from many countries came to America. Each of them claimed a part of the New World for his country.

For a long time Spain was the only country that sent people to live in America. Then, about one hundred years later, the first English settlements were made.

The first one was started at Jamestown, Virginia, in 1607. The second one began thirteen years later when the Pilgrims landed at Plymouth. 

It was indeed a difficult question to answer. No one knew what was the best thing to do. Should they stay in Holland where they had homes and friends? 

Or should they move across the ocean to the new land of America?

For more than ten years now a little group of people from England had lived in Holland. They had left their old homes in England for just one reason. 

They wanted to be free to worship God in their own way. 29

OUR AMERICA 

At that time only one kind of church was allowed in England. It was called the Church of England. 

Everyone was supposed to belong to it. But some of the English people did not want to belong. They wanted to separate from the Church of England and 

Brown Bros. 

Pilgrims escaping from England start a church of their own. These people were called 

Separatists. 

Life soon became very unpleasant for some of the Separatists. They had to worship at secret meetings. Their neighbors often made fun of them. Sometimes they were even beaten or put in prison. At last they decided to move to Holland. Here they would be free to worship as they pleased. 

Among the Separatists who moved to Holland was

a young man named William Bradford. He was born in a little English town almost one hundred years after Columbus discovered America. He joined the Separatists while he was still a boy. He was only eighteen years old when he went to Holland. As the years passed William Bradford became an important man among the Separatists in Holland. He was one of the first to be in favor of moving to America. 

There were many reasons why the little group of Englishmen talked about leaving Holland. It was difficult for some of them to make a good living. 

Their children were learning the Dutch language and the Dutch ways of living. After a time, they would become more like Dutch boys and girls than like English children. Then, too, their right to worship as they pleased might someday be taken away from them.

Of course, there were also reasons why they should not move to America. The trip was long and dangerous. There were savage Indians in the new land who might kill them. Perhaps worst of all, there was only one tiny English settlement in all of America. That was a little colony at Jamestown, Virginia. It had been started in 1607, about the time the Separatists were moving to Holland.

After many long talks they finally decided what to do. Only a part of them would go to America at first.

Each man was to decide for himself whether or not he wanted to go. Those who went were to start the

OUR AMERICA 

new colony. The rest would join the colony later. 

Less than half of the little group said they wanted to go. They began at once to make plans for the trip. 

First, they had to get permission to settle on some land in America. Then, they had to hire ships and 

Brown Bros. 

Pilgrims sailing from Holland buy supplies. There were also hundreds of little things which had to be done. 

At last everything was ready for them to leave Holland. In the summer of 1620 they went on board a little ship called the Speedwell. Their friends came along to say good-by. It was a sad parting. Even some of the strangers who watched the sailing could

not keep from crying. As the ship began to move away, those who stayed behind fell on their knees in prayer. With tears rolling down their cheeks, they asked God to bless the ship and their loved ones.

The Speedwell carried them to England. Here they were to be joined by a larger ship called the Mayflower, since some people in England also had decided to make the trip. Twice the two ships started out to sea and turned back. Each time the Speedwell began to leak so badly they dared go no farther. 

At last they decided to leave the Speedwell behind.

Some of the people had to be left behind, too, since the Mayflower was not large enough to hold all of them. When everything was ready once more, the 

Mayflower again sailed out to sea. A strong east wind filled the sails and drove the ship swiftly before it. 

Soon they could no longer see the land. This time there was no turning back.

About one hundred persons were on board the Mayflower, not counting the sailors. There were about sixty-five men and about twenty-five women. There were also about twelve children. Some of the men had left their families behind. Less than half of the 

Mayflower passengers belonged to the little group from Holland. The rest had joined them in England. 

But all of these people are today known by only one name. They are called the Pilgrims.

The Mayflower was a small ship. It was not much larger than the largest ship used by Columbus. The

OUR AMERICA 

ship was heavily loaded, and there was very little room. It was so low that the waves often swept over the deck. One day a young man named John Howland was washed overboard. He caught hold of a rope which had also been washed overboard. He was able to hang on to it until his friends fished him out of the ocean with a boathook.

It was a hard voyage for the Pilgrims. They were cold and wet for days at a time. They often had to eat uncooked food. Some of them became sick. One day a baby boy was born. He was named Oceanus because he had been born on the ocean. When little 

Oceanus Hopkins grew up he became a sailor. 

About two months passed before the Pilgrims first saw land. It was near Cape Cod along the coast of 

Massachusetts. Before anyone went ashore, a meeting was called in the main cabin. All of the men came to the meeting. They made a very important agreement called the Mayflower Compact. It was an agreement by which they would rule their colony. 

Each man could vote for the laws he thought would be best. Each man could also say whom he wanted to be the governor of the colony. All of them promised to obey the laws which were passed. After the agreement had been signed, they chose John Carver to be their first governor. This was very much like the kind of government we have today. 

When the ship was safely in the harbor, the Pilgrims began to explore the country. They wanted to

find a good place for their colony. Captain Miles 

Standish was in charge of the exploring party. William Bradford was one of the men who went with him. On one of these trips they saw some Indians and found a supply of buried corn. 

One time the exploring party was away from the ship for about a week. When they returned, bad news was waiting for William Bradford. His wife, Dorothy, had fallen overboard the Mayflower. She had been drowned in the icy waters of Cape Cod 

Harbor. 

At last the Pilgrims found a good place for their colony. The Mayflower sailed from Cape Cod to a new harbor called Plymouth. On December 21, 1620, a large party went on shore at the place they had chosen for their new homes. This "landing of the 

Pilgrims" has since become very famous. Thousands of people now go to Plymouth, Massachusetts, to visit the place where the Pilgrims landed and built their colony. 

The Pilgrims began at once to cut down trees and build homes. It was hard, slow work. The weather was cold and the men could not always work outdoors. Then, too, there was a great deal of sickness. 

More than twenty-five persons died during the first few months.

Most of the homes were ready by the end of winter that year. Some of the supplies on the Mayflower had also been unloaded. Early in the spring the Pilgrims

OUR AMERICA 

began to plant their crops of corn and beans. They planted them in some cleared fields near their homes. 

These fields had once been used by the Indians. 

During all this time only a few Indians had been seen. Once some of them appeared on a near-by hill. 

They stood and watched the Pilgrims for a while. 

When Captain Miles Standish saw them he made signs of peace. He wanted to talk with them. The 

Pilgrims did not yet know if the Indians would be friends or enemies. They seemed to be afraid of the white men, but the Pilgrims did not feel entirely safe. 

One morning there was great excitement in Plymouth. An Indian walked boldly into the little settlement. He walked straight down the street toward the meeting house. He did not seem to be the least bit afraid even though he was all alone. As he came toward the Pilgrims at the meeting house, he called out to them in English. "Welcome!" he said. Never had the Pilgrims been more surprised. He told them his name was Samoset. 

He said he belonged to a tribe of Indians in Maine. 

He had learned to speak English from the Englishmen who came to fish in the ocean near Maine. He had come to Massachusetts with the captain of one of the fishing boats. He had been visiting near Plymouth ever since. 

The Pilgrims asked him to eat dinner with them.

After dinner he told them about the tribe of Indians

which lived near Plymouth. The chief of this tribe was named Massasoit. 

Samoset also told them much about the country in which they were living. When he left the next morning, the Pilgrims gave him a knife and some other presents. These made him very happy. He went off saying that he would soon come back.

On his next visit Samoset brought five Indian friends with him. The Pilgrims treated all of them kindly. Still later Samoset made another visit. This time he brought along an Indian whose name was Squanto. He, too, could speak English. He had lived for several years in England where he had been taken as a slave by an English sea captain.

Samoset and Squanto told the Pilgrims some surprising news. Chief Massasoit was coming with his warriors to visit them. About an hour later the Pilgrims saw the Indians on a hill overlooking the settlement. One of the Pilgrims went to meet Chief 

Massasoit. He gave the chief and his brother some presents. Then he made a speech telling the chief that Governor John Carver wanted to see him. 

In a little while Massasoit and about twenty of his warriors went down to the Pilgrim town. Captain 

Miles Standish and some of his soldiers met them at the town brook. The Pilgrim soldiers saluted the chief. They took him to a house where a green rug and three or four cushions had been placed to receive him. Then the governor came marching in to the

OUR AMERICA 

Brown Bros.

Captain Miles Standish and some of his men marching to a friendly meeting with the Indians music of a drum and a trumpet. A small guard of soldiers came with him. The two men greeted each other and sat down.

After they had eaten, they made an agreement to be friends. They promised to live as good neighbors. 

They also promised to help each other if anyone else should attack either of them. This agreement was called a treaty of peace. It was the beginning of many years of friendship between the Pilgrims and the Indians. The agreement was never broken so long as Massasoit lived. He ruled as chief of the tribe for more than forty years after this first meeting. Many years after his death, the agreement was broken by his younger son, Philip.

The Indians helped the little colony of Plymouth in many ways. Squanto soon became one of their

most trusted friends. He showed them the best way to plant their corn. A small fish was buried with the seed corn to make the soil richer. He taught them how to tread eels out of the soft mud with their hands and feet. He showed them how to catch and use the fish in the town brook.

The Pilgrims also helped the Indians. Once Massasoit was very sick. A Pilgrim named Edward Winslow hurried to the chief's home. The Indians had given up hope for their chief. But Edward Winslow took such good care of the sick man that in a few days he was entirely well.

In the spring of 1621, soon after the meeting with 

Massasoit, Governor John Carver died. William

Bradford was chosen to be the new governor. He was still a young man, but the Pilgrims knew that he would be a wise and good leader. He was governor of 

Plymouth Colony for more than thirty years. Besides his work as governor, William Bradford found 

Brown Bros. 

Plymouth in 1622. William Bradford's home at the right

OUR AMERICA 

time to do one thing for which he will always be remembered. He wrote a book, giving the history of the Pilgrims. From this and other early records has come the true story of the Pilgrims. 

The summer of 1621 passed quickly. Soon it was fall and the Pilgrims began to harvest their crops. 

When the crops had been gathered, William Bradford sent four men out to kill wild game. The Pilgrims wanted to have a big feast. They wanted to celebrate their first harvest and give thanks to God for His goodness.

Governor Bradford also asked Massasoit and his tribe to come to the feast. The Indians were glad to join in the celebration. Some of them went hunting and brought deer to Plymouth for the feast. There were singing and dancing by the Indians, and outdoor sports and games for all. There was plenty for everyone to eat. Of course, that was as it should be, for this was the first Thanksgiving Day.

In the next few years more and more English people came to America. Some of them came to join the 

Pilgrims at Plymouth. Others started new settlements of their own. Many of these early settlers came to America for the same reason for which the Pilgrims had come. They, too, wanted freedom to worship God in their own way. Others came because they could make a better living in the new country. 

After a great many years there were colonies along the Atlantic Ocean all the way from Georgia on the

south to Maine on the north. Most of these colonies were started by people from England, but many people also came from other countries in Europe. Still later these colonies were to join together into a new country called the United States of America. 

William Bradford and a few of the Pilgrims lived to see many of these early colonies. But none of them ever dreamed that the little settlements would some day become a great nation. How surprised and proud they would be if they could only see America today!

IV BENJAMIN FRANKLIN 

"He did many things, and did all of them well." 

The Pilgrims came at the beginning of a great movement of people from Europe to America. In 

1720, when Plymouth was one hundred years old, the English colonies were still growing. 

A time was coming when a wonderful thing would happen to the little English colonies. They were to join together into a new country. 

One of the great leaders who was to help bring this about had already been born. His name was 

Benjamin Franklin. 

There was a brand new baby boy at the little home of Josiah Franklin in Boston. No one, not even the neighbors, seemed to be very much excited. After all, was he not the fifteenth child in the Franklin family?

Of course, they all loved little Benjamin, for that was his name. Father and Mother Franklin wrote down the birthday, January 17, 1706, just as carefully as they had for the first baby. 

Benjamin's father was a maker of candles and soap. It was not always easy to make a living for so 43

OUR AMERICA 

large a family, but everyone did his part and they managed to get along. When Benjamin was eight years old his father sent him to school. Two years later he had to leave to help his father make soap and candles.

There was play as well as work for young Benjamin. He liked to swim and to row boats and to play games and to make things. He had many good ideas, and he was often the leader of his playmates. Perhaps better than anything else, Benjamin liked to read. 

One of the books he liked best was Pilgrim's Progress. 

By this time most of Benjamin's brothers and sisters were grown men and women. He had two sisters younger than himself. Since he did not like to make soap and candles, his father put him to work for an older brother, James, who printed a newspaper. Benjamin liked this much better, but he thought to himself it would be much more fun to go to sea and become a sailor.

There was one thing about the printing office that Benjamin liked very much. He could get ever so many more things to read. Sometimes he would borrow books and sit up in his room reading until late at night. He was always careful to return them in good condition so that he could borrow more books. 

At this time Benjamin began writing some things of his own. He wrote some little poems. One of them was about the capture of Blackbeard the Pirate. His father did not think much of the poems, but he did

BENJAMIN FRANKLIN 

45 

Brown Bros. 

Franklin as a printer want Benjamin to practice writing. First, Benjamin would read something written by a good writer. 

Then he would try to put it into his own words. Sometimes he would write it over and over again. Before long he could tell his own ideas and use words very well indeed. He also became a good speller. 

Some of the men in Boston wrote little pieces which were printed in his brother's paper. Benjamin wanted to try this, but he was afraid his brother would not like what he wrote if he knew who had done it. So Benjamin tried to change his handwriting. 

One night he slipped a paper he had written under the door of the printing office. The next day James

OUR AMERICA 

found it and liked it well enough to print it in the paper. Benjamin then wrote several more. All of them were printed. They were liked so well that many of the good people of Boston wondered who this learned writer could be. 

But Benjamin and his brother did not always get along well. They had many quarrels. Sometimes when James became angry he would beat Benjamin. 

At last, Benjamin decided to leave his brother's office and look for work some other place. He sold some of his books to get money. Then he got on a ship and went to New York, but he could find no work. 

He started out for Philadelphia. He walked part of the way in the mud and rain. He went by boat the rest of the way. Benjamin was tired and hungry when he reached Philadelphia one Sunday morning.

He was only seventeen years old. He had but a few pennies in his pocket. He did not know a single person in the whole city. Twenty years later he was perhaps the best known and one of the richest men in all Philadelphia.

As he walked up the street he met a boy eating some bread. Benjamin then remembered how hungry he was and bought some bread. He got three big loaves. He put one under each arm, and began to eat the other while he went on his way down the street. 

Benjamin had not gone far before he heard someone laughing merrily. 

He turned and saw a girl standing on her father's

Benjamin Franklin eating bread on the streets of Philadelphia doorstep. The funny picture that he made with his three loaves of bread was too much for her. She could not help laughing at him. No one would ever have dreamed that this same girl, whose name was Deborah

The next morning he began to look for work. He was hired by a printer named Keimer. One day Mr.

He was looking for Benjamin. "I have heard about you from a friend," he said. "Will you do me the honor to have dinner with me so that we may talk?"

OUR AMERICA 

with the governor. Benjamin was still more surprised when the governor offered to help him start a printing office of his own. The idea seemed to be a good one. The next spring Benjamin went back to 

Boston to see if his father would lend him some money. Josiah Franklin was glad to see his son again, but he refused to help him. He said Benjamin was still too young to be trusted with so important a matter. "Never mind," said the governor, when Benjamin got back to Philadelphia. "Make out a list of the things you will need for the printing office. You can go to England and pick them out. I will give you a letter so that you may buy them." Not until he got to

England did Benjamin find that the governor was in the habit of making promises which he did not keep.

It was a great disappointment for Benjamin, but it taught him a good lesson. It helped him to become a better judge of people. He soon found work as a printer. He remained in London for a year and a half. While there he was called the "Water American" because he drank water instead of beer. The other printers laughed at this. But Benjamin knew how important it was to live wisely in order to keep good health.

Franklin returned to Philadelphia. He worked for a while as a clerk in a store. Then he went back to work as a printer for Keimer. After a while Benjamin

BENJAMIN FRANKLIN 

49 and a friend named Hugh Meredith decided to start their own printing office. Before long, however, Benjamin bought his partner's share. Then he married 

Deborah Read, the girl who had once laughed at him.

It was hard work to make a success of the new business, but Benjamin was never afraid of work. Often the people of Philadelphia would see a light in his office late at night. They began to think and say that 

Benjamin Franklin would be sure to succeed because he worked so hard. 

He made many friends. Some of them were very important men. They helped him get printing jobs. 

One of these jobs was to print a great deal of paper money for the government. Franklin soon became known as the best printer in Philadelphia. His newspaper was read by more people in the city than any other. 

Franklin still had as many ideas as ever. One day he thought of something which soon made him a very famous man. He began to print an almanac each year. An almanac is a small book. It has a calendar, showing each month and day. It tries to tell what the weather will be for every day of the year. Of course, no one can tell that far ahead what the weather will be. People liked to read the almanac anyway. It also contains wise sayings and a few jokes and facts about many different things. 

Franklin's almanac was called Poor Richard's

Almanac. It was supposed to be written by a man

OUR AMERICA 

named Richard Saunders. Of course, Benjamin was the real writer. He tried to make it both entertaining and useful. At that time very few people had books of any kind, but almost every family bought an almanac. Usually it hung by the fireplace where everyone could get it.

Perhaps better than anything else, people liked the wise sayings in Poor Richard's Almanac. Among them were these: "Early to bed, and early to rise makes a man healthy, wealthy and wise." "It is hard for an empty sack to stand upright." "A bird in the hand is worth two in the bush." "Never leave that till tomorrow which you can do today." 

Before long Franklin was selling about ten thousand copies of his almanac each year. It was also printed in England and even in France. It helped make him rich as well as famous.

Only a few people can do a great many things and do all of them well. Franklin was one of these persons. During his whole life he tried to make things better than they were. First, he tried to improve himself. This he did by reading and studying a part of each day.

He started a club where men could talk about important matters. Later, he started the first free library and also a school which is today the University of Pennsylvania. He helped to start a hospital,

BENJAMIN FRANKLIN 

51 and a fire company which formed a line from the fire to the nearest pump. Buckets of water were passed from man to man. The water was thrown on the fire by those who were nearest. Franklin also got the people of Philadelphia to pave and light the streets and to improve the police. 

He was also an inventor, and liked to find out about things. An iron stove which he made soon proved to be much better than the fireplace. It gave more heat and used much less wood. He invented a new kind of glasses so that people with poor eyes could see better. The lightning rod, which is put on house and barn roofs to keep the lightning from setting them on fire, was another of his inventions. 

The lightning was something about which he had often wondered. What was this great flash of light that could start fires and tear trees to pieces? 

Franklin decided to find out.

With the help of his son, William, he used a kite made of silk instead of paper. A door key was tied to the end of the kite string. One day a thunderstorm was seen coming. They went out into a field and sent the kite up into the sky. The rain began to fall. The lightning flashed sharply. Franklin touched his hand to the key. A spark jumped and he got a small shock.

Then he knew what the lightning was. It was electricity. News of this discovery soon spread all over the world. Franklin was more famous than ever. 

It was not long before people began to think, “A

OUR AMERICA 

man who has done all of these things would surely be a good man to have in the government." Soon Franklin became postmaster of Philadelphia. He did his job so well that he became postmaster general for all of the colonies in America. At that time the colonies in America belonged to England.

About this time Franklin's newspaper came out one day with a queer picture on the front page. It showed a snake cut into little pieces. Below the picture were the words "Join or die." Franklin meant that all of the colonies in America should join together. He thought this would make them much stronger. Then they could better protect themselves from the Indians who were beginning to attack the settlers. Many years later Franklin did help the colonies join together into a great new country called the United States.

Franklin was soon sent to England. The people of 

Pennsylvania wanted him to take care of their rights. 

While he was in England a quarrel began between the colonies in America and the government in England. Franklin did many things to help the colonies. 

He tried to make the English government see what the colonies wanted. When he got back to America war had already started between England and the colonies in America. It was called the Revolutionary War.

Many of the wisest men in the colonies came to a meeting at Philadelphia. This meeting was called

BENJAMIN FRANKLIN 

53 the Continental Congress. They were going to decide what to do. At this meeting the Declaration of Independence was approved. The Declaration of Independence said the colonies no longer belonged to England. They were to be a free country. It was adopted on July 4, 1776. That is why we celebrate the Fourth of July each year. It is the country's birthday. 

Benjamin Franklin was one of the men who helped write the Declaration of Independence. Although he was seventy years old at this time, he was still strong and healthy and full of fun. Many years later some one said, "Benjamin Franklin liked a joke so well it is a wonder he did not put one in the Declaration of Independence."

All during the war, Franklin helped his country. 

He was sent to France to get help for the colonies from the king of France. No better person could have been sent. Because of his great deeds, this wise old man was perhaps more famous than any other American. He was so wise and yet so kind that everyone loved him.

The people of France liked his wise sayings and his kind ways. When he went on the streets, crowds of people cheered and followed him. Thousands of his pictures were sold. Great dinners and parties were given in his honor. The king of France had a picture made of himself. It was set in a case with diamonds all around it. Then he gave this picture to Franklin.

The people of France loved Franklin. Dinners and parties were given in his honor. his advisers. At last, they said they would help the 

American colonies in the war with England. This made Franklin very happy. He had succeeded again. 

France helped the colonies mostly with money and ships. The French also fought with the Americans in some of the battles. Without this help the colonies probably would not have won the war. 

After the war Franklin could not return to America at once. He had to remain in France and help make peace between the United States and England. When the treaty of peace, or agreement, had been written he signed it for the United States. Then he was free

55 to come home. He came back, not to colonies owned by England, but to a free country for which he had worked so hard.

United States needed a good government. Again there was a meeting at Philadelphia. The wisest and best men in the country were there. Again Ben-

jamin Franklin, who was now eighty-one years old, had to go.

The meeting had been called to write a constitution for the United States. First, it had to decide what kind of government there should be. Some of the men wanted one thing. Others wanted something else. There were many long arguments. It was then that Franklin would try to get them to agree. He would get each side to give in a little bit, and soon the matter would be settled. 

The other members all respected this wise old man. 

He was the most famous man at the meeting, except, perhaps, George Washington. At last the constitution was written. 

While the members were signing it, Benjamin Franklin looked at the chair in which Washington had been sitting as chairman of the meeting. On the back of the chair was a painted picture of the sun. 

Then Franklin said, "All during this meeting while we were making plans for our new government, I have looked at that sun. I did not know whether it was a rising sun or a setting sun. Now I know. It is a rising sun.

He was right. The United States was at last on its way to become a great nation. And all of the wonderful things which Franklin had done helped to make it a great nation.

In the spring of 1790 eighty-four-year-old Benjamin Franklin died. Twenty thousand people followed

57 his body to its resting place in Philadelphia. When the news of his death reached France, the whole nation was saddened. The world always sorrows when a great life of friendship and love and service has ended.

ZUSMAH Thomas Jefferson writing the Declaration of Independence

THOMAS JEFFERSON 

"He had many great ideas.” 

When Benjamin Franklin was seventy years old, he helped a young man write the Declaration of Independence. The young man became a famous American leader who was noted for his ideas. great

Perhaps more than any other person, he gave to the United States the ideas in which this country now believes. The young man whose ideas are now a part of America was Thomas Jefferson of Virginia. 

When the Continental Congress decided that the colonies in America should become a free country, a young man from Virginia was given a very important job. Thomas Jefferson was asked to write a 

Declaration of Independence. Although Thomas Jef ferson was a young man, he was a good thinker and writer. Benjamin Franklin and John Adams and two other men were asked to help him. 

For eighteen days Thomas Jefferson worked. At last the Declaration of Independence was finished. It had been difficult to find just the right words for the things he wanted to say. Some parts of the Declara- 59

OUR AMERICA 

tion of Independence he had written over and over. 

Sometimes Franklin or Adams or one of the other men helped him with an idea. Sometimes they found the right word for him. But Jefferson thought of most of the ideas, and did all the writing. 

The other men of the Congress now began to study what Jefferson had written. Most of them liked it very much, but there were a few parts they wanted to change. This did not please Jefferson. He was afraid they might make too many changes. So Ben- 

Brown Bros.

Signing the Declaration of Independence

THOMAS JEFFERSON 

61 jamin Franklin told him a funny story to make him feel better.

After some little changes were made, Congress voted in favor of it. This was on July 4, 1776. The 

United States of America was born on that day. 

The first man to sign the Declaration of Independence was John Hancock. He was chairman of the 

Congress. He wrote his name in large letters. Then he said, “The king of England will not need his eyeglasses to read this."

When Benjamin Franklin signed the Declaration of Independence he said, "Gentlemen, now we must all hang together, or we shall surely hang separately."

There was great joy in all the colonies when the people heard that the Declaration of Independence had been signed. There were parades, and bonfires, and speeches in all the towns and cities. In 

New York the people pulled down a big lead statue of the king of England. They melted the lead into bullets to shoot at the English soldiers.

Best of all, the people liked the fine ideas which Thomas Jefferson had written into the Declaration of Independence. They liked the idea that "all men are created equal." They agreed that all persons should have the same right to live, to be free, and to find happiness.

The people also liked the idea that the government should help them to keep these rights. Then, too, they

OUR AMERICA 

thought the idea that the government should do what the people wanted was very good indeed. Before long every good American came to love those ideas just as much as we love them today.

But Thomas Jefferson had a great many other ideas. At that time there was a strange law in most places. It said that when a man died all of his land and goods should go to the eldest son. Neither the wife nor any of the younger brothers or sisters got anything.

Jefferson did not think this was fair, although he had been an eldest son and his own land had come to him in this way. He thought the wife and the younger children should each have an equal part. He got some of his friends to help him. They began working to get the law changed. 

One day a man said to Jefferson, "The eldest son should have at least two times as much as the rest." "No," answered Jefferson, "not unless he can eat two times as much or work two times as hard." At last the new law was passed. It was much fairer than the old one. Now, all of the children would have an equal chance.

Soon Jefferson began another fight to make things better for the people of Virginia. In many of the colonies the people had the right to belong to any church they pleased. They had freedom of religion. 

This meant that each person could worship God as he wanted to. In Virginia, however, the law permitted only one church.

THOMAS JEFFERSON 

63 All of the people paid taxes to support this church, even if they did not belong to it. There were also some laws which said that persons belonging to certain other churches should be put in jail.

Jefferson did not believe these laws were right. He thought that all people should be allowed to go to any church they wanted to. Each church should have the same rights as any other. There should be no taxes for any religion. The cost of each church should be paid by its members.

Eight years passed before the old laws were changed. The new law gave freedom of religion to everyone. Jefferson was in France when this happened, but the news made him very happy. He had been sent to France to take the place of his old friend, Benjamin Franklin. 

Like many other men in Virginia, Jefferson owned a large plantation, or farm. Many negroes lived on these farms. They did all the hard work, but they were not paid for their work. They were slaves who could be bought and sold or punished whenever the master wished. They had no rights of their own. 

Jefferson did not think it was right to have slaves. 

He said, "Slavery is cruel and shameful. No man should make a slave of another man.' 

He was very kind to his own slaves. He planned to set them free. He even had them taught trades so that they could take care of themselves. Some of them learned to weave cloth, and to make clothes and fur-

OUR AMERICA 

niture. Others became carpenters and blacksmiths.

Thomas Jefferson wanted to stop slavery. This was another of his great ideas. He worked out a plan to free the slaves, but he knew it could not become a law. 

There were too many people against the idea. But he and his friends did get one law passed. It said that no more new slaves could be brought into Virginia from Africa. Many years after Thomas Jefferson was dead, slavery came to an end. But that is another story.

Still another of Jefferson's great ideas was that every boy and girl should go to school. What a surprise this was to many people. In Virginia only the sons of rich men went to school. As for girls going to school, that was not even thought of. 

That was not all. He believed there should be schools in every part of the state. Taxes should be paid by the people who had property. They should pay taxes even if they did not have children. Then the money should be used to pay for the schools. In this way the boys and girls whose parents were poor could go to school just as well as the children whose parents were rich. He also wanted high schools, a good university, and a free library. 

Jefferson had always liked school. When he was five years old he went to a little school on his father's plantation. He learned to read and write and do arithmetic. When he became older, he went away to another school. Still later, he went to college.

THOMAS JEFFERSON 

65 He liked to read and thought almost as much of his books as he did of his horse. He also liked to play the violin. He got much pleasure from music and dancing, as well as from outdoor sports such as riding and hunting. 

Monticello, the home of Thomas Jefferson

One time, when Tom was away at school, his father's house caught fire and burned. An old negro told him the bad news. "Were my books saved?", asked Tom. "The books were all burned," said the negro, "but we did save your fiddle."

Many years later he had to sell a great number of his books. That was the only way he could get money to pay some bills. It almost broke his heart. He wrote in a letter to John Adams, "I cannot live without my books."

OUR AMERICA 

There were so many things about which Thomas Jefferson liked to learn. He was not afraid to ask questions when he did not understand. He found that was a good way to learn. An old lady who knew him once said, "I have never seen anybody who asks so many questions." 

He studied all his life, even when he was President of the United States. But he did not forget to take care of his health. He said, "No less than two hours each day should be spent in exercise. A strong body helps make a strong mind." 

Thomas Jefferson.

By Permission of Bowdoin College

THOMAS JEFFERSON 

67 When he was an old man he started a school. It was the University of Virginia. This was one of the things of which he was most proud.

Thomas Jefferson served his country in many ways. He was Governor of Virginia. He went to 

France to take care of certain matters for the United States. When George Washington became the first 

President, he asked Jefferson to help him get the new government started. When John Adams became the next President, Jefferson was the Vice-President.

Then Jefferson was chosen as the third President of the United States. He was President for two terms. or eight years.

Even though he was President of the United States, Jefferson did not change his way of living. He did not put on airs. He was still a common man like all of the thousands of Americans who loved him so much.

On the day he became President, he walked from the boarding-house, where he lived, to the capitol building. Both Washington and Adams had ridden in fancy coaches drawn by handsome horses. 

Jefferson would not take presents which were sent him. He did not want his picture to be put on pieces of money. The birthdays of the other presidents had been celebrated, but Jefferson did not want the people to celebrate his birthday. He did not dress in fancy clothes, or put powder on his hair. He was always glad to see anyone who cared to call on him. The poor

OUR AMERICA 

man and the unknown man were just as welcome as the rich and the famous.

Jefferson did these things because he believed that all men were equal. He did not like people to feel they were not so good as somebody else. More than any other great leader of his time, Jefferson believed in the common people of America. 

Many important things took place while Jefferson was President. But more important than anything else was the fact that he made the United States a very large country. 

At first, the United States only reached as far west as the Mississippi River. Most of the people in the 

United States lived in the states along the Atlantic Ocean. Now, more and more of them were moving farther west.

Already there were many settlers living in the rich lands of the west. The settlers put their grain and furs on boats. They floated the boats down the Mississippi River and sold their goods at New Orleans. 

All of the country west of the Mississippi River, as far as the Rocky Mountains, was called Louisiana. 

It belonged to Spain. New Orleans also belonged to 

Spain. The Spanish began to stop the Americans from coming to New Orleans. The American settlers did not like this. They needed the Mississippi River and a place to sell their goods. 

Then France got New Orleans and Louisiana away from Spain. Jefferson knew how much the American

THOMAS JEFFERSON 

69 settlers needed New Orleans. He thought this would be a good time to try to buy New Orleans and some of the land around it. Then the Americans could float their goods down the river and sell them. 

He sent two men to France to see if they could buy New Orleans. The French government was fighting a war. It needed money badly and offered to sell all of Louisiana. This was more than the Americans expected, but they were wise men. So they paid France fifteen million dollars for all of the land in Louisiana. 

This was in 1803. Jefferson was very happy. The new land of Louisiana made the United States more than twice as large as it had been. Later it was found that all of this fine land had cost only three cents an acre.

No one knew very much about this new part of the United States. Jefferson wanted to find out what it was like. He sent a party of men to explore it. The leaders were named Meriwether Lewis and William Clark. They went across the United States until they came to the Pacific Ocean. Then they came back and told what they had found. The story of their trip is a very interesting one.

The great ideas that Jefferson believed in are now the ideas in which America believes. They are a part of America. Without them the United States would be a very different country. 

Jefferson, too, thought more of his ideas than of anything else he did. Before he died, on July 4, 1826,
