VI 

DANIEL BOONE 

"He wondered what was on the other side."

When Thomas Jefferson wrote the Declaration of Independence in 1776, most of the people in 

America lived along the Atlantic Ocean. Only a few men had dared to cross the mountains and explore the unknown land to the west. 

And yet, one year before, a tiny new settlement had been started in the western country of Kentucky. It had been started by the greatest of all early western explorers, Daniel Boone. "Daniel! Oh, Daniel!" There was no answer. 

The woman called again. "Daniel! Daniel Boone, where are you?" Still there was no answer. She looked around. There was no one to be seen. Once more she called. At last, she went back into the little log cabin. "Where can that boy be?" she thought.

But all the time Daniel Boone's mother had a pretty good idea where her son was. There was nothing that ten-year-old Daniel liked better than to be out in the woods.

He liked to take his rifle and go out alone. He was 71

OUR AMERICA 

never afraid, and he always knew how to find his way back. Daniel was a very good shot and liked to hunt. Even now he did most of the hunting for the family. He would bring back squirrels and deer and other wild game.

Daniel's family had always lived where the country was very wild. There were only a few small pieces of land from which the trees had been cut. The woods were all about them. They had very few neighbors. 

Daniel Boone was three years younger than George 

Ewing Galloway

Traveling was difficult in the time of Daniel Boone's boyhood.

DANIEL BOONE 

73 Washington and eight years older than Thomas Jefferson. At that time most of the people in America lived near the Atlantic Ocean.

Daniel loved his home in the woods. He learned to walk as softly as a cat and to go through the woods without being seen. He learned to look out for 

Indians, and to keep his eyes open wherever he went. 

Daniel knew which berries and nuts were good for food. He knew all the little sounds and cries of the animals that lived in the forest, and could read their tracks and tell where to look for them. He could build a fire and cook his food, and find his way about even when there was no sun. 

Yes, indeed, there was much that Daniel Boone could do even if he could not read and write very well. 

He could shoot as well as most men. Whenever he shot a squirrel Daniel felt ashamed if he did not hit it in the head. He could make traps and camps that were difficult to find. He could walk swiftly, mile after mile, without getting tired.

Then, too, Daniel Boone knew a great deal about the Indians. He heard his father and other men tell about Indian fights. He learned some of the Indians' tricks. All of these things, and more, Daniel learned well. They were his lessons. The forest was his school.

It was a great life, and Daniel loved every bit of it. 

As the years went by, he grew to be a tall and strong man. When he was twenty-one years old he fell in

OUR AMERICA 

love with a pretty, dark-eyed girl. Her name was 

Rebecca Bryan. Soon they were married.

They decided to move farther back into the hills, away from the other settlers. Here Daniel took some land and built a cabin. The neighbors came over and helped him.

Near his cabin was a little field which had been cleared of trees. He planted corn and vegetables, but he did not like farming very well. So he spent most of his time trapping and hunting. The skins and furs were sold or traded for the goods which he needed.

From the door of his cabin he could see the blue tops of the mountains to the west. Daniel had never gone that far. He liked to look at the mountains, and wondered what was on the other side. Sometimes he thought, "I would like to go there."

Each year more and more people began to come to the part of North Carolina where Daniel and his wife lived. The new settlers began to cut down the trees. 

Daniel did not like to hear the sound of axes and falling trees. Before long there were many little cabins and farms.

Daniel now had to go farther and farther on his hunting trips. He began to feel crowded and restless. More and more he would look at the mountains to the west and think, "I wonder what it is like over there." 

Then, one day, a stranger came to the little settle-

DANIEL BOONE 

75 ment. His name was John Finley. He had just come back from a trip to the other side of the mountains. 

He had wonderful stories to tell about beautiful forests and bright skies and blue rivers. The land was rich. Wild game could be found everywhere.

No, he had never seen a finer country. What was it called? It was called Kentucky. In the Indian language this meant the dark and bloody ground. Were there many Indians? Yes, but they did not live there.

They only used it as a place in which to hunt and as a battle ground.

Daniel Boone listened to the stories, and his eyes began to shine. He asked questions and had long talks with Finley. They made plans for a trip to Kentucky. Four other men wanted to go with them. 

Daniel was chosen as the leader.

In the spring of 1769 the men said good-by to their families and started out. It took them five weeks to reach the top of the mountains. Then they had their first look at Kentucky, the country on the other side. 

It was every bit as beautiful as Finley had said it was.

They built a cabin on the banks of the Red River. 

All summer and all fall they hunted and trapped. 

The piles of skins and furs grew higher and higher. 

Daniel had never seen such fine hunting. And best of all, they had not seen a single Indian. 

Then, three days before Christmas, something happened. Daniel Boone and his friend, James Stewart,

OUR AMERICA 

were hunting. Suddenly, before they could fire a single shot, a large number of Indians ran toward them from all sides. The white men were prisoners. 

Daniel Boone was not afraid. He and Stewart pretended that they did not care. They went with the 

Indians willingly. The Indians liked Daniel's courage. They wanted to make him one of their tribe. 

Daniel Boone and James Stewart did not try to get away. They knew they must wait until everything was just right for their escape.

Seven days later they got their chance. The Indians were all asleep. It was late at night and very dark. 

The fire had almost gone out. Daniel Boone, who had pretended to be asleep, carefully awoke Stewart. 

They looked at the sleeping Indians. All was quiet. 

The two men took their rifles and began to steal away from camp. A single sound would have awakened the Indians. But Daniel Boone and James Stewart made their escape. All night long they traveled. 

By morning they were far away and safe.

When they reached their cabin they found the Indians had been there. The furs were gone. Their four friends were nowhere to be found. None of them was ever seen again. 

Daniel Boone and James Stewart decided to stay on. They built a little hut where the Indians would not find it. They started to trap and hunt again. A few weeks later Daniel saw two men coming through the woods. He found a place to hide, and watched them.

DANIEL BOONE 

77 He thought they were Indians, but much to Daniel's surprise, the strangers were white men. One of them was Daniel's younger brother, Squire Boone. They had come to look for Daniel and his party. 

One night James Stewart did not come back to camp. Daniel could not find a single trace of him. 

This frightened Squire's friend so much that he decided to go back.

Only the two brothers were left. They continued to hunt and trap all winter. In the spring they decided that Squire would make the trip back with the furs. Daniel was to stay in Kentucky and explore the country.

More than once that summer Boone ran into Indians, but he always got away from them. One day he was standing on a high cliff and looking at some new country. Suddenly the Indians began to come upon him from all three sides. He saw that he was trapped. Then he jumped over the cliff. He landed in the branches of a large tree sixty feet below. He climbed down the tree and was safely away before the Indians could get over their surprise. 

Later in the summer Squire Boone came back with supplies. Again they spent all fall and winter trapping and hunting. In the spring they went back to their homes in North Carolina. Daniel had been in 

Kentucky two years. He had already made up his mind to start a settlement in the new country. 

Daniel Boone's friends and neighbors never tired

MAURICE USMAN 

Daniel Boone jumping over the cliff to escape from the Indians

DANIEL BOONE 

79 of hearing his stories about Kentucky. They thought it was too dangerous a place in which to live. They did not want to take their families where the Indians were so warlike. But Daniel would not give up his idea of starting a new settlement. 

Several years went by. Daniel and Squire Boone, and a few other men, took their families and started for Kentucky. They had not gone far before Indians attacked them. Daniel Boone's eldest son, James, and some other men were killed. Most of the families were so discouraged that they went back. Daniel and 

Squire made new homes in a near-by valley. 

By this time many people in America had heard stories of Kentucky. Some of them began to think as 

Daniel Boone did, that it would be a good place in which to live. One man started a company to bring settlers to Kentucky. He hired Daniel Boone to cut a road through the forests and choose a place for the first settlement.

In a few months Daniel Boone's woodsmen had cut a road through the forests and over the mountains into Kentucky. It was not much of a road. It was really a trail for horses. It was called the Wilderness Road.

Then Daniel Boone and his men began to build a fort and some cabins. Posts were put into the ground to make a high wall of logs all around the fort. One corner of the fort was near a river bank. This made it easy to get water if they were attacked by Indians.

OUR AMERICA 

When it was finished, the men named the fort Boonesborough, after Daniel Boone.

Some men were left to guard the fort. Daniel 

Boone and the rest went to get their families and more supplies. Before long they were back at Boonesborough. Mrs. Boone and her daughter were the first white women to come to the new settlement. Soon other families began to arrive. This was in 1775, one year before Thomas Jefferson wrote the Declaration of Independence. At last, Daniel Boone's dream of a new settlement in Kentucky had come true. 

During the first year all went well with the little settlement. The crops were good, and there was plenty of game. Everyone liked the new Kentucky country. But the next summer there was trouble with the Indians. The happy, peaceful days were at an end. 

One day Jemina Boone, Daniel's eldest daughter, and two of her friends, Betsy and Frances Calloway, were riding in a canoe on the river. A little band of 

Indians watched them. When the canoe came near the bank the Indians rushed out and seized the girls. 

No one at the fort heard the girls cry out. When they did not come back that evening, Daniel Boone and some of the men began to look for the girls. After 

Daniel found the canoe and saw the tracks, he knew what had happened. They followed the trail as far as they could that night.

At daylight the next morning they went on. They

DANIEL BOONE 

81 looked for every little sign which would tell them where the Indians had gone. The girls had tried to mark the trail as well as they could. In some places they had dragged their feet. They had also torn little pieces from their dresses, or broken off twigs from the bushes along the way. 

At last Daniel Boone and his party caught up with the Indians. Daniel asked three men to go with him. Inch by inch they crept up to the camp. 

There were the girls sitting just a few feet from the fire. Daniel knew that if the Indians saw him they would kill the girls at once. At last they got close enough. They raised their guns and fired. Then they jumped up and ran toward the Indians, yelling loudly as they dashed into the camp. The Indians were taken by surprise. Two of them were killed. 

Culver Service 

Daniel Boone rescues his daughter.

OUR AMERICA 

The girls were brought back to Boonesborough, a little frightened, perhaps, but none the worse for their adventure. Three weeks later Betsy Calloway married one of the young men who had helped to save her. This was the first wedding in Kentucky. Before long the other two girls also married men who had been in the rescue party. 

There were many fights with the Indians that year. Again Daniel Boone was taken prisoner. Once more he made the Indians believe that he was happy to be with them. Blackfish, the chief of the tribe, decided to adopt Daniel as his son.

They pulled out part of his hair, a little at a time. 

At last he had only a little bit left on top. This they made into braids. They wrapped them with bands of beads. That was the way the son of a chief should wear his hair. Then they painted his face and body with bright colors.

Blackfish took him by the hand and made a long speech. Then he took Daniel to the river. Here he was given a bath to wash out the white blood. The other Indians brought presents. Some red feathers were put in his hair. He was given a new name, Black Turtle. 

The Indians were glad to have Daniel Boone in their tribe. He was a brave and famous man. Daniel also liked them. Although he fought the Indians many times, he never hated them. He lived with them for many months. He found out they were

DANIEL BOONE 

83 planning to attack Boonesborough. He knew that he must escape and warn his friends. 

The Indians let Daniel go hunting for them. But they always counted the bullets they gave him and made him give back what he did not use. Daniel soon found that half a bullet was big enough to kill game. He began to cut each bullet in two and hide one-half of it. 

One day he got his chance to escape, but the Indians soon found that he had gone. They sent their fastest runners after him. For five days and nights he traveled. He could not take time to rest or hunt for food. 

The Indians were too close behind him. Only once did he stop to eat. He walked and ran more than one hundred and fifty miles through the roughest kind of country. At last he reached the fort. 

The settlers had never expected to see him again. 

Several months before, his wife had gone back to 

North Carolina. She had believed him to be dead.

Daniel Boone began at once to get the fort ready for an attack. Soon the Indians came. The Boonesborough settlers were ready for them. The battle lasted nine days. There was a great deal of fierce fighting. 

Once the Indians set the fort on fire, but a sudden rain storm put it out. Then the Indians dug a tunnel so that they could get inside of the fort. More rain came and loosened the dirt so much that the tunnel caved in. When all of these things happened to help

OUR AMERICA 

the settlers, the Indians became discouraged and left. 

The next few years were full of excitement. More and more settlers came. One year, more than twelve thousand persons moved to Kentucky. No longer was 

Boonesborough the only settlement. Slowly but surely the Indians were being driven from the country. 

Daniel Boone was the one leader whom the settlers trusted. 

Again and again he barely escaped with his life. 

One time he was in a little house which he used for drying tobacco leaves. He was up in the loft tying some of the leaves together when four Indians came in the door. One of them said, "Boone, this time we have you. You will not get away now. You will not fool us any more."

Daniel Boone saw that they were from the tribe into which he had been adopted as the son of Blackfish. "Well, my old friends, I am glad to see you,' he said.

They told him to come down. He asked them to wait until he finished hanging up the tobacco. All the while he kept on talking to them. Then he quietly took an armful of dry leaves. They were covered with tobacco dust. Suddenly he jumped down among them, throwing the dust in their faces.

As he ran out the door he looked back. The Indians were coughing and sneezing. They were shouting and throwing their arms about, and feeling around in all directions. But Daniel Boone was no longer among them.

DANIEL BOONE 

85 ​Ewing Galloway 

Daniel Boone escaped from the Indians by throwing tobacco dust in their eyes.

Kentucky kept on growing. Finally it became a state. Daniel Boone was its best-known person. His many adventures with the Indians had made him one of the most famous men in America. He had done more than any other man to bring settlers to Kentucky, and to make it safe from the Indians. He has often been called "The Father of Kentucky." 

Then one day his land was taken away from him.

He tried to get help from the state government, but it would do nothing. He was fifty-five years old. He had been in Kentucky for twenty years. Now, everything for which he had worked so hard was gone. 

For a few years he went here and there, hunting and trapping, and fighting Indians. There were

OUR AMERICA 

many people in Kentucky now. Again he began to feel crowded and restless. 

One day he met three hunters who had been on the other side of the Mississippi. They told him what a fine country it was, with so much wild game and so 

Daniel Boone few settlers. Again Daniel Boone decided to move.

He took his family and started west. He went across the Mississippi River to a country called Missouri. 

Here he was happy, but not for long. Once more he lost his land. Then he turned to trapping to make a living for his family. He made long trips into the western country. At last, the government remem-

DANIEL BOONE 

87 bered him and gave him a thousand acres of land in Missouri.

On these long trips into the west, he saw the white tops of the Rocky Mountains. He looked at them a long time, but they were far away and he was an old man. "I wonder what is on the other side," he whispered to himself.

He began to plan a trip, but there were other plans for Daniel Boone. He became ill, and three days later eighty-six-year-old Daniel Boone died, still wondering what was on the other side.

VII JEDEDIAH SMITH 

"He knew what was on the other side." 

Just as Daniel Boone explored the "country on the other side," another fearless American explored the land west of the Rocky Mountains. He started on his first trip in 1823, three years after 

Daniel Boone's death.

In 1826, when Thomas Jefferson died, he was at Great Salt Lake, Utah, getting ready to start on his first trip to California. This first great pathfinder of the western mountain country was 

Jedediah Smith. 

There were many men who came to the country west of the Mississippi and looked at the white tops of the Rocky Mountains. Like Daniel Boone, some of them wondered what was on the other side. Most of them were too busy trapping to find out. 

Never before had a country so rich in furs been found. Great numbers of beaver lived along the streams and rivers. If a trapper had a good year he could bring back enough skins to make himself rich. 

Of course, there was always danger from the Indians. 

That was the kind of life these men liked. 88

JEDEDIAH SMITH 

89 St. Louis, on the banks of the Mississippi, was the chief town for the fur trade. It was also the starting place for most of the trapping parties that went into the mountains after beaver. That was why Jedediah 

Smith had come to St. Louis.

Jed was born in the state of New York. He had received a good education. Then he had worked as a clerk on one of the boats on the Great Lakes. Here he heard many stories of the fur trade in the west. 

He wanted to take part in it. 

General William Ashley was one of the most important fur traders in the west. He needed one hundred young men for his next trip into the mountains. 

So Jed Smith, who was not yet twenty-five years old, went to General Ashley. The general liked Jed's looks and hired him as a hunter.

In the spring of 1823 the party started out. They had two boats in which the supplies were carried. 

The men also rode in the boats. They went up the 

Mississippi River until they came to the mouth of the Missouri River. Then they went up the muddy waters of the Missouri. 

When the wind blew from the right direction they used sails. At other times they rowed or pushed the boat with long poles. Sometimes the water was too swift. Then the men would take long ropes and walk along the bank, pulling the boat up the river. 

It was hard work, and very slow, but Jed liked it. 

He spent most of his time hunting and bringing back

OUR AMERICA 

wild game for the men to eat. There was plenty of deer, elk, wild turkeys, bear, and buffaloes. Everyone had enough to eat. Sometimes the hunters found a bee tree, and then the men enjoyed honey with their meals. 

Early that summer Jed had his first adventure with the Indians. General Ashley had decided to stop near an Indian village. He wanted to buy some horses from the Indians. Then some of his men could travel across the country to the place where they were to trap.

The Indians seemed to be friendly, and General Ashley bought some horses. He picked about forty men, one of whom was Jed, to watch the horses during the night. They tied the horses' front feet together with short ropes so they could not run away.

Then the trappers built fires and sat down to watch. 

Just before daylight the Indians attacked. It was the first time Jed had heard the whiz of bullets going by him. He saw at once that the horses must be set free or they would all be lost. He began to cut the ropes which held their feet together. Some of the other trappers ran to help him, but they were shot almost at once. Jed then left the horses and helped in the fight.

The battle was short but fierce. Many of the trappers were killed. The others got away by diving into the river. They swam down the stream to the boats where the rest of the party had spent the night.

JEDEDIAH SMITH 

91 

Culver Service 

Jed Smith and the other trappers built fires and sat down to watch the horses during the night.

It was a sad blow to the trappers. All of the horses were lost except a few of those set free by Jed. It was not safe to pass the Indian villages in the boats. At last General Ashley decided to send for help. There was another party of trappers about two hundred miles away.

It was a dangerous trip through Indian country. 

The general had his men stand in line. He asked all who would go for help to take one step forward. Only one man stepped out of line. He was Jedediah Smith. 

General Ashley persuaded one of the other men to go with Jed. The best horses were picked out for the

OUR AMERICA 

two men. When it was dark, they started on their long ride for help. They did not dare to travel by day because of the Indians. The North Star was their only guide.

Several times they ran into small bands of Indians. 

Once they were cornered and had to fight their way out. Many times they did not dare to build a fire. 

Heavy rains began to fall, and for days they could not dry their clothing. They had to cross many swift rivers and climb out of deep valleys and ride across rough, rocky country. But at last they made it. In less than a month from the day he left, Jed returned with help for General Ashley.

Jed Smith worked for General Ashley for three years. During that time he learned the ways of the trappers. He became as good a mountain man as any of them. He had a great many interesting adventures. Wherever trappers gathered there were stories told about Jed Smith. There were stories about his adventures, about his courage and brave deeds, and about his fairness and wise leadership. 

One of the stories they liked best to tell was about 

Jed's fight with a bear. One day he was leading his horse through some thick brush. Suddenly he came face to face with a large, fierce grizzly bear. The bear jumped at Jed and bit him about the head. With both paws the bear struck Jed terrible blows.

By this time some of Jed's friends had come to his rescue. Jed was badly hurt, and none of the men knew

JEDEDIAH SMITH 

93 what to do. Even though he was suffering great pain, he quietly told them what to do as if nothing had happened. "One or two of you go for water," he said. Then he spoke to his friend, James Clyman. "If you have 

Culver Service 

Jed Smith's fight with the bear was one of his most dangerous adventures. a needle and thread, get it out and sew up the wounds around my head." Clyman got a pair of scissors and cut off Jed's hair. Then he began to wash the cuts in 

Jed's head and sew them up as well as he could. One of Jed's ears was almost torn off. Clyman said he could not do anything with it. "You must try to sew it up some way or other,"

OUR AMERICA 

Jed told him. Clyman did his best. Several of Jed's ribs were broken from the blows of the bear. After his wounds were taken care of in this manner, Jed got on his horse and rode to camp. Here the men stayed for several weeks until Jed's wounds began to heal and his strength returned.

Another story the mountain men liked to tell was about Jed's pet beaver. Jed was trapping along a stream when he took a liking to a fine, young beaver. 

Instead of killing it and taking the skin, he began to tame the beaver. At last the beaver became so tame that Jed put a red collar around his neck. How the men did laugh then! One day the pet beaver suddenly disappeared. Jed offered to reward the other men if they could find it for him. But Jed's tame beaver with the red collar was never seen again. 

In many ways Jed was not like most of the mountain men. He was very religious. No matter where he went, there was a small Bible inside of his hunting shirt. Some of the men joked with Jed about this, but he did not change his ways of living and believing. When one of the trappers was killed, Jed was always asked to say a prayer for the dead man.

There was also one other thing that many of the mountain men thought was strange. Jed kept a careful record of everything that happened, and of every place he went. At camp in the evening, Jed would sit by the firelight and write in his journal, or diary. 

Some of these papers have been lost or destroyed, but

JEDEDIAH SMITH 

95 others were saved. That is how we know about 

Jedediah Smith and his travels and adventures. 

It was difficult for the trappers to understand Jed. 

Here was a man who carried a Bible and could pray like a parson. Yes, but here also was a man who could fight like a wildcat and who had more courage than any of them. The trappers were sure of one thing. They liked Jed Smith, and they knew he could be trusted.

General Ashley, too, soon found that Jed could be trusted. More and more the general began to depend upon him. Jed became the leader of several parties of mountain men who worked for the general. In 

1826, the general sold his fur trading business to Jed 

Smith and two of his friends. In three years Jed had saved enough money from his share of the furs to buy this great business. It was something no mountain man had ever done before.

During his three years with General Ashley, Jed made several trips to the other side of the high snowcovered Rocky Mountains. Better than any other man, he knew what Daniel Boone had wondered about. Jed Smith knew what was on the other side. 

On one of these trips he found a great lake with salt water. This is now known as the Great Salt Lake of Utah. It is near Salt Lake City. How some of the mountain men must have laughed when he told them of a lake in which a certain kind of stone would not

OUR AMERICA 

sink. Perhaps he even tried to swim in it and found that he could lie in the water like a cork. 

Jed was more than a trapper and fur trader. He was also an explorer. Like Christopher Columbus and Daniel Boone, he wanted to find new routes and to see new country. He, too, was one of the men who wanted to know what was on the other side. 

At that time the country west and south of the 

Great Salt Lake was unknown. Jed Smith decided to find out what it was like. With a small party of men he left the Great Salt Lake in the summer of 1826. 

For days they traveled south and west. They ran short of food, and there was almost no game. Many of the horses died of thirst or were killed to provide food for the men.

After a hard trip he came to some Indian villages in what is now the state of California. He traded with the Indians for fresh horses and food. They told him about the Spanish settlements in California. 

Two of the Indians went with him to show him the way across the hot, dry Mohave Desert. Late in November, 1826, he reached one of the Spanish settlements. It was the Mission San Gabriel. The city of 

Los Angeles now stands only a few miles away. 

Father Sanchez was the head of the mission. He was very much surprised to see the wild-looking trappers. He was still more astonished when Jed told him how they had come across the country. No other white men had done that before.

Jed Smith and the trappers were welcomed by Father Sanchez.

OUR AMERICA 

He made the trappers welcome. He gave them food and a place in which to stay. He asked Jed to stay at his house with him. Father Sanchez treated the little party of explorers so kindly that Jed's friend, Harrison Rogers, wrote in his journal, "Old Father Sanchez has been the greatest friend that I ever met with in all my travels."

California then belonged to the country of Mexico. 

But most of the white people and settlements were Spanish. Mexico did not like to have strangers come to California. So Jed went to see the governor at San

Diego. Jed wanted to explain how his party happened to come to California. He also wanted the governor to let him leave California by going north into

Oregon. He gave the governor a present of eight fine beaver skins.

While Jed was away, the men stayed at the mission. The trappers were greatly interested in what they saw. The Spanish priests had built the mission. 

They wanted to teach the Indians to be Christians. 

There were many missions along the coast of California. Each was just one day's ride from the next.

The Indians came to work and live at the missions. 

The priests taught them to be carpenters, blacksmiths, and good farmers. The women learned to spin and to weave cloth. Some of the Indians learned to read and write. The priests taught them a great many things. 

Near this mission were apple and peach orchards.

JEDEDIAH SMITH 

99 There were many grape vines and a few orange and fig trees. The people had fields of grain with little ditches of running water around them. They had a mill where the grain was made into flour. There were large herds of cattle, horses, sheep, and hogs. 

About a thousand Indians lived at the mission. 

They took care of the cattle and the fields and orchards. They did most of the hard work. There were also a few Spanish soldiers at the mission. There was a large church so that everyone at the mission could go.

The Americans liked the easy life at the mission. 

They liked the pleasant sunshine and the warm weather. There was not much for them to do. One day a priest asked some of them to make a large bear trap. He wanted to put it in his orchard to catch the 

Indians who were stealing his oranges.

Before long, however, Jed came back from San Diego. The governor would not let Jed and his men go north along the coast. He said they must go back the same way they came. So Jed and his party began to get ready for the trip across the mountains. 

They said good-by to the priests at the mission and started out. After another hard trip they got back to the Great Salt Lake. Here Jed met some of his old friends. 

That year Jed made another trip to California.

This time he returned by way of the Oregon country. 

The next few years he spent in trapping and trading.

OUR AMERICA 

Then he sold his fur trading business. In the spring of 1831 he and his friends started a new business. 

They planned to take goods to Santa Fe, New Mexico, and carry on a trade there.

Jed never got to Santa Fe. On the way across the sandy desert land the party ran out of water. Jed went on ahead to look for a supply. At last he found some. He got down on his knees to take a drink. 

When he stood up he saw Indians all about him. 

There was no way to escape. He put up as hard a fight as he could and died bravely like all good mountain men.

During his short life, Jed Smith had explored more new country than any other person. He was the first white man to explore the country west of the Rocky Mountains. He was the first to find a way across the desert and over the mountains to California. He had explored the country along the Pacific Ocean all the way from San Diego to Canada.

Years later other great trappers and explorers were to do some of these same things. Men such as 

Kit Carson and Captain John C. Fremont would also go over the mountains to California. Then still later would follow thousands of men going to California for gold.

Jed Smith was the first great pathfinder in the western country. Better than anyone else, he had known what was on the other side.

VIII  

"His courage and leadership built a great railroad."

While Jedediah Smith was making his last trip into the western country, the first railroads in the United States were being built. These railroads were short lines between some of the cities in the eastern states.

About thirty years later the United States had grown so much that a railroad was needed from the Pacific to the Atlantic. During this time, Texas and all of the country west of the Rocky Mountains had been added to the United States. 

Thousands of new settlers had moved into the western country, and a better way of traveling between the east and the west was needed. That was why General Grenville M. Dodge was asked to build a great railroad.

Some of Jed Smith's friends saw a great many things happen to their beloved western country. 

The daring days of the fur trade passed. Thousands of men, with their families, began to cross the country in wagons. They went to live on the rich 101

OUR AMERICA 

farm lands of Oregon. Later great numbers of people made the trip across the plains and mountains to 

California. There was gold in California, more gold than had ever been dreamed of. 

Ewing Galloway

Thousands of families crossed the country in wagons to reach 

California, Oregon and other western states.

Some of the people who moved west did not go so far as Oregon and California. Many of them, who were called Mormons, went to live in Utah. They settled in the valley near the Great Salt Lake. 

Others found silver and gold in the Rocky Mountains and built their homes there. Still others were satisfied with the prairie lands east of the Rocky Mountains. Soon little settlements and towns and

trading posts could be found in all parts of the western country.

Most of the people who moved west came from the eastern part of the United States. They had friends 

Ewing Galloway

An old-fashioned stage coach and relatives in the east. Each wanted to get news from the other.

Soon stage lines were started. They made trips from the Missouri country to the west. The stages carried both mail and people. Still that was too slow. 

Then the Pony Express was started. Daring riders were hired. One of them was "Buffalo Bill." His real name was William F. Cody. They rode fast horses and carried the mail from Missouri to California in eight days. Even this was not fast enough.

OUR AMERICA 

A telegraph line was built across the country to California. Now news could travel in a few minutes. 

But there was no way for people to travel quickly and safely between the east and the west. 

At last, in 1862, the Government of the United States decided to help build a railroad across the country. Abraham Lincoln was then President of the United States.

Two companies were started. One was called the 

Central Pacific. It was to start from Sacramento, California, and build toward the east. The other company was called the Union Pacific. It was to start from Omaha, Nebraska, and build toward the west.

One day some of the men in the Union Pacific Company had a meeting. One of them said, "We need a man who is a strong leader. He must be able to build the railroad and to fight the Indians at the same time." "Yes," replied another, "but there is only one man in the United States who can do it. That man is General Grenville M. Dodge."

For many years General Dodge had been in the United States Army. He was a good engineer as well as a brave soldier. He had built hundreds of miles of railroad for the army. Then, too, he had spent a great deal of time in the west among the Indians. He knew the country over which the railroad was to be built.

The Indians had a name for General Dodge. They

,called him "Long Eye." That was because he could see so far when he looked through the strong glasses on his engineer's instruments. It was like looking through a telescope or a good pair of field glasses. 

But the Indians did not know this. Some of them thought he could shoot as far as he could see. They were afraid of "Long Eye."

When General Dodge was asked to take the job of building the railroad, he said, "I will be your chief engineer, but you must place me in full charge." The 

Union Pacific men agreed. Then General Dodge went to work.

He sent out small parties of engineers. They had to study the country carefully and find the easiest way for the railroad to go. Then, when they had decided upon the best route, the next job was to mark it. 

Soldiers were sent along with the engineers to protect them from the Indians.

After the engineers, came large parties of workers who built the bed upon which the railroad was to be laid. They had to dig a way through hills and fill up the little valleys. Then there were bridges to build, and large rocks to move out of the way. All of this had to be done to make a fairly level roadbed upon which the rails could be laid. 

Then came the men who put in the ties. The ties were big pieces of wood. The heavy steel rails were next laid down on the ties. Then they were fastened with large iron spikes so it would be safe for the

OUR AMERICA 

trains to run on them. Soldiers were also sent along to guard the workers. 

The railroad began to crawl westward across Nebraska a few miles at a time. It was almost like a long, brown and yellow worm moving over the prairie country. After many weeks it began to creep over 

From Richardson's Beyond the Mississippi 

Indians watching one of the first trains on the Union Pacific the brown hills of what is now the state of Wyoming. 

General Dodge had to be everywhere. Sometimes he was in Omaha trying to get more supplies for the railroad. At other times he went with the engineers far ahead of the railroad. Then there were many times when he had to go with the soldiers and try to make peace with the Indians. Sometimes he helped fight the Indians when they attacked the workers or the trains.

Many discouraging things happened. Often it seemed as if the railroad would never be built. Once a river overflowed. The rushing water washed away many miles of track. Bridges were torn down and telegraph poles carried away. 

The Indians stole the horses and attacked the workmen. They set fire to large piles of ties and pulled up the sticks with which the engineers had marked the way. They killed one of Dodge's best engineers. The Indians were fighting for their hunting grounds and their homes. They knew if the railroad were built, white people would soon come and take the country away from them. 

It was hard to get enough soldiers, and without soldiers it was difficult to get workers. They did not like to take chances on being killed by the Indians. 

General Dodge sent scouts out to see what the Indians were doing. In this way he knew which Indians were warlike and which were peaceful. One of his best scouts was a mountain man named Jim Bridger. 

Many years before, Jim Bridger had trapped and hunted with Jedediah Smith.

Some of the officers of the Union Pacific Company did not like General Dodge. They wanted him to quit, but Dodge got the president of the company and the United States Government to help him. Once, when troubles were thickest, Dodge was asked to stop work until more soldiers could be obtained. General Dodge would not listen. His answer was, "I'll build this rail-

road to Salt Lake City in one more year or give myself up to the Indians."

General Dodge was a splendid leader. He knew how to get men to work together. He did not ask them to do anything he was not willing to do himself. Once he asked some of his men to cross a river, but they turned back. The water was too swift. Then Dodge got on his horse and led the way. "Follow me," he shouted. "If you are going to help me build the Union Pacific Railroad, you must learn to cross streams more swift than this one. 

Slowly but surely the railroad pushed its way across Wyoming and on into Utah. In the meantime, the builders of the Central Pacific road were also busy. They had crossed the high mountains in eastern California and were now building through the flat country of Nevada.

In March, 1869, the Union Pacific track reached Ogden, Utah. The Central Pacific track was still one hundred and fifty miles away. General Dodge went to see the men building the Central Pacific. He wanted to decide upon a place where the two railroads should meet.

At last it was agreed that the two tracks should be joined together at Promontory Point, Utah. Then began a great race to see which railroad would get there first.

Never did men work harder or build such a railroad in shorter time. Each group of workers wanted to beat

OUR AMERICA 

the other. Most of the Central Pacific workers were 

Chinese. The greatest number of Union Pacific men were Irish. All of them were good workers. 

On April 1, 1869, the Central Pacific was three miles ahead. It had only fifty-four miles left to go, but the Union Pacific still had fifty-seven miles of track to lay. One day the Central Pacific men won a bet by laying ten miles of track in a single day. 

Everywhere in the United States people were watching the contest, and wondering who would win.

On the first day of May the two railroads were only a few miles apart. They were still about even when 

General Dodge got some bad news.

The Union Pacific owed money to some of the men who had helped build the road. These men said they would make the workers quit unless they were paid at once. Hundreds of their workmen had not received any pay for several months. They, too, were ready to quit.

Dodge had to act fast. He sent a telegram to the president of the Union Pacific, telling him what the trouble was. At once he sent Dodge a large sum of money. The men were paid, and the work continued. 

On the ninth day of May the two groups of workers were close enough to see each other. When the sun went down that day they were only a hundred feet apart. There was great excitement.

The next morning all of the rails but one were laid. 

Both groups of workers reached the meeting place at

about the same time. Then the Central Pacific and 

Union Pacific trains were brought steaming down the tracks until they faced each other, just a few feet apart. Between the two engines stood the leaders of each railroad. The celebration was about to begin. 

General Dodge stood quietly to one side. Now his job was finished. His courage and leadership had built a great railroad. He thought of the days of hard work, of the heat and cold and danger, and of the men who had given their lives. He thought of how useful this railroad would be to America. Perhaps his eyes even grew a little dim as he watched the celebration. 

First, there was a prayer of thanks. Then the last rail was put in place. Soon the railroad joining the east and the west would be finished. A California man made a little speech and took a railway spike, made of gold, from his pocket. He gave it to Leland 

Stanford, the president of the Central Pacific Railroad.

The Governor of Nevada also made a short speech, and took a silver spike from his pocket. This he gave to Thomas Durant, the vice-president of the Union 

Pacific Railroad. 

The last tie was shoved into place. There was a silver plate on it with these words, "The last tie laid in the completion of the Pacific Railroad, May 10, 1869."

OUR AMERICA 

Driving the last spike in the completion of the railroad The golden spike was put against the tie. A silver hammer was handed Leland Stanford. He raised it above his shoulders and struck the golden spike. As the blow fell a signal was sent along the telegraph lines to let the people know that the railroad had been completed. 

A short time later a telegram was handed to U. S. 

Grant, President of the United States. It simply said, "We have the honor to report the last rail laid and the last spike driven. The Pacific Railroad is finished."

IX 

"He found a way to talk over a wire.”

The work of many men was needed to make the United States into a large and strong country. 

The thinkers and explorers and builders all helped in their own way.

Perhaps the men who made great inventions, such as the cotton gin, the steamboat, the reaper and the telegraph, helped as much as anyone. 

Each of these inventions soon became a very important part of American life. 

Seven years after General Dodge finished the

Pacific Railroad, a very important invention was made. It was the telephone, the invention of 

Alexander Graham Bell. "We must get that young man to come to Boston." 

The members of the Boston School Board had heard some wonderful stories about Alexander Graham Bell. He could teach deaf children to talk. He knew a way to make them hear with their eyes instead of their ears. 

Of course, they did not really hear sounds with their eyes. They simply watched a person's lips when he talked. They could tell from the movement of the 113

OUR AMERICA 

lips what was said, even though they could not hear a single sound. It is called lip reading today. 

Alexander Bell had learned to do this from his father. Alexander's father was a university professor. He taught people to speak better. 

Alexander was born in Scotland. He was a bright little lad, with black eyes and thick, dark, curly hair.

He seemed to be interested in almost everything. Perhaps better than anything else, he liked to learn about something new. He liked especially well the things his father taught him about how to speak. 

One day he took a kind of wax and made a skull.

There were places for the eyes and nose and mouth. 

He made a little box and put it inside the skull. 

There were some little pipes leading to the box. When 

Alexander blew into the pipes the box made sounds. 

It seemed as if the skull was trying to talk. 

Alexander went to a university in Scotland. He studied very hard and learned a great deal. He learned many things about electricity. He began to wonder if sounds could be sent over a wire. But he also kept on learning how to teach people to speak better. He began to work with deaf children, and to teach them how to talk.

While he was studying and teaching, his two brothers became sick and died. Alexander, who was only twenty-three years old, also became sick. The doctor told his father, "You must go some place where the climate is better or Alexander will die, too."

Professor Bell moved his family to Canada. Alexander's health soon got better. He started teaching deaf children again. He liked to help these children who were not so fortunate as others.

Soon people in other towns began to hear about the young man who could teach children to hear with their eyes instead of their ears. The school board in 

Boston, Massachusetts, heard about his work. They decided he was the right man to take charge of their new school for deaf children. 

So twenty-four-year-old Alexander Bell came to

Boston in 1871. He took charge of the new school. 

He was as successful in teaching deaf children here as he had been in Canada.

One day a very important man came to visit his classes. The man was Dom Pedro, the emperor, or ruler, of Brazil. Dom Pedro wanted to see things in the United States that he could do to help his people in Brazil. He liked Alexander and his work with the deaf children. Of course, Alexander was pleased, but little did he think that one day Dom Pedro would do him a great favor.

About a year after he came to Boston, Alexander became a professor at Boston University. He also started a little school of his own for deaf children. 

He was so busy that he almost forgot about his idea of sending sounds along a wire.

One of Alexander's pupils was a girl named Mabel Hubbard. She was fifteen years old, and had been

OUR AMERICA 

Brown Bros.

Alexander Graham Bell deaf for many years. But Alexander soon taught her to talk and to read the lips of other persons. She liked her bright, young teacher, and Alexander liked his lovely pupil very much indeed. Before long they were in love with each other. Later, they were married. 

Mabel's father was a rich lawyer. He loved his daughter dearly. It made him very happy when he saw that she was learning to talk. Mr. Hubbard liked 

Alexander Bell and soon became one of his best

friends. The two men had many long talks together. 

Sometimes they talked about Alexander's idea of sending sounds over a wire.

At this time Alexander was also teaching a fiveyear-old boy to talk. The boy's name was George

Sanders. He had been born deaf, but Alexander was able to help the little fellow. George lived in a town. a few miles from Boston. 

One day George's father said, "Alexander, it would be easier for you to teach George if you would come and live with us." Alexander decided to go. Soon he and Mr. Sanders were good friends. Mr. Sanders also liked to talk with Alexander Bell and listen to his ideas. He encouraged Alexander to work on an invention to send sound over a wire. "You can fix up a part of my house for a workshop," said Mr. Sanders. Alexander did not wait long to accept the kind offer. Night after night he worked in his little shop. After a while he decided to give up all of his pupils except Mabel and George. 

Then he would have more time to work on his invention.

At first, he thought only of finding a way to send sounds over a wire. One day another idea came to him. Why not send the voice over a wire? Why not send words instead of sounds? Then people could talk to each other over a wire. "If I can make a deaf child talk," he said, "I can make iron talk."

OUR AMERICA 

Alexander Bell began to work harder than ever. 

He went to a friend who was a doctor. They talked about Alexander's idea. The doctor was very much interested. Alexander wanted to know how the human ear worked in hearing sounds. The doctor said, "I will give you a whole human ear. Then you can take it to your shop and see for yourself how it works." 

Alexander got many good ideas from his studies of the ear. He was sure that he was on the right track. Alexander believed in himself. He was sure that he could invent a way to talk over a wire. 

All of this time Mr. Sanders and Mr. Hubbard were giving him the money he needed for his inventions. They did not think much of his idea of talking over a wire. They thought it was rather foolish. Both of them wanted Alexander to work on some of his other ideas. Mr. Hubbard liked best the idea of sending only sounds over a wire. There was another idea they liked also. It was a plan Alexander had to send several messages over one telegraph wire at the same time. "We will not give you any more money," they told

Alexander, "unless you agree to stop work on that foolish idea of talking over a wire. If you will work on a way to make the telegraph better, we will help you." Alexander did not want to give up, but at last he agreed.

Before long there was not room enough in the little

workshop in Mr. Sander's home. Alexander moved to a bigger shop in Boston. Then he got a young man named Thomas Watson to help him. One day he told 

Watson about his idea of talking over a wire. "I never had such a great surprise in all my life," said Mr. Watson some years later. The two young men began to work in the new shop. They put wires from one room to the other. They made all kinds of strange little things to try out. Alexander furnished most of the ideas. Thomas Watson made most of the parts.

One hot evening in June, 1875, they were working on the telegraph. Alexander Bell was in one room. 

Thomas Watson was in the other. Suddenly Alexander heard a faint sound on his end of the wire. He rushed to the room where his friend was working. "What did you do then, Watson?" he asked. "Don't change anything. Let me see." 

That was the beginning of the telephone. Alexander was now more sure than ever that he could find a way to talk over a wire. For nine months the two men worked. They tried first one thing and then another. At last they succeeded.

It was a day in March, 1876. Again Thomas Watson was in one room and Alexander Bell in the other. 

Watson was listening at his end of the wire. Soon he heard Alexander Bell's clear voice saying, "Mr. 

Watson, please come here. I want you."

That was the first complete sentence spoken over the telephone. Alexander Bell had invented a way to

OUR AMERICA 

Daughter of Alexander Bell using a model of the first telephone

Globe Photos talk over a wire. Forty years later he and Thomas 

Watson used the same telephone again. This time they talked to each other from New York to San Francisco.

During that same month of March, 1876, Alexander Bell got a patent for his telephone from the United States Government. This was one of the most valuable patents ever given to any inventor. Once

Mr. Hubbard offered to sell Alexander's patent to the 

Western Union Telegraph Company. He asked them to pay one hundred thousand dollars for it, but they refused. Two years later they would have been glad to get it for twenty-five million dollars.

In 1876 a large exposition, or fair, was held in Philadelphia. It was held to celebrate the one hundredth birthday of the United States. It was one hundred years since the Declaration of Independence had been written by Thomas Jefferson and signed by the members of Congress.

Mr. Hubbard was one of the men in charge of the 

Philadelphia fair. He lived in Philadelphia most of that summer. One day his wife and Mabel visited him. Mabel was now a grown young lady. She and 

Alexander Bell were engaged to be married.

Alexander went with them to the train. He wanted to go along, but he had a great deal of work to do in Boston. As the train began to leave, Mabel started to cry. She was disappointed because Alexander was not going. When he saw the tears, he jumped on the train.

He did not have enough money, so he borrowed from his friends. He had no clothes other than those he was wearing. Mabel's brother, William, packed a trunk and sent it to Alexander at Philadelphia.

In one part of the fair was a large room filled with all kinds of new inventions. Mr. Hubbard had helped 

Alexander to get his telephone entered at the fair. 

There was to be a prize given for the best invention. 

No one seemed to notice Alexander Bell's telephone. 

Then came the day when the judges went around to look at all of the inventions. At last they reached

the table where the telephone stood. All day long they had been looking at inventions. Now they were tired and hungry. They wanted to get through as soon as they could.

Alexander tried to tell them about the telephone.

listened. Still they did not think it was an important invention. They thought it was only a good toy. "It will amuse the visitors," they said. 

Thousands of people came to see the telephone at the fair. Although it did not win the prize, more persons came to see it than any other invention. Only those who understood it had any idea of how wonderful it was. A famous Englishman said, "The telephone is the most wonderful thing I have seen in America."

Most people thought it was only a toy. They did not see how it could be of any use. One newspaper in England called it an American humbug. Some persons thought it was a fake. A few of them said, "There must be a hole through the middle of the wire." 

The next year Alexander Bell and his friends. started the Bell Telephone Company. That same year 

Alexander and Mabel were married. On their wedding day he gave her a present of his share in the company. Some years later this present was worth many millions of dollars. 

At first, people were slow to use the telephone. Two years after it was invented less than one thousand of them were being used. But at the end of the next two years over thirty thousand telephones were in use.

Since that time the telephone has spread to every part of world. Now it is possible even to talk to people on the other side of the ocean.

The world was sad at the death of 

Alexander Bell in 1922.

Alexander Bell lived until 1922. He became a rich and famous man. He received great honors from many different countries for his invention of the telephone. He did many kind things to help others. He worked on other great inventions. 

All his life he was interested in deaf children. It was his work with them that made possible the telephone. One time, when he was an old man, he made a speech in Boston. He said, "I am proud to think that 

I have been a teacher of the deaf."

"He gave the world a new light."

While Alexander Bell was teaching deaf children in Boston, a young man in New Jersey was making a new invention almost every month. No other man has ever made so many important inventions or become so famous as an inventor. 

Perhaps the greatest of all his inventions was made in 1879, three years after Alexander Bell finished the telephone. That was the year Thomas

A. Edison gave the world the electric light. 

A three-year-old boy stood on the porch of a red brick house. Some strange-looking wagons with white tops were standing in front of the house. "Where are you going?" asked the little boy. "To California," said the people in the wagons. "Where did you come from?" "From the east," they told him. "What are you going there for?" "Gold," they said. "Why?"

The little boy who asked so many questions was 126

Thomas Alva Edison. His mother and father called him Al. He was born in Ohio in 1847. It was the same year that Alexander Bell was born in far-away 

Scotland. 

All of his life he asked questions about things he did not understand. "Why?" he would say. Sometimes the answer was, "I don't know." But this did not end the boy's questions. "Well, why don't you know?" he would ask.

Not far from his home was a place where men built ships. It was called a shipyard. He liked to go there and watch the men work. He asked questions of every man who would talk with him. What was that thing? What was it used for? Why did they do this? Why did they do that?

One day one of the men said, "It would save us a great deal of time if we had one man who would do nothing but answer the boy's questions."

Little Al Edison liked to try things out for himself. It was another way of finding out about things. 

One time when he was six years old his father found him in the barn. He was sitting on a nestful of eggs. "I'm hatching out chickens," he said. Then his father remembered. A few days before the boy had asked many questions about a goose that was sitting on some eggs. Now he was trying to find out for himself if he could hatch the eggs by keeping them warm. 

It was hard for a boy who wanted to find out about so many things to keep out of trouble. Once he

OUR AMERICA 

started a fire to find out what would happen. The fire spread and soon a barn was burned down. He was given a good whipping for that. 

Another time he found a bee's nest in a field. He wanted to see what it was like. He bent over and started to dig it up. A goat saw the boy and ran over and butted him. Never was a boy more surprised.

He got up as quickly as he could and ran toward the fence. The goat started after him. He got over the fence before the goat could butt him again.

Little Al went to school only a few months. The teacher could not understand the boy who asked so many questions. She thought he was a little queer. 

Many of the neighbors thought so, too. But Al's mother did not think so. She knew what a bright, active lad her son really was. She began to teach him at home. Before long he was much better educated than any of the other boys his age. 

There were ever so many ideas that came to him. 

Once when he was eleven years old he thought of raising a garden. All summer he worked. The planting and hoeing and weeding were not easy work for a boy, but he raised a fine crop of vegetables. Then he found a place to sell them. When he was all through he had made about six hundred dollars. This he gave to his mother to keep for him.

That year he became interested in a study called chemistry. He used the cellar of his home as a place to experiment, or try things out. There were more

than two hundred bottles of all kinds in his cellar. 

On each of these he printed the word "Poison" so that no one else would bother them.

Before long he learned a great many things about chemistry. He did not believe what he read about his chemicals until he tried them out for himself. He liked to mix them together to see what would happen. 

Sometimes the most terrible smells would come out of the cellar. 

Mrs. Edison did not like these awful smells. She did not like the mess in her cellar very well either. 

But she knew that Al was learning many things. She let him keep on with his experiments. Many years later he said, "My mother was the making of me.'

When Al was twelve years old he begged his father and mother to let him sell papers on a train. He needed more money to buy supplies for his experiments. They did not want him to go, but at last they consented.

So Al Edison began to sell candy, papers, magazines, and books on the train that went from his town to Detroit, Michigan. He had to get up at six o'clock in the morning. He did not get back until about ten o'clock at night. It was a long day for such a young boy. 

When the train reached Detroit, Al hurried away to the Detroit Public Library. Here he would read until it was time for the train to start back again. 

He decided to read every book in the library. He

OUR AMERICA 

started out with one shelf. He read the first book on the shelf, then the next one, and so on. 

After several weeks he had finished about fifteen feet of books. But he saw now that he could not read them all. He thought to himself, "I cannot read them all. There are too many. Why would it not be better to choose carefully and read only the best books?"

Al Edison had many interesting adventures while he was working on the train. One day two men from the south were on the train. They had a negro servant with them. Al came into the railroad car with his papers. They took all he had and threw them out the window. Then they told their negro servant to pay for the papers. 

The negro took some money from a bag and gave it to Al. Pretty soon Al came back with a big load of magazines. Again the men took them and threw them out the window. Once more the negro paid the boy. 

By this time all the other people in the car were watching this strange business. Soon Al came in with a high pile of books. Everyone began to laugh. The two young men bought the whole pile and threw them away. A few minutes later Al brought in all of his candy. Out the window it went. The negro gave him some more money from the bag. 

Al did not know what to make of this. He decided to sell his goods as long as the young men would buy. 

He put everything he had left into a big trunk. 

Then he dragged the trunk into the car where the

two men were riding. How the people did laugh when they saw the little newsboy coming with the big trunk. "What have you got?" asked one of the men. "Everything I have left that is for sale," replied the boy. 

The two men bought the trunk. "Pay the boy for it," they said to the negro. "Then take it and throw it out the door."

Al decided to fix up a little place in one of the railway cars. Then he could try some of his experiments while the train was moving. Later, he started to print a little newspaper. It was called The Weekly 

Courtesy, Henry Ford 

Edison fixed up a workshop in one of the railway cars.

OUR AMERICA 

Herald. The paper sold for three cents a copy. It was the first newspaper ever to be printed on a train. 

One day the train was traveling over some bumpy places in the track. Some of Al Edison's chemicals fell on the floor. A fire started and began to spread. 

Al tried to put it out. 

Just then the conductor came in. He seized some pails of water and helped put out the fire. The conductor was so angry that he gave Al a blow on the ear. From that time on Al Edison was partly deaf.

At another time he was walking home. He saw a little boy playing in the sand between the tracks. A railroad car was coming down the track straight toward the boy. There was not a second to spare. He dropped his papers and jumped for the boy. Another jump and they were off the tracks. 

The boy's father was standing a few feet away. 

He saw the whole thing happen. "What can I do to repay you?" he asked. The man thought for a moment. "It isn't much," he said, "but I'll teach you how to work the telegraph." 

This was one of the things that Al Edison wanted most to know. He gladly accepted the man's offer. 

For three months he studied and practiced. At the end of that time he was as good as his teacher. He decided to get a job with the railroad as a telegraph operator. 

In the years that followed, Edison worked in many different towns. Most of the time he worked as a

telegraph operator. He did not stay very long in any one place.

Wherever he went he kept on studying. He kept on trying things out for himself. He worked hard and long. Many times he worked more than twenty hours a day. And never once did he stop asking questions.

In 1870, he sold his first invention. He was paid forty thousand dollars for it. Perhaps most young men would have stopped working, but Edison was just getting started. He took the money and built a workshop in New Jersey.

During the next six years he made more than one hundred and twenty inventions or an average of nearly two inventions a month. His workshop kept growing larger and larger. He had to hire more and more men to help him. Many times he worked all day and all night without stopping to rest.

Edison was a famous man before he was twentyfive years old. Most of his inventions were machines to make the telegraph better. He also helped to invent the typewriter. At one time he was working on more than forty inventions.

The same year that Alexander Bell invented the telephone, Edison moved his workshop to Menlo Park in New Jersey. It was here that he made his greatest inventions. One of them was a little machine to make the telephone better. Another was the phonograph, or talking machine.

OUR AMERICA 

Ewing Galloway 

Thomas Edison experimenting in his

Menlo Park workshop 

Perhaps the greatest of all was the electric light. 

He began work on this late in 1878. First, he studied everything he could find that might be of some help. When an idea came to him he wrote it down in a little notebook. He filled more than two hundred notebooks while he was working on the electric light. 

Next, he began to experiment, to try things out for himself. There were so many different ideas to try

out that he had about fifty men helping him. They worked for long hours at a time without sleeping. 

Their food was brought in to them. 

Sometimes, they became so tired and discouraged that they felt like giving up. Then Edison would play music on an old organ until everyone felt better. At other times they would all sing while they ate their meals during the night.

The whole world was watching for news from the Edison shops. Many persons thought the electric light was impossible. Some of them said Edison was a dreamer. Others did not know if it could be done or not. They said, "Edison can do it if anyone can." 

Month after month Edison worked. Every experiment failed. It did not seem as if the electric light would ever be invented. Most men would have been discouraged, but not Edison. "Wait," he said. "Give me more time. I am sure I can do it. I will make the electric light so cheap that only the rich can afford to burn candles." 

At another time one of his men said to him, "Isn't it too bad that we have worked all these months without any results?" "No results!" Edison answered. "Why, we have lots of results. We know several thousand things that won't work." Sometimes it is just as important to know what won't work as to know what will work. 

In the spring of 1879, Edison knew that he was on the right track. He found that a glass globe, or bulb,

OUR AMERICA 

from which all the air had been pumped was necessary. Then he succeeded in making a light by running electricity through some tiny wires which he put in the bulb.

The wire that he used was made of platinum. This was a precious metal more costly than gold. He knew that he must find something cheaper and better. 

Again he started out, trying first one thing and then another.

At last he thought of trying a piece of cotton thread which he prepared in a certain way until it was hard and stiff. Time after time the thread broke before the men could get it in the glass bulb. Finally they got one that did not break.

The electricity was turned on. The thread began to glow. It gave off a soft, white light. Edison and his men sat and looked at the glowing electric light. 

They did not know how long it would burn. But the light did not go out.

Hour after hour they sat and watched it. It burned ten hours, fifteen, twenty, twenty-five. Already the light had burned steadily for more than one day. 

Still it did not go out.

Thirty hours passed. There was no sleep for any of the men. None of them wanted to go to bed. At last, after more than forty hours the little white light went out. Then Edison and his men went home to bed. 

The electric light was not impossible. Edison had succeeded after more than one year of trying out

Brown Bros.

Thomas Edison's library which he sometimes used as a sleeping room while working on an invention thousands of ideas. It was on October 21, 1879, that the little electric light began burning. 93 "If it will burn forty hours now," said Edison, "later I can make it burn more than a hundred hours.' 

Now began a search for something that would burn better and longer than the prepared cotton thread. 

Again, thousands of experiments were tried.

OUR AMERICA 

One of the men had a long, red beard. Some of the others pulled a hair from his beard. They treated it and put it in a glass bulb. It burned with a soft red light. How the men did laugh then. They said that the light was red because the man's beard was red.

Edison even sent men all over the world to find something that would burn well in the electric light. 

His men traveled to South America, to Asia, to Cuba, and other places. They sent back thousands of things which Edison tested. One of them found a certain kind of bamboo in Japan that worked very well. 

More than one hundred thousand dollars were spent in looking for something to burn in the electric light. Perhaps better than anything else, this showed the world how carefully Edison worked. The great search finally came to an end in Edison's own shop. 

He discovered a way to make what he needed.

There was great excitement when the news about the electric light first appeared. All of the newspapers printed stories about it. So many people came to see it that Edison had to put guards outside his shops. Then he could work without being disturbed.

One of the newspapers printed a joke. It said, "What everyone thinks is the evening star in the sky is really an electric light. Edison sends it up on a balloon every night." Many people were foolish enough to think this was true. 

On New Year's Eve, 1879, Edison fixed up a little show. He put hundreds of electric lights from tree

to tree in a little park near his shop. Thousands of people came to see them. The railroads ran special trains. Everyone was eager to see the marvelous new invention. Now every busy street in every large town glows with thousands of the lights which Edison gave to the world.

In the years that followed Edison worked on many inventions. There was the storage battery, the electric motor, the moving picture machine, the electric railway, the mimeograph, and hundreds of other things. He took out more than one thousand patents 

Photo by George Ebling Thomas Alva Edison

OUR AMERICA 

for his inventions. No other inventor has ever equaled this number.

Many times people spoke of him as a great genius. "No," he would say, "Genius is only two parts of ideas and ninety-eight parts of hard work." Hard work and good ideas, asking questions and trying things-that was how Edison succeeded where others failed.

He worked steadily until a short time before he died in 1931. Some months before his death he made a talk over the radio and gave the world a message. "Be courageous," he said. "Have faith-go forward."

XI 

WALTER REED 

"He was a soldier of science." 

The men who fight disease stand high among those whose work has made the United States a better and stronger country. 

At the same time that Thomas A. Edison was working on the electric light, a young army doctor was working at his calling in Arizona. About twenty years later, the United States helped the people of Cuba in a war against Spain. 

A deadly disease killed many of the American soldiers who were sent to Cuba. At times the people in the southern part of the United States were also attacked by it. Then, in 1900, the cause of the disease was discovered by an army doctor whose name was Walter Reed. 

More deadly than bullets was a sickness that attacked the American soldiers in Cuba. The sickness was called yellow fever.

It was a very strange sickness. It would appear suddenly and then disappear just as suddenly. It might strike all of the soldiers in one tent, but none of those in the next tent. It might attack one person, 141

OUR AMERICA 

but leave alone those who worked and lived with him. 

It was impossible to tell who would be next. 

There was so little that the doctors could do about it. They burned the clothes and bedding and houses of the sick. They kept those who had yellow fever apart from the rest. They tried everything they could think of. But still the deadly yellow fever struck without warning.

Only a few facts were known about the terrible disease. If a person were lucky enough to get well, he never again was taken sick with yellow fever. Most of the cases appeared during the summer when the weather was hottest. With the coming of winter and colder weather, the yellow fever seemed to disappear. 

During the summer of 1900, there was a great deal of yellow fever in Havana, Cuba. Many American soldiers in the city died from the disease. The men in charge of the United States Army were tired of having their soldiers killed by something they could not fight.

Four army doctors were given orders to study the mysterious yellow fever. The four men were Major

Walter Reed, Dr. James Carroll, Dr. Jesse W. Lazear and Dr. Aristides Agramonte. Their job was to find what caused the yellow fever. They were also to find what could be done to prevent it. 

Walter Reed was in charge of the work. He had been an army doctor and officer for many years.

WALTER REED 

143 

Ewing Galloway 

Walter Reed 

None of them, except Dr. Agramonte, had ever had the dangerous yellow fever.

So Walter Reed and his friends became detectives, and started out to track down the mysterious yellow enemy that attacked without warning. It was a dangerous job, but one that had to be done. 

First, they examined some of the soldiers who were

OUR AMERICA 

sick. They took little samples of blood and tested them. They were looking for some kind of germ which might be the cause of the yellow fever. They made test after test. There was no germ to be found. 

Next, Dr. Reed made a trip to a little town where the yellow fever had struck again and again. About thirty-five soldiers were sick in the little army hospital. But none of the nurses who cared for the sick men had caught the fever, and none of them had ever had it before. "Surely," thought Dr. Reed, "the disease is not spread by being near someone who has it. If it were, then why did not the nurses get it? Why have I not taken it from the sick men I have visited?" 

Another strange thing happened. Nine men had been put in jail for a time. Six days later one of them became ill with the fever. None of the other prisoners caught it. One of them even slept in the sick man's bed. Still nothing happened to him. 

Could it be that some kind of insect flew in through the jail window, bit this one man, and then flew out again? Dr. Reed began to think about this. He knew that typhoid fever was carried by the common house fly. Maybe that was how yellow fever was spread.

Many years before, a man in Havana named Dr. 

Carlos Finlay had said the mosquito was the cause of yellow fever. But Dr. Finlay had no proof. He believed in his idea, but he did not know for sure that he was right.

WALTER REED 

145 When Walter Reed returned to Havana, he told his three friends what he had seen. They decided to experiment with Finlay's idea about the mosquito. 

How could they experiment? They could not use animals, because animals did not get yellow fever. 

Did they dare to use human beings? It was a hard question to answer. At last, they decided to take the risk. After all, it would be a great service to the world if they could find a way to stop the yellow fever. "Yes," said the doctors, "we must take the chance. 

But we shall experiment on ourselves first."

It was a brave step to take. Each of them knew it might mean death, but these men were true soldiers. 

They were soldiers of science, fighting a war against disease. They were glad to have this chance to do something which might help others to live better. 

Dr. Lazear got some mosquito eggs. The eggs were hatched. Soon the mosquitoes were full grown. Then 

Dr. Lazear.allowed the mosquitoes to bite some of the men who were sick with yellow fever. Now they were ready for the first experiment.

The first man to roll up his sleeve was Dr. Lazear. 

The mosquitoes were given a chance to bite him. 

Seven other brave men also offered to take a chance.

Days went by. Nothing happened. All of the men were well.

Walter Reed had been called back to the United States before this first experiment. His last orders

OUR AMERICA 

were to try mosquitoes. So a second time the mosquitoes were allowed to bite men with yellow fever. 

Dr. Lazear picked out a mosquito that had bitten four sick men. This time Dr. Carroll held out his arm. As he watched the mosquito bite him he said, "If there is anything in this idea, I should get a good dose." 

Five days later Dr. Carroll was carried to the yellow fever camp. For three days he lay in the shadow of death. Then he slowly began to get better.
