One day he told the nurse, "I got my yellow fever from the bite of a mosquito." The nurse looked surprised. Some time later he read the records of his case. He found that the nurse had written this note, "Dr. Carroll says he got his illness from the bite of a mosquito. He must be out of his head."

In three weeks he was able to get up. Before long he was back on the job again. Some years later he said, "The thing I am most proud of is that I was the first on whom our experiment worked." 

On the same day that Dr. Carroll got up from his bed, an accident happened to Dr. Lazear. He was in the hospital getting samples of blood from men who were sick. A mosquito sat down on the back of his hand. He saw it and could have brushed it off. Instead, he let the mosquito bite him.

A few days later he began to feel sick. It soon turned out to be a bad case of yellow fever. In a week's time brave Dr. Lazear was dead. He had risked

WALTER REED 

147 and lost his life in the service for others. The world of science will always honor his memory. He is one of its great heroes.

Walter Reed came back to Cuba. "The experiment 1.441 

Courtesy, Metropolitan Life Insurance Co. 

Camp Lazear must go on," he said. A little camp was built. It was called Camp Lazear in honor of their friend. 

A call was sent out for men who were willing to take part in the experiments. John R. Kissinger and 

John J. Moran came to see Walter Reed at once. Dr. 

Reed then told them about the danger and suffering they would have to face. They were not afraid. "We are willing," they said. Dr. Reed then offered to pay them. The two men refused, saying, "No, sir.

We are doing this in the interest of humanity and for the cause of science."

OUR AMERICA 

Dr. Reed stood up. He touched his cap. "Gentlemen," he said, "I salute you." Later he wrote in his report, "This display of moral courage has never been surpassed in the history of the United States Army."

Other men also offered their services. The experiments started once more. John Kissinger was the first to let a mosquito bite him. Three days later the fever struck him. Four other men were bitten by mosquitoes. All of them became sick with yellow fever. 

Dr. Reed was now sure that yellow fever was spread by the mosquito. But were there any other ways by which the disease could be spread? Another experiment was tried.

Three young Americans spent twenty nights in a little house at Camp Lazear. They slept in the same beds and between the same sheets which yellow fever patients had used. They even slept in the same shirts which the sick men had worn while they were ill. But none of the men got the yellow fever.

Now Walter Reed was sure that yellow fever could not be caught from being near those who had it, or from the things they used. A few more experiments and Walter Reed knew how yellow fever was spread. 

He also knew how it could be prevented. He found that yellow fever was carried from one person to another only by the female mosquito.

The mosquito had to bite a person sick with yellow

WALTER REED 

149 fever during the first three days of his illness. Then, at least twelve days had to pass before the mosquito bit someone who had never had yellow fever. The person who was bitten then became sick within six days. 

Yellow fever could be prevented by getting rid of mosquitoes. The mosquito eggs were laid in still water. "Empty tin cans and bottles. Put covers over water barrels and cisterns. Fill up the little pools of still water," said Walter Reed. "Then the mosquitoes will have no place to lay their eggs." "Get rid of the mosquitoes and there will be no more yellow fever," said the doctors. This has been done so well that today yellow fever is no longer a common disease.

It was all so very simple, now that it was over. 

It took brave men who were not afraid to die to track down the yellow murderer. They worked and took chances that others might live.

On New Year's Eve, Walter Reed sat down and wrote a letter to his wife, "The prayer that has been mine for twenty years, that I might be permitted in someway or at sometime to do something to prevent human suffering, has been granted. A thousand 

Happy New Years...."

From Stories of Luther Burbank and His Plant School 

Courtesy, Charles Scribner's Sons

The Shasta Daisy. Luther Burbank worked eight years before he was able to grow this flower with all its beauty.

XII 

LUTHER BURBANK 

"He understood the ways of Mother Nature."

The men of science have helped in many ways to make the United States a better country. Some of them have invented wonderful machines or discovered ways to prevent disease. 

Other men of science have spent their lives in studying plants. They have made it possible for the farmers to grow new and better crops. 

While Thomas A. Edison was making his inventions and Walter Reed was fighting disease, a man in California was giving his life to a great work. Luther Burbank was training plants to work for man.

A young man looked out the window of a railway car. He watched the brown prairie of Nebraska slip by. It was 1875, six years after General Dodge had finished building the Union Pacific Railroad.

The young man's name was Luther Burbank. He was thin and pale and seemed to be very tired. No one would have picked him as a person who would some day become a great man.

Luther Burbank was on his way to California. He 151

OUR AMERICA 

had very little money, but he had something more precious than money. He had hope and courage, and a little bag with ten potatoes in it. 

Those ten potatoes were named after him. They were called Burbank potatoes. Of course, the Burbank potato was not nearly so famous then as it is now. Some years before the young man had been walking through the garden at his home in Massachusetts. He noticed something on one of the potato plants. It was a seed ball. 

All his life Luther Burbank had been interested in plants and flowers. He knew that a seed ball on a potato plant was very unusual. He looked at it for a moment and then went on his way. "I wonder what kind of potatoes would grow from those seeds," he thought. He decided to find out.

When he went back to look for the seed ball it was gone. Day after day he searched for it among the potato vines. At last he found it. There were more than twenty little seeds in it.

The next spring he planted the seeds. Everyone of them grew. He watched those plants very carefully.

Some of them were quite different from the others. 

In the fall he dug his potatoes. Almost every plant had a different kind of potato. 

Luther Burbank saved seven different kinds. The rest he threw away. Two of those which he saved were especially fine. They were large and smooth and white. He knew that by planting them in the

LUTHER BURBANK 

153 ground he could grow more like them next year. 

He took his new potatoes to the fair that fall. 

Many people came to look at them. One day a great seedsman stopped to look at them. His business was to sell seeds and plants to farmers. He saw that the new potato might be very valuable. "How did you raise these potatoes?" he asked

Luther Burbank. The young man told him about the seed ball. Then the seedsman said, "I would like to buy some of your potatoes. If you will grow some more for me I will pay you one hundred and fifty dollars.' 99 The potatoes were grown, and the seedsman called them Burbank potatoes. Today, Burbank potatoes are grown in every part of the United States. With the money he got from his potatoes, Luther Burbank bought the ticket to California. And he did not forget to take along ten of his potatoes. 

After he had been in California for a few weeks, he moved to Santa Rosa. The city of Santa Rosa is in a rich valley north of San Francisco. He tried to find work, but jobs were difficult to get. 

One day he heard that a man who was building a house needed help. "Yes," said the man, "I will give you a job putting shingles on the roof. But you must bring your own hatchet." Luther Burbank agreed.

He spent nearly all of his money buying a hatchet. 

When he got back someone else had already taken the job.

OUR AMERICA 

After a while he got a job cleaning out chicken houses on a chicken ranch. He could not afford to rent a room, so he had to sleep in one of the chicken coops. Later, he found work in a nursery. He liked to work with the plants and flowers in the nursery. 

Again he did not have money enough to rent a good room. He slept in a little place over the hothouse. It was very warm and damp. Before long he became ill with a fever. Had it not been for a kindhearted woman, who gave him some fresh milk each day, he might not have got over his sickness.

When he became well he rented a little piece of land. Then he started a small nursery of his own. 

During the day time he worked as a carpenter and at odd jobs. At night he worked with his flowers and plants.

Luther Burbank's mother came to California in 1877. She bought a house and four acres of land in 

Santa Rosa. Her son rented a part of the land and moved his nursery. He was happy with his plants and flowers, but it was slow, hard work to get the nursery started. The first year his sales amounted to exactly fifteen dollars and twenty-five cents.

Most men would have been discouraged, but not Luther Burbank. He began to study the different plants in California. He made trips to the valleys and mountains to get seeds and young plants. In the meantime, he had planted his ten potatoes and saved each year's crop. Soon he had enough to sell to farmers who wanted to plant the new potatoes.

LUTHER BURBANK 

155 

Courtesy, Oscar E. Binner Co. 

Luther Burbank's flower house and garden

He learned all that he could about plants and flowers. Then he began to experiment with some of them in his nursery. Before long his nursery was so well known that he made more than ten thousand dollars each year. 

One day an order came for twenty thousand young prune trees. The man who gave the order wanted the trees the next year. Luther Burbank knew that it took three years for the slow-growing prune trees to become large enough to put in an orchard. How could he fill that order? Then an idea came to him. 

Why not plant almond trees? They grow quickly.

He planted about one hundred thousand almond

OUR AMERICA 

trees. When they were large enough he picked out the twenty thousand that were the strongest. Then he took little cuttings from prune trees. He grafted them on to the almond trees.

In this way the top part of each tree was the prune tree, but the roots and trunk were those of the almond tree. Since the top part of the tree bears the fruit, the trees would grow prunes instead of almonds. That was why Luther Burbank could plant almond trees that would bear prunes. When people heard of this wonderful deed they began to call 

Luther Burbank the "plant wizard."

Next to the members of his family and his plants and flowers, Luther Burbank liked Jumbo best. 

Jumbo was a little black and white Japanese dog. He liked to follow his master along the rows of flowers. 

Sometimes, when the day was warm, he would lie in the shade and sleep while Mr. Burbank worked. 

Never did he dig up any of the flowers. 

After ten years of work with the nursery, Luther Burbank decided to sell it. He wanted to spend all of his time experimenting with plants. He bought a seventeen-acre farm and began the work which was to make him famous all over the world. 

Seeds and plants came to the little farm from near-by farms and forests as well as from far-away lands. Soon there were plants and trees from almost every country in the world. Once a strange thing happened to some apple trees sent from South

LUTHER BURBANK 

157 

Brown Bros.

Luther Burbank gave to the world many new flowers, fruits, and vegetables.

America. When it is fall in the United States it is spring in South America. So when fall came, the apple trees began to put out little green leaves and pink and white blossoms. It was several years before they learned to blossom in the spring instead of the fall. 

Before long, a great many people were coming to

OUR AMERICA 

see Luther Burbank's beautiful gardens. They also wanted to see the plant wizard who could do such wonderful things with plants. Better than anyone else, he understood the ways of Mother Nature.

As the years went by, Luther Burbank gave to the world many new fruits and flowers. He went out into the desert and saw the cactus with its sharp spines. Even cattle could not eat it. "Why not grow a cactus without spines?" he thought.

He began to experiment. After a long time he succeeded in growing a cactus plant without spines. Cattle liked to eat the new plant. He also made the cactus plant grow a fruit that men could eat. It tastes something like a melon. It can be used for many different things.

The blackberry vine, too, had sharp thorns, but Mr. Burbank worked until he had a blackberry bush without thorns. Then he grew blackberry plants so large that more than a bushel of delicious berries could be picked from a single bush. Most wonderful of all, he even grew white blackberries.

He produced a new fruit called the plumcot. This he did by joining together the plum and the apricot. 

He grew a tree that bore plums without pits. He got a new berry by joining together the raspberry and the blackberry.

He developed a strawberry plant that would bear fruit throughout the year. He grew a peach that had an almond nut in the center instead of a hard stone.

LUTHER BURBANK 

159 He raised grapes and cherries and apples that were better than ever before. Once, he grew an apple that was half sweet and half sour.

He worked until he got fruit trees that could stand cold weather. He found a way to grow walnuts with thin shells and much meat instead of thick shells and little meat. He raised potatoes on tomato vines. He even joined the two together until he got a new vegetable which he called the pomato. 

Much of Luther Burbank's time was spent with the flowers he loved so well. He worked with the beautiful golden poppy that grows wild in California. 

Soon he was able to grow red poppies and white ones. 

He grew a new flower with beautiful white petals and a dark yellow center. He called it the Shasta daisy. It was named after a beautiful snow-covered mountain called Mt. Shasta.

He grew new kinds of roses. He raised gladiolas more colorful than any ever seen before. He took the white calla lily and worked until it had a perfume as lovely as the flower. He changed the dahlia and took away its bad smell.

There was no end to the wonderful things Luther Burbank could do with plants. He kept careful records of all of his great experiments. In that way he could tell just exactly what he had done to grow the new plants and flowers. Sometimes he would also write some of his thoughts. "The greatest happiness in the world is to make

OUR AMERICA 

others happy," he said. "The next greatest is to make them think." Another time he wrote, "If we love and admire our friends, let us tell them so today. It does them no harm, and they will pass along the kindness."

Although Luther Burbank worked quietly, the visitors who came to his gardens spread the news of his work. Great scientists and learned men came to visit him. He received more than fifty thousand letters a year. Of all the visitors, he liked best the school children who came to see his gardens.

Honors came to him from England, Sweden, Italy, and other nations. He was invited to speak at great universities. One famous scientist said of his work, "The flowers and fruits of California are less wonderful than the flowers and fruits which Mr. Burbank has made."

All his life he lived simply and worked hard. He kept on with his work until just before he died in 

1926. Now, he lies buried beneath a beautiful tree.

It is near the vine-covered cottage in Santa Rosa where he lived. He lived a full life, and a useful one. "I shall be content," he wrote one time, "if because of me there shall be better fruits and fairer flowers."

XIII 

JANE ADDAMS 

"She wanted to help people who were poor." 

About three years before the Pacific Railroad was finished, a little girl in Illinois decided she would spend her life helping those who were poor. 

In 1889, she started the work of Hull House in the poorest part of Chicago.

In that year the United States grew to be a country of forty-two states. Thomas Edison's electric light and Alexander Bell's telephone were being used more and more.

Luther Burbank was busy with his plants and trees and flowers. And Jane Addams of Hull

House began helping the poor to help themselves. 

Jane Addams liked to go riding with her father. It was so pleasant to sit back in the buggy while the horse trotted down the road. There were so many in- teresting things to see and talk about. And father

Addams was such a good listener. He always understood what a little girl meant, and he knew just how to explain things so that even a very little girl could understand. 

One day, when Jane was six years old, she went 161

OUR AMERICA 

with her father to a little city. In one part of the city she saw pretty homes. They had large, beautiful yards in which children could play. In another part of the city the houses were old and ugly. They were built close together. There was no place for children to play except in the streets. "Why do people have to live in such a horrid place?" she asked. "Why can't they all live in the nice, big houses?" Then Father Addams explained.

Some people were poor and some people were rich. 

Those who were poor did not have enough money to get a good house. Sometimes they could not even get enough to eat. "When I am grown up," replied Jane, "I am going to have a big house. I am going to build it where the poor people live. They can come to see me and the children can play in my yard."

Jane Addams lived in a little town in the state of Illinois. She was not so straight and strong as most children because her back had been made weak by a disease. Her mother had died soon after Jane's birth in 1860. So Mr. Addams had to be both father and mother to his little girl. Perhaps that was why she loved him so much.

Mr. Addams was a tall, strong, handsome man. 

He was also an important man. One of his friends was Abraham Lincoln, the President of the United

States. Sometimes Jane was afraid that her handsome father was ashamed of her.

JANE ADDAMS 

163 Once, when she went to Sunday school she saw some strangers in the church. She did not want them to know that her father had such a plain little girl with a crooked back. So she ran to her Uncle James. 

She walked home with him instead of with her father. 

One day, however, she met her father coming out of his bank. It was a busy street, and there were many people. Would he be ashamed of her and pretend not to see her? Mr. Addams smiled when he saw

Jane. He lifted his high, silk hat and bowed to her just as he would to the finest lady. Then Jane knew how foolish she had been to think that he was ashamed of her.

Jane's father loved her as much as she loved him. 

He wanted her to be well educated. He taught her many things at home. He encouraged her to read good books. When Jane became older, he sent her to a college. Very few women went to college at that time. After Jane finished her college work, she went on a trip to the different countries in Europe. 

Jane never forgot her wish to help people who were poor. In 1889, when she was twenty-nine years old, she came to Chicago. A friend, Ellen Starr, came with her. She, too, wanted to help others. 

They began to look for a large house where they could live among the poor. At last, they found a large, red-brick house. It was called Hull House because it had been built many years ago by a man

OUR AMERICA 

named Charles Hull. On all sides of it were the homes of the poorest people in Chicago. 

The two women rented a part of the house. They hired men to clean and paint it. Then they began to move in some furniture of their own. Beautiful rugs were laid on the floors. Lovely pictures were hung on the walls. A piano was put in one of the rooms. Before long the old house became a bright, cheerful home. 

In September, Jane Addams and Ellen Starr moved into Hull House. They were joined by Mary 

Brown Bros.

Hull House, where Jane Addams helped the poor

JANE ADDAMS 

165 Kayser, who came to do the housework for them. 

None of them was afraid to stay in the big house alone. They did not mind living in what was called the toughest and dirtiest part of Chicago. 

On one side of their house was a saloon. On the other side was an undertaking parlor. People from more than thirty different countries lived in the neighborhood. Some of them were Italians and Jews and Russians. Many had come from Ireland, Germany, Poland, Greece, and other far-away countries. 

Most of them were very, very poor. 

The two young women began to invite their neighbors to come to see them. At first, the people were suspicious. They could not believe that Jane

Addams and Ellen Starr only wanted to help them. 

Then a few of the women began to come. Some of them stayed for tea or supper. They liked the beautiful house and the friendly women who lived in it. 

Before long, the people began to trust Jane Addams. They learned that she really did want to help them. Mothers who had to work began to leave their babies at Hull House during the day. Girls who worked in the shops and factories began to come to the reading parties in the evenings. They liked to hear Ellen Starr read good stories to them.

Other women, who wanted to help the poor, came to Hull House. They offered to help Jane Addams with her work. One of them started a little class for girls. Another one started a kindergarten for the

OUR AMERICA 

Ewing Galloway

A play room in Hull House for children during the winter months children. Still another started the first boys' club. 

Years later the boys' club was so large that it used a five-story building all for its own. 

A great many interesting things happened that first year. One day a little Italian boy refused to sit beside a little Italian girl whose name was Angelina.

The kindergarten teacher asked him why he would not sit by Angelina. "We eat our macaroni this way," he said. Then he moved his hand as if he were using a fork to carry the food from his plate to his mouth. "But Angelina eats her macaroni this way." He held his hand up in

JANE ADDAMS 

167 the air and opened his mouth wide as if he were going to drop something into it. 

Once an Italian woman saw some beautiful roses in Hull House. "Isn't it wonderful," she said, "that these roses came all the way from Italy and are still so fresh?" She would not believe that they had been raised in America. She had never before seen roses in Chicago although there were many flower shops only a few blocks away. She thought that all of 

America was like the street on which she lived. 

As the weeks went by, Jane Addams and Ellen Starr were asked to help in many ways. They were asked to wash new-born babies and to care for the sick. They brought food and medicine to the poorest homes. They protected mothers and children from husbands and fathers who treated them unkindly. 

They tried to comfort children whose mothers or fathers had died.

Sometimes they even helped to wash floors and windows. Hour after hour, and day after day they worked. There was so much to be done and so little time in which to do it.

The work at Hull House also grew rapidly. More men and women came and offered to help carry on the work. Soon more room was needed. Hull House was becoming too small. Another building was obtained.

One part of the new building was made into a coffee shop. Here people could get good food to eat

OUR AMERICA 

at low prices. It was also a place where people could meet and talk together. 

A gymnasium was fixed up below the coffee house. 

Soon there was a fairly good library, and some rooms which could be used for dancing and parties. Still later, two buildings were rented and fixed up as a place where working girls could live. Then, as the years went by, building after building was added. 

Today, the work of Hull House is carried on in more than a dozen buildings.

Before long more than two thousand people were coming to Hull House each week. In the mornings the little children came to the kindergarten. In the afternoon the older children came to the different boys' and girls' clubs. In the evening the grown people came to their clubs and classes. Every room in the house was used every evening. "Young people need pleasure as truly as they need food and air," said Jane Addams. And what grand, new pleasures the poor people found in Hull House. There were games and parties and dances. 

Sometimes the clubs put on little plays. There were basket ball and swimming. There were meetings where men and women could talk about what was happening in the world. There were books and magazines and papers to read. 

There were also clubs and classes where people could learn about a great many things. Boys learned to use tools and to make things. Girls learned to sew

JANE ADDAMS 

169 and to make their own clothes. Men and women, too, learned about the things in which they were interested. 

Once an old blind man came to Jane Addams. He had been taking the part of a shepherd in a Christmas play. "Kind Heart," he said, "I have been waiting all of my life to hear some of these things." "Kind

Heart" was the name he always called Miss Addams. "We have the best times in our Hull House Club," exclaimed a girl who was one of the members. "It makes a girl glad she's alive."

At another time a little boy tossed a stone through a store window. The judge asked him why he had done it. "I did it on purpose," answered the boy. "I wanted to be sent to the school where the fellows learn to make things."

Three years after it had been started, Hull House was famous. Stories of the wonderful work which 

Jane Addams was doing spread all over the United States. Great men and women came from all parts of the world. Many of them came to Chicago only to visit Hull House. One of the visitors was the king of 

Belgium. Another was Theodore Roosevelt who became President of the United States.

Jane Addams soon was called the greatest woman in Chicago. Other men and women became interested in her work. Some of them helped by giving money to carry on the work at Hull House. One man

OUR AMERICA 

put up a whole new building for the boys' club. Another helped fix up an art gallery. 

One day a friend told Miss Addams that a certain man wanted to give her a large sum of money. 

Jane Addams knew that this man had not earned the money in a way that was right. So she refused to accept the gift. She wanted to teach him that people did not approve of his way of doing business. 

Another time Jane Addams was trying to get a law passed. It was a law that would keep men from hiring little children to do hard and dangerous work. 

Some of the business men did not want this law. They could make more money by using child labor. A few of these men came to Miss Addams. "We will give you fifty thousand dollars," they said, "if you will forget about this law." Jane

Addams looked straight at the men. She did not say a word for several minutes. They began to wish they had not come. "We have no wish to make Hull House so very large," replied Miss Addams. "But we do want very much to protect our people and their children. We want to protect them from bad and dangerous working conditions. We want to do this even if Hull House has to be destroyed."

In the years that followed, Jane Addams kept on fighting to make things better for the people. She made many enemies, but she made many more friends. She seemed never to become discouraged,

JANE ADDAMS 

171 and she never gave up. Slowly but surely conditions in the factories and shops where the people worked became better and better. 

When the great World War came, Jane Addams worked for a way to end the war. She believed as did the man who said, "There never was a good war, and there never was a bad peace." Many people made fun of her for this work. But in 1931, thirteen years after the war was over, she was given the 

Nobel Peace Prize. This was one of the greatest honors in the world.

During all of this time Hull House kept on growing. A new theater building was put up. A boys' camp was started. It was in a beautiful place many miles from the city. Here boys could go and live for a while in the fresh air and sunshine. A club in the country for grown people was also started. 

The classes and clubs kept on growing, too. In a few years the boys' club had more than fifteen hundred members. The University of Chicago started a number of new classes for men and women. Sometimes more people came than could be seated. One men's class, which met on Sunday night, had more than seven hundred members. More than six thousand people were now coming to Hull House each week.

Many of the boys and girls who came to Hull House became famous men and women. Some became great artists or musicians. Others became good

Jane Addams and a young artist whom she has helped 

Globe Photos

JANE ADDAMS 

173 lawyers or doctors or teachers. Still others became business men or writers or workers for the government. All that the poor boys and girls needed was a chance. Jane Addams gave them that chance. 

Most of them never forgot Hull House or Jane

Addams. A young newspaper man came to Miss 

Addams one day. "Do you remember me?" he asked. "I used to belong to a club in Hull House." "How did Hull House help you?" asked Jane Addams. "It was the first place I had ever seen," he replied, "where books and papers lay around as if there were plenty of them in the world. Don't you remember how I used to read at the little table in the back of the library?"

For more than forty years Jane Addams lived and worked at Hull House. She was never too busy to help someone who needed her. She treated all people kindly and fairly. She believed, as did Thomas Jefferson, that all people are equal. At last, in 1935, Jane 

Addams died, but the work at Hull House goes on. 

Many honors came to Jane Addams during her life. She accepted them and then went on with her unselfish work. She was often called "the uncrowned queen of America." One noted visitor said she was the only saint in America. Many other fine titles were also given her. Perhaps better than anything else, she liked to be called "Jane Addams, the good neighbor."

XIV 

GUTZON BORGLUM 

"He is making giants that will live forever."

The men and women of today are adding to the story of America. They are doing their part just as surely as did those who lived in other years. 

A part of this story is told in the pictures of great artists. One of them was beginning his work when Jane Addams started Hull House.

Today he is cutting the figures of four great Americans into the side of a mountain. The artist is Gutzon Borglum.

A mountain of solid stone is coming to life. An artist with a great idea is putting life into it. The artist is Gutzon Borglum. The idea is to cut the figures of great American men into the side of this mountain.

The mountain is in the Black Hills of South 

Dakota. It is called Mount Rushmore. The figures of four men are being cut into the side of this mountain. They are George Washington, Thomas Jefferson, Abraham Lincoln, and Theodore Roosevelt.

Gutzon Borglum has been cutting figures out of 174

GUTZON BORGLUM 

175 

Publishers' Photo Service 

Mount Rushmore, where Gutzon Borglum is cutting giant figures of four great Americans. The heads of Washington and 

Jefferson are seen in this picture. stone for a long time. Sometimes he has shaped them with his hands in wet clay. At other times he has drawn or painted them. He has been an artist for many years.

He was born in Idaho in 1867. His father was a doctor in the wild western country of that time. Both

OUR AMERICA 

his father and mother were born in Denmark. They were well educated. In the language of Denmark the name Gutzon means "good son." One of his brothers,

Solon Borglum, also became a great artist. 

The family moved to Nebraska when Gutzon was a little boy. Nebraska, too, was still a wild country.

Many people passed by on their way to the west. 

Gutzon liked to watch the horses and wagons moving across the country. Later he made some very famous figures of horses.

He was an active boy. Once, when he was seven years old, he ran away from home. He lived for three days with a settler near the town. Many years later he said, "I don't know why I ran away. My life at home was always very pleasant. I guess it was just because I was bubbling over with life. I wanted to be doing something new."

Even when he was very young he liked to draw pictures. His parents were sure that he would be an artist. They encouraged him to keep on with his drawing. His mother got some books on art and read them to him. His father read to him in the evening.

He read stories about the lives of great men. Gutzon liked these stories, but he liked to draw pictures better. 

Soon Gutzon was sent to a boys' school in Kansas. 

It was called St. Mary's. He kept on with his drawing. The priests liked to have him draw pictures of saints. One day he drew some pictures of Indians

GUTZON BORGLUM 

177 and wild horses. He was scolded for this. Later he ran away from school and returned home. 

For a while he went to a public school. Again he kept on with his drawing. He did not get any special training such as pupils now receive. Then his family moved to Los Angeles, California. He spent nearly all of his time painting pictures of Indians and horses. He sold some of these pictures and saved the money. When he had enough money he went to an art school in San Francisco.

Several years later he sold some more paintings. 

He used the money to go to Paris, France, where he studied art for several years. He traveled in Holland and Belgium. While he was in France he made a figure of a horse standing over a dead Indian. Everyone who saw it liked it. This work brought him his first great honor.

Later he went to England and Spain. He lived in 

London, England, for six years. He had no money when he went to London. Somehow, he was able to rent a hall. Into this he put his paintings and figures of Indians and horses. He invited people to come and see his art work. His work was so well liked that the 

Queen of England sent for him. She bought one of his pictures. 

When he was thirty-four years old he returned to America. He began to work in New York. Soon he made another figure which became a famous work of art. It showed some frightened horses being taken

OUR AMERICA 

away by a horse thief. Gutzon Borglum has sometimes been called "the man who rode to fame on horseback." 

The figure of General Phil Sheridan is one of Mr. Borglum's most famous pieces of work. General 

Sheridan was a brave leader during the Civil War. 

One day he made a famous ride to tell his soldiers to turn back and fight. Mr. Borglum's figure of General 

Sheridan shows him seated on a beautiful horse.

While Mr. Borglum was making this, he asked 

General Sheridan's son to help him. The young man would ride up to the artist. Then he would stop the horse quickly. Mr. Borglum watched carefully until he knew just how the figure of the horse should be made.

Gutzon Borglum also made two wonderful figures of Abraham Lincoln. One of them is in the United

States Capitol Building in Washington, D. C. The other is perhaps the better. It is in Newark, New 

Jersey. It shows Lincoln seated on one end of a bench. 

It looks as if Lincoln were sitting alone in his garden and thinking. It is so real that it does not seem to be a statue. Many people come to sit on the bench beside the figure of Lincoln. Sometimes children sit on his lap. His arms seem to be holding them. It is just what Lincoln would do if he were alive.

As the years go by, Gutzon Borglum becomes more and more famous as an artist and sculptor. But he is interested in other things, too. He likes to box. Once

GUTZON BORGLUM 

179 he boxed against the champion of the world. He likes farming and engineering and politics. He likes to study about airplanes. He enjoys horseback riding and fishing. He owns a large farm in Connecticut on which he lives.

He made speeches for Theodore Roosevelt when he was running for President. He did some government work while Woodrow Wilson was President. 

He worked for better roads in his county until they were built. He believes that a person should take part in what is going on about him. "An artist should be interested in everything," says Mr. Borglum.

In 1915 he was asked to come to Atlanta, Georgia. 

Some ladies wanted him to cut a figure of General Robert E. Lee on a stone mountain. They drove out to look at the mountain. He saw at once that it was too large for the little figure they wanted. "A figure of General Lee on Stone Mountain," he said, "would look like a stamp on the side of a house. 

It must be something larger than that." He stayed at a house near the mountain for several days. A plan began to come to his mind. Why not have hundreds of large figures marching across that mountain? 

He made a drawing of his plan. Then he went back to Atlanta. He showed it to the ladies who wanted the figure of General Lee. They liked it very much. 

He made talks and explained his plan to others. Some of them said it couldn't be done. But Mr. Borglum went ahead with his plan.

OUR AMERICA 

He wanted to make Stone Mountain tell a great story. It was to be the story of men who were not afraid to fight for what they believed to be right.

They were the men who fought for the South in the Civil War of 1861 to 1865. The mountain was to be a great monument to their memory. 

His plan was to cut the figures of about twelve hundred men into the side of the mountain. From below they would look like a long line of marching men. 

Some of them were to be on horseback. Others would be walking. At the head of the line would be important leaders, such as General Robert E. Lee, Jefferson

Davis, and General Thomas (Stonewall) Jackson. 

The real work on Stone Mountain did not start until 1922, four years after the World War was over. 

First, Mr. Borglum made a little model of the figures which were to be cut on the mountain. Tools for cutting into the stone were brought. Machinery was fixed so that men could work on the steep sides of the mountain.

It was necessary to put an outline of the figures on the mountain. Then the men would know where to cut away the rock. But how could he put a good outline on the side of the mountain? At last, he worked out a way.

He made a large magic lantern with a very strong light. Then he drew pictures of the figures on glass slides. In this way he could throw the pictures on the mountain. It was just like putting pictures on a

GUTZON BORGLUM 

181 screen in a moving picture theater. Of course, his magic lantern worked only when it was dark. 

When the outline of the figures was thrown on the mountain, men painted a white line around the edges. 

This showed the men where to cut away the rock so that the figures would stand out. 

Stone Mountain was planned as a monument to the men of the South who were not afraid to fight for what they believed to be right.

OUR AMERICA 

Because of the great size of Stone Mountain, the figures had to be very large. Otherwise they could not be seen by persons standing below the mountain. 

Some of the figures were more than a hundred feet high. It was about twenty feet from the top of General Lee's head to his chin. The figure of a man seated on a horse was as high as a sixteen-story building. The lower part of a horse's leg was as large as a barrel. 

After several years, the people in charge of the work and Mr. Borglum began to disagree on how the work was to be done. Mr. Borglum left. Another man was put in his place. A little while later the money gave out. Since that time no work has been done on 

Stone Mountain. Perhaps some day the work will be finished.

In the meantime, Gutzon Borglum was asked to come to South Dakota. Some people there wanted him to cut some giant figures on Mount Rushmore. In 

1927 he began work on the figure of George Washington. Thomas Jefferson was the next to be started. 

Then Abraham Lincoln and Theodore Roosevelt will follow. A short history of the United States will also be cut into the mountain. 

The magic lantern could not be used in marking the outlines on Mount Rushmore. He had to make small models of his figures. He measured them carefully. Perhaps it was only an inch between two different parts of a face. But an inch on the little model might be equal to a foot on the big figures.

GUTZON BORGLUM 

183 

Publishers' Photo Service 

Gutzon Borglum at work on the model of 

Mount Rushmore Memorial 

Then he measured how much of the rock to cut away. 

In this way Mount Rushmore is slowly coming to life.

The figures on Mount Rushmore are even larger than those on Stone Mountain. George Washington's head is as high as a five-story building. It will be more than two hundred feet from his waist to the top of his head. Washington's nose is a little longer than the entire head of the famous Sphinx in Egypt. 

It will take many years of work to finish cutting these figures. But they will last until the mountains wear away. People who live a thousand years from

OUR AMERICA 

Publishers' Photo Service 

The workmen are cutting away rock to make the eyes of one of the figures on Mount Rushmore. now will be able to see them. Perhaps they might even last a million years.

In the years to come, someone will surely look at them and say, "Who cut these great giants into this mountain?" Then another story will be told. It will be the story of the man who is bringing a mountain to life. It will be the story of Gutzon Borglum, an artist with a great idea.

XV WALTER DISNEY 

"He makes millions of people laugh."

After the day's work is done, people like to rest and play. But the ways of finding pleasure have changed as America has changed. 

Thomas A. Edison's invention of the moving picture machine led to a new kind of entertainment. For more than twenty-five years now, great numbers of people have found pleasure at the movies. 

When Gutzon Borglum began the great figures on Mount Rushmore, a young man in Hollywood was making a new movie hero. The young man was Walter Disney. The new movie hero was 

Mickey Mouse. 

There is a very special white building in Hollywood, California. It is the home of Mickey Mouse. 

Of course, everyone knows Mickey Mouse. He is a funny little fellow with big ears and a long tail. Millions of people go to the movies to see him. They like to laugh at Mickey and Minnie Mouse and Pluto the

Pup. Mickey can do almost anything. His life is just one grand adventure after another. 185

OUR AMERICA 

universaire ouse de Dickey 

Bemmage à

Dalt Disney unication téléphoniqu ollywood Fusion par le it Français

Courtesy, Walter Disney Productions, Ltd. 

Walter Disney. The paper he is holding contains a message written in French for Mickey Mouse's seventh birthday.

And that is where Walter Disney comes into the picture. Walter Disney is Mickey's "papa." He plans all of Mickey's wonderful adventures. He makes 

Mickey and Minnie and Pluto the Pup do all of the things people like to see in the movies. 

Walter Disney was born in Chicago in December, 1901. He had three brothers and one sister. His father was a builder. Walt lived much as any other boy of that time. There was only one way in which he seemed to be different.

WALTER DISNEY 

187 "As far back as I can remember," he said one time, "I was interested in drawing." None of his family was interested in art, but his aunt encouraged him. 

She kept him supplied with pencils and paper. One of the neighbors also encouraged him. This neighbor was an old doctor. He gave Walt some little presents for his drawings. "Once when I was about seven years old," said Mr. 

Disney, "I made a drawing of his horse. He stood and held it while I drew. The drawing was really very poor, but the old doctor liked it. Of course, I was proud to think that he liked my picture of his horse." 

But Walt was interested in other things, too. When the family moved to Kansas City, Missouri, Walt began to carry newspapers before he went to school in the morning. For a while, he wanted to become an artist and draw cartoons for a newspaper. At another time, he wanted to be an actor. He liked to take part in the school plays. Once he won some prizes doing imitations of Charlie Chaplin. Walt was also interested in sports. He was a member of the school track team. 

Walter Disney had many experiences before he was twenty years old. He sold papers and candy on a train. He worked for a little while as an actor in a 

Chicago theater. Next he got a job as a mail carrier. 

The World War was then being fought. So he left his job as a mail carrier. He went to France as a driver for the American Red Cross.

OUR AMERICA 

When he returned from France in 1919, he got a job as an artist. He worked for an advertising company in Kansas City, Missouri. His job was to draw pictures for advertisements. He made drawings of hens eating a certain kind of chicken feed, and of cattle licking a block of salt, and of farmers using a certain kind of machinery.

Several months later he got a new job. He had to draw pictures, or cartoons, on glass slides. These slides were then shown in moving picture theaters. 

The company for which he worked also made moving picture films. These films were made by taking pictures of cartoons. When the film was shown the cartoon figures seemed to move. 

Walt Disney soon became interested in making these cartoon films. He borrowed an old moving picture camera from the company. He began to experiment with it. Then he made a short cartoon film of his own. The film showed things that were happening in Kansas City. Walt sold the film to one of the moving picture theaters in the city. 

This work gave him an idea. Why not make cartoon films about the old fairy tales? He got some young artists to help him in the evenings. They began to make a cartoon film of "Little Red Riding Hood."

Six months later the picture was ready. Walt then started a company to make and sell these cartoon moving pictures. Seven films were made, but the new company soon went out of business.

WALTER DISNEY 

189 "There was nothing left for me to do in Kansas 

City," said Mr. Disney some years later. "So I decided to go to Hollywood. I came to Hollywood in

August, 1923. I had a suit of clothes that was full of holes and a suit case full of drawing materials. I only had forty dollars. What was worse, I had plenty of debts back in Kansas City."

Walter Disney's brother, Roy, was living in California. They decided to go into business together. 

Roy had two hundred and fifty dollars. They borrowed five hundred dollars more from an uncle. Then they started to make their first moving picture. It was to be a picture about a little girl named Alice who played with the fairies.

They had to live very cheaply while they were making their first picture. For a while they ate in a cafeteria. They would go in together. One of them would get a meat order. The other would get some vegetables. Then they would go to a table and divide the meat and vegetables. Later, they rented a room where they cooked, ate, and slept. "When I remember all this," said Walter Disney one time, "it seems that we had a lot of fun in those days." 

The "Alice" pictures were not very successful.

Finally, Walter Disney stopped making them. He began to make some new cartoon moving pictures. 

They were about "Oswald, the Lucky Rabbit." The "Oswald" pictures soon became quite successful.

OUR AMERICA 

After a while, Walter and the company that sold the "Oswald" films could no longer agree. So Walter had to stop making the "Oswald" pictures, too. 

Walter Disney began to make plans for other cartoon pictures. He had been in Hollywood about five years. He knew that other people were using cats and dogs and rabbits in their pictures. About the only thing they had not used was a mouse. 

He liked the idea of using a mouse as the hero of a moving picture. When he was working in Kansas 

City the mice would come to look for bits of food in his office. He caught some of them and put them in a cage on his desk. One of them became quite tame and crawled all over his desk while he worked. 

Walter gave this mouse a pet name. 

So Walter Disney decided to make his next pictures about a mouse. But what would he call the mouse? At first he thought of the name "Mortimer

Mouse." This did not seem to fit. He finally decided on the name of "Mickey Mouse." Of course, Mickey had to have a girl friend. She was called "Minnie

Mouse." Later, other characters such as Pluto the 

Pup were added.

The first Mickey Mouse film was made in the garage at Walter Disney's home. He wanted to keep his new picture a secret until it was finished. When it was ready, he tried to sell it to a moving picture company. He tried one company after another. No one seemed to want the Mickey Mouse picture.

WALTER DISNEY 

191 

Courtesy, Walter Disney Productions, Ltd. 

This artist is making an outline drawing of Mickey Mouse. 

Walter still did not lose hope. He made two more 

Mickey Mouse pictures. The third one was finished during the summer of 1928. It was called "Steamboat Willie." In the meantime, a wonderful thing had happened to the movies. A way had been found to make sound or talking pictures. Walt Disney de-

OUR AMERICA 

cided to make "Steamboat Willie" into one of the new sound pictures.

Late in September, 1928, this picture was shown in a New York theater. The people liked Mickey at once. They laughed until their sides hurt. Before long almost every theater in the country was trying to get Mickey Mouse pictures. Mickey made new friends everywhere. Hundreds of letters began to come to Walt Disney. Some of them were written to 

Mr. Disney. But perhaps most of them were sent to 

Mr. Mickey Mouse, Hollywood, California.

Mickey Mouse soon became known all over the world. His pictures were made in several different languages. In Greece, Mickey is known as "Mickel 

Mus." In Italy his name is "Michele Jopolino." In

Japan he appears in the theaters as "Miki Kuchi." 

In Germany, he is known as "Mickey Maus," in 

France as "Michel Souris," and in Spain as "Miguel

Ratonocito." He is even more popular in many of these countries than he is in the United States. 

Before long Walter Disney began to make some new moving pictures. They were called the "Silly 

Symphonies." These pictures, too, had a hard time getting started. But the people liked them as soon as they saw the pictures. The most popular of the "Silly Symphonies" was that of the "Three Little Pigs." 

Walt Disney no longer makes his Mickey Mouse pictures in a garage. He now has an up-to-date

WALTER DISNEY 

193 

WALT DISNEY STUDIOS MICKEY MOUSE WILLY JYMPHONY SOUND CARTOONS 

Walt Disney Studios where the Mickey Mouse moving pictures are made studio. More than four hundred persons work there. 

About two hundred and seventy-five of them are artists. The rest are camera men, sound men, and other workers. Walt now makes one Mickey Mouse picture every four weeks. He also makes one Silly 

Symphony every four weeks.

The first step in making a Mickey Mouse picture is to plan the story. Walt calls a meeting of some of his artists. They decide upon the adventures Mickey is to have. Of course, Mickey has to be the hero. In several pictures Walt Disney let some of the other characters get the best of Mickey. The people did not like this. They wanted Mickey to win out at least in the end.

OUR AMERICA 

It is hard to work out a good story which will please everyone. It must please the children who will see the picture. It must also please the grown-ups who like the Mickey Mouse movies. Walter Disney has found that children and grown-ups do not always laugh at the same thing. This means that he must put something into each picture that both old and young will enjoy.

After Mickey's adventures have been planned, the story is divided into scenes, or parts. One scene might show Minnie Mouse in danger. Another scene might AA 

PLUTO Courtesy, Walter Disney Productions, Ltd. 

One of Walter Disney's artists working on characters that are well-known

WALTER DISNEY 

195 show how Pluto the Pup gets in Mickey's way when he tries to save Minnie. The artists then plan the pictures to show the scenes and tell the story. 

When the story is finished, it is given to another group of men. Their job is to find music which will fit the story. The music has to be the same as Mickey's feelings. If Mickey is happy, then the music is light and gay. If Mickey is feeling bad, then the music is slow and sad.

The plans for the story and the music are next given to the artists. They begin to draw the pictures. 

About twelve thousand different drawings are needed for each Mickey Mouse picture. If the picture is to be a color film, the drawings are made with colors. 

When the drawings are ready, a camera takes the pictures of them. In this way, the drawings get on the film which is shown in the theaters. 

The next step is to fix the film so that it will be a sound, or talking picture. Each of the characters in the film has some one who talks for him. A large orchestra plays the music. Many sound effects are used. An egg beater is used to make the sound of machinery. An electric fan sounds like an airplane. 

Walter Disney has learned much about making Mickey Mouse pictures. He has always tried to make each one better than the others. He wants Mickey to be a gentleman in all of his pictures. Mickey must never be cruel or unkind. He must never act too smart without being sorry afterwards.

OUR AMERICA 

Perhaps that is why Mickey is liked so well. Anyway, Mickey Mouse has become one of America's best loved movie stars. His pictures have been put on all kinds of goods. There are Mickey Mouse dishes and books and there are Mickey Mouse adventures in the newspapers. There are Mickey Mouse games and toys. 

His picture has even been put on milk bottles. "I am just as surprised at the success of Mickey 

Mouse as anyone else," said Walter Disney one day. "Sometimes I feel like pinching myself to see if I am dreaming. But, of course, Mickey's success makes me happy."

Walter Disney lives in Hollywood with his family. 

His brother, Roy, is still his partner. Roy takes care of the business matters. For many years they have worked hard to build up their company. Once Walt's health began to fail. Since then he has taken more time for rest and play.

Perhaps more than any other person, Walter Disney has given a new kind of entertainment to the people. He has made millions of them laugh. He has made them forget their troubles for a while. He has brought happiness and joy into the lives of people in all parts of the world. 

What does Mickey Mouse think of all this? He wrinkles his nose and smiles a great, big smile. He looks at Walt Disney and winks his eye. Then he begins to laugh in his own funny way. "Heh! Heh! You know me!"

XVI 

JULIUS ROSENWALD 

"He built a great business.”

Among the builders of this country are the men of business. At first the stores and factories and other businesses were very small. 

In the years after the Pacific Railroad was built, there was great growth for American business. 

Many of the large businesses of today began during this time.

One of them began with a small store in Chicago about five years after Jane Addams opened Hull House and about seven years before Walter Disney was born. The man who built the single store into a great business was Julius Rosenwald.

Across the street from the home of Abraham Lincoln lived a Jewish family. The Rosenwalds liked to look over at the plain, little home of the great man. 

Perhaps, when he was no longer President of the

United States, he would come back and live there again. 

Perhaps little Julius Rosenwald might even go over and play in his yard. Everyone knew that the great Abraham Lincoln loved children, and Julius 197

OUR AMERICA 

would no longer be a baby then. "What a fine thing that would be," thought Mother Rosenwald as she looked at her baby. 

Julius Rosenwald was born in Springfield, Illinois, in 1862. When Julius was about two and a half years old Abraham Lincoln was killed. He was brought back to his home in Springfield and buried. The 

Rosenwalds, like everyone else in the United States, were saddened by the sudden death of the great President. 

When Julius grew older his mother told him stories about the man whose home stood across the street. Perhaps she hoped that he, too, might some day become a great man. The years passed, and 

Julius was sent to school. He did all kinds of odd jobs after school.

For a while he earned money by pumping a church organ. He sold newspapers and ran errands and carried packages. Once he sold some pictures. At another time he sold some little books about the life of Abraham Lincoln. 

There was a celebration in Springfield that day. 

General U. S. Grant was a visitor. He wore some yellow gloves. Julius liked to look at the famous general, but again and again his eyes turned to the general's yellow gloves. He had never before seen a man wearing fancy gloves.

One summer Julius got a job in a store. He had to wait on the customers when they came in. He soon

JULIUS ROSENWALD 

199 learned the best ways of selling goods. It was good training for him. Many years later he became one of the greatest merchants in the world. 

His wages were not very large, but he carefully saved his money. There was something that Julius wanted very much to buy. It was a china tea set. His brother was also saving money to help buy the china tea set. At the end of summer Julius had saved about twenty dollars. His brother had about the same amount. 

They took their money and bought the china tea set. They carried it home and carefully unpacked it early the next morning. They spread it out on the table. Then they waited until their mother came down to breakfast. Julius and his brother wanted to surprise her with the beautiful present. It was the day on which she had been married twenty years. "That was a big day for us boys," said Julius

Rosenwald when he was telling the story many years afterward.

When he was sixteen years old he went to New York. He got a job working in his uncle's store. He had to do all kinds of work. He swept out the store and ran errands and helped wait on the customers.

Again he learned much about how to run a business. 

What was most useful of all, he learned to keep his eyes and ears open.

After a while he left his uncle's store. He got a new job with a company that made and sold clothing.

OUR AMERICA 

He was interested in much more than his job as a clerk. Whenever he could spare the time, he would watch the workers. He learned how the parts of a suit were cut from the cloth and how those parts were sewed together. He saw how the lining was put in, and how the buttons were fastened. He learned as much as he could about how clothes were made. 

One day he discovered that the company got many orders for clothing in the mail. The orders came from people who lived in other towns. This gave Julius 

Rosenwald a great idea. He went back to Springfield and explained his plan to his father. He wanted to start a business of his own and sell clothing by mail as well as over the counter.

Mr. Rosenwald agreed to help Julius get started in the new business. They decided Chicago was the best city in which to start. Julius got one of his cousins to be his partner. They rented a part of a building in 

Chicago. Then they began to make clothing. Soon they had a good supply ready. Now they could begin to sell it.

Julius was only twenty-three years old, but he had a good business training. His new business was a success from the very first. It grew larger and larger. 

Julius saved a part of each year's profits. 

Nine years later he met a business man whose name was Richard W. Sears. He was the owner of a business called Sears, Roebuck and Company. This company sold watches and jewelry by mail. Julius

JULIUS ROSENWALD 

201 

Courtesy, Julius Rosenwald Fund Julius Rosenwald 

Rosenwald and Richard Sears decided to become partners. They began to sell clothing as well as jewelry and watches. The new business kept the old name of Sears, Roebuck and Company. Mr. Sears was the president, and Julius Rosenwald was the vice-president and treasurer.

For a long time Julius Rosenwald had been gathering ideas. He had done a great deal of thinking

OUR AMERICA 

about new and better ways of doing business. He decided to try some of his new ideas. One of the first things he did was to let customers know they could get their money back if they were not satisfied with the goods.

He believed that it was only fair to let people see what they had ordered by mail. He wanted them to be satisfied with what they bought. It was more than an honest way of treating the customer. It was also a clever way of getting people to buy more goods from his company.

People soon learned that they really could get their money back if they were not pleased. Orders began to pour into the offices of Sears, Roebuck and Company. They began to sell goods other than clothing and jewelry. Before long they were selling thousands of different articles. 

In 1908, Mr. Sears decided to retire from the business. He was a wealthy man and wanted to spend the rest of his life away from work. Julius Rosenwald became the new president of the company. 

In less than twenty-five years he made his company one of the largest in the world. He sold more goods than any other merchant in the country. At one time his sales were equal to fifteen dollars a year for each family in the United States.

Julius Rosenwald was a very modest man. He would not take any credit for his great success. "The man at the top," he said, "often gets too much credit.

JULIUS ROSENWALD 

203 It is the able, willing fellows around a man who really do things. What could any man do if no one would help him?"

Another one of his ideas about business was to treat his workers well. He made it possible for them to buy shares in his company. Then the workers. could also get a part of the profits. He worked out a plan to help them save money. He was always kind and friendly to them. He was glad to listen to their ideas. If the ideas were good, he was willing to reward the person who had thought of them. 

Once there was a great depression. Times were very hard all over the country. Business was no longer good. For a while it looked as if the workers would lose all the savings they had put into the company. Julius Rosenwald did not want his workers to suffer. So he used several million dollars of his own money to keep them from losing anything. 

He was never too busy to remember his mother. 

When she came to his office he stopped whatever he was doing to talk with her. After a while she became too old to travel about. Then he went to see her every morning before he went to work. He thought much more of his mother than he did of his business. 

Julius Rosenwald was more than a great business man. He was a good citizen. The mayor of Chicago asked him to be on a committee to prevent crime. Mr. 

Rosenwald was a busy man, but he felt it was his duty to help. The Governor of Illinois asked him to

OUR AMERICA 

help work out a plan to make different races of people feel more friendly toward each other. Again he did his duty.

He served for twenty-two years on another committee. Its work was to make plans so that Chicago would be a better city. He fought to get good men elected to important offices in the city government. 

He fought against men who were not honest. He helped Jane Addams of Hull House keep on doing her great work among the poor. 

When the World War came, Woodrow Wilson, the President of the United States, asked him to help. 

Once more he agreed. He gave advice to the men in the United States Government. He helped to buy food and clothing and supplies for the army. 

During the war many people in Europe and Asia suffered greatly. They were driven from their homes. 

All of their property was destroyed or taken away from them.

The Jewish people in eastern Europe, perhaps, suffered more than any other group. In 1917, more than six million of them needed food and clothing and help. 

A committee was formed to get money to help them. 

One of the men on the committee went to see Julius 

Rosenwald. Mr. Rosenwald was then living in a hotel in Washington, D. C. Late one evening the man told him about the suffering of the Jews in the war lands. He asked Mr. Rosenwald to make a large gift of one million dollars to help them.

JULIUS ROSENWALD 

205 "Do you think it will do any good?" asked Mr.

Rosenwald. 

"Yes," replied the man. "Very well, I will do it," said Julius Rosenwald. "You may go back to New York and tell them that

I'll do it." 

The news of his great gift was sent all over the world. Before long the committee had raised ten million dollars. They used it to buy food and supplies for the suffering Jews in Europe. 

The example set by Mr. Rosenwald made it possible for the American Red Cross to raise ten times as much for their work. When Woodrow Wilson heard of the gift, he sent a message to Julius Rosenwald praising the noble deed.

Of course, Julius Rosenwald made a great deal of money from his business. He became one of the richest men in the United States. But he did not care about money for itself. He was not the selfish kind of business man. Once he said, "It is a shame for a man to pile up money after he has enough for himself and his family."

He used his great riches and power to make life better for others. When he was fifty years old he gave away almost one million dollars. But he did not give his money foolishly.

He gave a large sum to the University of Chicago. 

He helped both the Young Men's Christian Association (the Y.M.C.A.) and the Young Women's Chris-

OUR AMERICA 

tian Association (the Y.W.C.A.). He also helped clubs which were a part of the Catholic Church. 

It made no difference to him to which race or church a person belonged. He was very much inter- 

A large gift by Julius Rosenwald made this Chicago building into a famous museum. ested in the negroes. He built an apartment house in 

Chicago for negroes. He helped start a negro

Y.M.C.A. Still more important was his work in starting schools for the negroes in the south. 

Many of the southern states could not afford to give negro boys and girls a good education. Mr. 

Rosenwald made it possible to build more than five thousand schools for them. Most of these schools were built in the poorest parts of these states. 

More than six hundred thousand negro boys and girls go to these schools. They have about fifteen thousand teachers. Almost all of the teachers are negroes. Mr. Rosenwald also gave money to help

JULIUS ROSENWALD 

207 

Courtesy, Julius Rosenwald Fund 

The Julius Rosenwald Fund replaced this old schoolhouse in Georgia with a new one like that on the next page. negro colleges and universities. "Why are you doing so much to help the negro," he was asked one time. "I am interested in America," he replied. "I do not see how America can go ahead if part of its people are left behind. That is why I help the negroes." 

Julius Rosenwald was like Abraham Lincoln in many ways. Both lived a simple life. Both had a great heart that was open to every good cause. Both tried hard to be good citizens at all times. Each did what he could in his own way for people who needed help. 

Before Julius Rosenwald died in 1932, he worked

OUR AMERICA 

Courtesy, Julius Rosenwald Fund 

One of the school buildings for negroes in Tennessee made possible by the Julius Rosenwald Fund out a plan to use his money for the good of others. 

He started the Julius Rosenwald Fund. He decided to leave most of his money to this fund. He said that it must all be spent within twenty-five years after his death. He believed that the people should have the benefit of his riches now and not many years later. 

Julius Rosenwald is dead, but his work lives on. 

The Julius Rosenwald Fund is carrying out his wishes. It is using his money to make life better for mankind. Its work can be seen in almost any of the southern states. "Doing good for others," he once said, "is the one pleasure which never wears out."

XVII THEODORE ROOSEVELT 

"He worked to make the government better.” 

Every country needs a good government if its people are to be happy. Benjamin Franklin and 

Thomas Jefferson helped to give the government of the United States a good start.

As state after state was added, and new inventions were made, some of America's ways of living began to change. From time to time, the people also made changes in their government. 

During the years when business grew so fast, a number of new laws and other government changes were needed. So while Julius Rosenwald was building a great business, Theodore Roosevelt was building a better government. 

Many of America's great men were country boys from poor families. Theodore Roosevelt was different. 

He was a city boy from a rich family. 

Theodore Roosevelt was born in New York City on

October 27, 1858. It was about two years before 

Abraham Lincoln was chosen to be President of the United States. The Roosevelt family was an old and well-known American family. The first Roosevelt 209

OUR AMERICA 

had come to America in 1644, twenty-four years after the Pilgrims landed at Plymouth.

Theodore was a weak, sickly boy. He suffered much from asthma. This often made it hard for him to breathe. Sometimes he would wake up at night, gasping for breath. When he could not sleep, his father would carry him in his arms and walk the floor hour after hour.

The family took him on many trips to find a place where he could breathe easier. They would often go to a home in the country. Theodore and the other children liked it there. There was so much fun to be had, and so many pets with which to play. One of the best-loved pets was a pony named General Grant. 

Theodore had one brother and two sisters. He played most with his brother, Elliott, and his younger sister, Corinne. He called them "Ellie" and "Conie."

They called him "Teedie," but later he was called "Teddy." Of course, there were many other playmates. One of them was Edith Carow. Many years later, she and Theodore Roosevelt were married.

One of the things the children liked best was to hear their Aunt Anna tell stories. She knew ever so many wonderful stories about "Br'er Rabbit." Teddy also could tell exciting stories to the other children. He made them up himself. Sometimes he would keep adding to a story for weeks at a time. 

Teddy did not go to school. He was taught at home instead. He learned to become a good reader. He did

THEODORE ROOSEVELT 

211 not skip the big words just because they looked hard. 

That was too much like quitting, and Teddy was no quitter. He soon began to read a great many books. 

One of them was a large book for grown-up people. 

It told the story of David Livingstone's travels in Africa. The stories he liked best were about men such as Daniel Boone, Natty Bumpo, and Davy Crockett.

Together with his father, these men were his heroes. 

When he was nine years old, he started to keep a diary. He also decided to write a book. For a long time he had been interested in all kinds of animals. He called his book a Natural History on Insects. It was written in several pages of an old notebook. His ideas were much better than his spelling. 

He was still sick much of the time. His body was thin and weak. The asthma seemed to trouble him as much as ever. In addition to this, he had poor eyes. He could not see well. During most of his life he had to wear thick heavy glasses.

Teddy was about eleven years old when his father talked to him one day. "Theodore," he said, "you have the mind, but you have not the body. Without the help of the body, the mind cannot go as far as it should. 

You must make your body. It is hard work to make one's body, but I know you will do it." 

The boy looked at his father. He threw his head back. His eyes were bright. "I'll make my body," he answered. 

A little later something happened that made him

OUR AMERICA 

remember this promise. He was sent to a place in 

Maine where the air was cool and clear. On the way, he met two boys about his own age. They began to make life unpleasant for Teddy.

He stood it as long as he could. Then he tried to fight them. The boys took him one at a time. Each was able to handle him like a kitten. They did not even hurt him because he was so helpless in their hands. 

For a long time Teddy had wanted to be like the heroes in his favorite books. He was brave enough, but the fight showed him how weak he really was. He made up his mind never again to be so helpless. He began to take boxing lessons. "I was a slow and awkward pupil," he said later. "I worked two or three years before I began to improve." There were other ways, too, in which he tried to build his body. He made good use of the gymnasium in his home. He went horseback riding and took long walks.

His interest in birds and animals helped to keep him outdoors. One winter the family took him to 

Egypt. Here he spent much of his time riding about on a donkey. He went out looking for wild birds to put in his collection. When he caught one, he would skin and stuff it.

This was good fun and training for Teddy, but it was hard on the rest of the family. If Teddy thought it was necessary to keep a bird or an animal in his

THEODORE ROOSEVELT 

213 room, he kept it there. If he thought some other part of the house was better, he made use of that place. 

The Roosevelt family could never be sure of what they would find next.

In 1876, Theodore Roosevelt went to Harvard University in Massachusetts. That was the year in which Alexander Bell invented the telephone. By this time Teddy's health was better. He was able to take part in many different sports. But even yet, his body was not strong enough.

He kept on with his boxing and wrestling. If he won a match, he was a good sport about it. If he lost, he was still a good sport. Once he was boxing with a boy when time was called.

Teddy dropped his hands to his side. The other boy hit him a hard blow right on the nose. "Foul! Foul!" shouted the boys who were watching the match. 

Teddy's face was covered with blood. "Stop,” he said. "He didn't hear! He didn't hear!" Then he shook the other boy's hand and went on with the match. 

During his first year in college, he made another trip to Maine. He went camping and hunting with a woodsman, named Bill Sewall. They made a strange pair, but they soon became fast friends. 

From Bill Sewall, Teddy learned to love the great wilderness country. He also learned what men like 

Bill Sewall thought and believed. He began to understand their ways of living. All of this was very im-

OUR AMERICA 

portant for the young man who would some day be 

President of the United States. 

"We got along well from the start," said Bill Sewall one time. "He wouldn't let anyone carry his gun or help him in any way. He never tried to get out of anything, no matter how tired he was. He was grit clear through. He took pains to learn everything. 

Nothing was beneath his notice." 

Again and again Teddy went back to his friends in Maine. The rough outdoor life seemed to help his health. When he finished college, his body was fairly strong. He was still doing everything he could to build it up.

Many years later Theodore Roosevelt became famous for his great strength. No matter how much he did, he never seemed to be tired. "I made my health what it is," he said one day. "I was determined to be strong and well. I did everything I could to make myself so.' 99 Another Roosevelt also won a long fight against poor health. A part of his body had been made weak by a disease called infantile paralysis. He was one of Teddy's cousins. He, too, became a famous man. 

In 1932, Franklin D. Roosevelt was elected President of the United States. Four years later he was again chosen as President.

Teddy finished his studies at Harvard in 1880. He was then twenty-one years old. His family had enough money so that he did not have to work. But

THEODORE ROOSEVELT 

215 this did not suit the restless Teddy. He became interested in politics. Then he decided to join the Republican political club in his part of New York City. 

Some of his rich friends were surprised. "You can't do that," they told him. "These political clubs aren't run by gentlemen. They're run by the saloonkeepers and the men who drive the horse-cars." "Well," answered Teddy, "if that is true, they are the men who are governing this city. You are being ruled by them. I think they are the men I want to know. "If they are too tough for me, I suppose I'll have to quit. But I'm not going to quit until I have tried. 

I want to see if I am really too weak to hold my own."

So Teddy joined the club. He went to the meetings in a large room over a saloon. At first the members did not know what to make of him. They were not used to having rich men from good families join their club. Some of them thought he looked like a "dude." But Teddy's courage and big grin made them his friends.

Before long Teddy had a chance to do a little governing himself. In 1881 he was elected to his first government office. He was made a member of the 

New York state legislature. It was only a few months until something happened that made him known all over the state.

A judge of a court in New York was known to be dishonest. Yet no one did anything about it until

OUR AMERICA 

Teddy Roosevelt asked the legislature to look into the matter. Teddy believed that every man in a government office should be honest.

The judge had rich and powerful friends. They defeated Teddy's plan two times. Each time Teddy led a new attack. "I shall keep on trying until I win," he said. At last the legislature voted in favor of his plan. 

Teddy's fight against the dishonest judge made both friends and enemies for him. At the next election all of the judge's rich friends worked against Teddy. The people who wanted an honest government worked for Teddy. He was again chosen to be a member of the legislature. 

A few months later his old enemy, the asthma, attacked him again. He decided to go west. Perhaps the dry air and the outdoor life would help him. Then, too, he wanted to hunt buffalo. He wanted to see what the western country was like. And so Theodore 

Roosevelt went to the "Bad Lands" of North Dakota.

He liked the country so well that he bought a ranch. A little later he bought another one. It was named the Elkhorn Ranch. Then he got Bill Sewall to come west and help him run the ranch. During the next two years Teddy spent most of his time there. 

At first the ranchers and cowboys looked upon him as a tenderfoot. But Teddy asked no favors. He rode the range with the best of them. He helped to care for the cattle and look for stray horses. He

THEODORE ROOSEVELT 

217 worked side by side with the other ranchers from early morning until late at night.
