He was not always as good at the work as they were, but he was never afraid to try. Before long he had won their respect. He showed them that he was willing to face danger. He proved that he had the courage to fight for what he believed was right. 

Once Teddy went into a little hotel in a nearby town. A man was walking up and down the room with a gun in each hand. He was talking and swearing loudly. When he saw Teddy's glasses he yelled, "Four-eyes!" Then he pointed his guns and ordered

Teddy to treat the crowd. 

Teddy knew that he had to act quickly. He pretended to obey the bully and started forward. His fist flashed up to the bully's jaw. Two more quick blows and the bully was lying on the floor. Before he fell, the bully fired his guns but no one was hit. 

The next day he left town.

At another time Teddy heard that a man named Maunders was going to kill him. He got on his horse and rode over to the man's house. He knocked at the door, and Maunders opened it. "Maunders," said 

Teddy, "I hear that you want to shoot me. I came over to find out why."

Maunders was completely taken back. He decided he didn't want to shoot Teddy after all. That was the end of any trouble with Maunders. 

Teddy had many other adventures in the west.

OUR AMERICA 

Once he helped to catch some thieves. Time after time he told men what he thought about them. All of these things helped to spread Teddy's fame among the ranchers and cowboys. They began to talk about sending him to Congress. But there were other plans ahead for Teddy.

He was asked to come back to New York City. 

Some of his friends wanted him to run for mayor. 

He did, only to be defeated. After the election, he married Edith Carow. He built a house at Oyster

Bay, and settled down to write books. One of his best books is called The Winning of the West. 

Three years later he accepted a job as a Civil Service Commissioner in the United States Government. 

He moved to Washington, D. C. It was his duty to see that the civil service law was obeyed. The purpose of this law was to get better workers for certain government jobs.

At that time it was the custom for the winning side to give the government jobs to its members. If the 

Democrats won, then only those who belonged to that party were given jobs. If the Republicans won the next election, the Democrat workers were fired. Republicans were hired in their place. This was a very bad custom.

The civil service law said that only the best workers should be hired. It did not make any difference to which party they belonged. Before any person was hired he had to pass a test. It was a test of how

THEODORE ROOSEVELT 

219 well he was fitted for the job. In this way, the government could be sure of getting good workers. 

Teddy Roosevelt believed in any law that would make the government better. For six years he worked hard to enforce the civil service law. During this time he made many good friends. He met all kinds of interesting people.

One of his friends told how he liked to hear Theodore Roosevelt talk about men, and books, and politics. "I would curl up in a chair," said the friend, "and listen and wonder until the world seemed to be spinning around, and Teddy was the spinner."

It was during this time, too, that Teddy became known all over the country. The newspapers printed stories about his fight to have the civil service law obeyed. He made many talks in favor of the law. 

Perhaps the people soon forgot what he said, but they did not forget about Teddy Roosevelt.

In 1895, he took a new job as the Police Commissioner of New York City. He was in charge of all the 

New York policemen. On this job, too, he worked for good honest government. He put a stop to the custom of paying to get a job on the police force. He gave better jobs only to those who had earned them by doing good work.

He believed that all persons should be treated fairly. He said that the laws should be obeyed by everyone. No man should be allowed to break a law simply because he was rich and powerful. Of course,

OUR AMERICA 

these ideas made enemies for Teddy Roosevelt. They also made a great number of friends for him. He became better known than ever. 

After two years in New York, Teddy Roosevelt went back to Washington. William McKinley was then President of the United States. He asked Teddy to be the Assistant Secretary of the Navy. Teddy began at once to make the American navy larger and stronger. There was trouble ahead.

In the spring of 1898, a war started between Spain and the United States. Teddy Roosevelt gave up his job in the government. "There is nothing more for me to do here," he explained. "I must get into the fight myself. I have no right to ask others to do the fighting while I stay at home.”

Teddy and his friend, Leonard Wood, got together a group of men known as the Rough Riders. They met in San Antonio, Texas. Here they were given some army training. The Rough Riders were mostly men from the west.

There were cowboys from Arizona and New Mexico. There were hunters, and miners, and gunmen, and sheriffs. About one hundred of the Rough 

Riders were from the east. Among them were writers, actors, and college athletes. A few were quite rich. It was one of the strangest groups ever to get together. But all of them were brave men, good riders and good shots.

These were the men with whom Teddy Roosevelt

يالادى

Teddy Roosevelt leading his Rough Riders

OUR AMERICA 

went to Cuba. One part of the war was being fought on the island of Cuba. The people of Cuba did not want to belong to Spain any longer. Leonard Wood was in charge of the Rough Riders. He was a Colonel. 

Teddy was a Lieutenant-Colonel. He was second in charge.

The Rough Riders took part in one very famous fight. It was called the battle of San Juan. The 

Spanish soldiers were upon a hill. The Americans were down below. There was only one thing to do. 

The Spaniards had to be driven from the hill. 

Up the hill charged Teddy, waving his hat as he went. Behind him came the Rough Riders and some other soldiers. They were yelling, cheering, and even singing as they rushed forward. The Spanish bullets were flying all about them. But on they went, up the hill. And ahead of them was Teddy, shouting to them to come on.

The war with Spain was a short one. Something else in Cuba was more deadly to the American soldiers than the war. The terrible yellow fever struck men down everywhere. It was not until two years later that Walter Reed discovered the cause of the yellow fever. By that time most of the soldiers were back in America. 

Teddy Roosevelt and his Rough Riders landed in 

New York in August, 1898. His brave work in Cuba had made him a great hero. A few weeks later he was asked to run for Governor of New York. The

THEODORE ROOSEVELT 

223 election was close, but Teddy won.

On the second day of January, 1899, Teddy Roosevelt became Governor of the state of New York.

The next two years were another fight for good government. Again Teddy was successful. Then came the election of 1900. Thousands of his friends begged him to run for Vice-President of the United States. For a while he refused, but at last he agreed. 

Teddy Roosevelt fighting for good government

OUR AMERICA 

When the election was over, William McKinley was President and Theodore Roosevelt was Vice- 

President of the United States. Six months later 

President McKinley was shot. Teddy hurried to the bedside of his chief. The doctors said he would get well. There was no need to worry. 

So Theodore Roosevelt went to join his family in the Adirondack Mountains. Two of his children had been very ill. One day he went for a walk in the mountains. When he returned there was news that 

President McKinley was worse. About midnight there was another message. "Come at once," it said.

He climbed into a wagon and began a wild ride down dangerous mountain roads. The nearest railroad station was about thirty miles away. Once he helped the driver to change horses. When he reached the station, another message was waiting for him. President McKinley had died during the night. Theodore 

Roosevelt was now the President of the United States.

It was not long before he won the hearts of all America. He put his trust in the people. They put their trust in him. He wanted to see every person get a "square deal." That was one reason why the people of the north and south, and the east and west loved him so much. "The door of the White House," he said one day, "shall swing open as easily for the poor as for the rich, and not one bit easier."

THEODORE ROOSEVELT 

225 One time he made a trip to a town in Maine.

"Where is Bill Sewall?" he asked. When Bill Sewall came, Teddy asked him to ride in the parade with him. Down the street they went, talking about old times in Maine and North Dakota.

At another time an old woman came to a house where Teddy was visiting. She was the woman who had washed Teddy's clothes when he was in college. "It was very kind of you," he told her, "to come over and see me." 

Teddy never forgot his old friends. The Rough 

Riders, the Dakota ranchers, the New York policemen could always be sure he was glad to see them.

Just as often as not, he would remember to ask about their wives and babies.

He asked many people for advice. But there was one thing he would not do. "I will not let anyone tell me what I must do," he said. That, too, was another reason why he was so well-liked. At the end of his first term, the people again chose him to be their 

President. 

Many important things happened while Theodore Roosevelt was President. He put an end to the wasteful cutting of trees in the forests. He settled a great strike of coal miners.

He made some of the big steel, oil, and meat companies obey the law. When this happened, they said he was trying to destroy the Constitution. But the people were not fooled. They knew Teddy was doing what was best for the country.

Brown Bros.

President Theodore Roosevelt at his desk in the White House He got the right to build a canal through the country of Panama. Today hundreds of ships pass from one ocean to another by way of the Panama Canal. He helped to bring a war between Japan and 

Russia to an end. But always he worked to make his own country and his own government better. 

Theodore Roosevelt finished his second term as

He went on a hunting trip to Africa. He traveled in 

Europe. Everywhere he was greeted by great crowds. 

He came back to America. From time to time he went hunting in the western country. He took part in important elections. He went on an exploring trip into the jungles of South America. Nobody knew where he found the time and strength to do so many things. 

Soon after he came back from South America, the great World War began. The year was 1914. Then,

in 1917, the United States took part. Teddy Roosevelt wanted to lead some soldiers. He was then almost sixty years of age, and that was too old for the army. 

So Teddy Roosevelt stayed at home, but he did not stop fighting. He kept on fighting for honesty and a square deal to the very end. In January, 1919, he died in his sleep. "Only those who are fit to live do not fear to die," he said one time. "Both life and death are parts of the same great adventure." And so it was with Theodore Roosevelt, the great American.

XVIII 

HORACE MANN 

"He was the father of the public schools."

Soon after the first settlers came to America a few schools were started. In most of the colonies, however, only a small part of the children received a good education.

Thomas Jefferson was one of the first men to believe that all boys and girls should go to school. 

He knew that well-educated people were needed to make the United States a great country. 

Eleven years after Thomas Jefferson died, a man in Massachusetts began to work for better schools. Theodore Roosevelt had not yet been born when Horace Mann finished this work. "I don't like to miss a single day," said eight-yearold Betty to her mother's visitor. "We have such good times in our room." "And we learn a lot, too," said Billy, who was two years older and in the fourth grade.

But schools were not always such pleasant and happy places. The boys and girls of Horace Mann's day had a hard time in school. They were whipped far too often and much too hard. Horace Mann believed 229

OUR AMERICA 

they should be taught more and punished less. That was one of the reasons why he began working to make the schools of his state better.

Horace Mann was born on a farm near the little village of Franklin, Massachusetts. He was born on 

May 4, 1796, almost twenty years after Thomas

Jefferson wrote the Declaration of Independence. 

Horace Mann's family was poor, and his father was in bad health. So Horace began to help his parents as soon as he could work about the farm. When he was thirteen years old his father died. Then Horace had to work harder than ever. "My childhood was not a happy one," he said many years later. "I do not remember the time when I began to work. In the winter time, I worked indoors. 

This kept me in too much. In the summer, the work on the farm was too hard. Sometimes it lasted so long that I did not get enough sleep."

During the winter he helped his mother make straw braids. These were later used in making straw hats. Most of this work was done by women and girls. It was not considered a proper kind of work for men or boys. The people of Franklin made fun of 

Horace for braiding straw. They looked upon him as a "sissy." But Horace knew how much his mother needed the money she earned in this way. So he continued to help her until he was almost a grown man. 

The school to which Horace went was one of the smallest and poorest in the state. It was open only

HORACE MANN 

231 

Culver Service 

A log schoolhouse used many years ago for a short time in the winter and a few weeks more in the summer. Horace could not go in the summer because he was needed on the farm. Until he was fifteen years old, he never went to school for more than ten weeks in any one year. "My teachers were good people but they were very poor teachers," said Horace Mann one time. The teachers of that time were mostly men who had some other trade. They taught school only because it was a way in which to earn a little extra money. Most of them knew nothing about teaching school, and many were just as ignorant about everything else. 

The master told the class what pages to study in a certain book. After a while the class was called upon to recite. Those who could not tell the master exactly what the book said were usually whipped. Sometimes they were only scolded and sent to their seat or told

OUR AMERICA 

to stand in the corner. Those who learned the lesson were given several more lines or paragraphs to learn. 

That was their reward.

Everything had to be learned out of a book. There was no science or nature study. There was no music or art. And, of course, most of the fine things which boys and girls now enjoy in school were then unknown. The teachers did not even try to get the children to think for themselves. School was really a discouraging place for bright, active youngsters like 

Horace Mann.

One time he started to draw a picture. He wanted to show in a drawing what he could not say with words. He felt that he just had to express himself in some way. But no sooner had he started than the teacher's heavy ruler struck his hand.

Even this kind of teaching did not keep Horace from wanting to learn. He read the few books which his mother had at home. Then he went to the little public library in Franklin. This was one of the first public libraries in America. It had been given to the town by Benjamin Franklin about ten years before 

Horace was born.

The town in which Horace lived was named after Benjamin Franklin. Some of the people in the town told Mr. Franklin about this. They said they would put a steeple on the church if he would give them a bell for it. Benjamin Franklin replied that he would give the town a library instead of a bell. "Sense is

HORACE MANN 

233 better than sound," he said and sent away for some books for the library. 

Books were very precious things to Horace Mann.

He knew how hard they were to get. All of his own books had been bought with money he earned by braiding straw. He was taught to take care of his books as if they were priceless. "I never dog-eared one in my life," he said many years later, "and I never marked up the pages. I would just as soon have stuck a pin through my own flesh as through the pages of a book." 

At another time he said, "If I had the power, I would scatter libraries all over the country." This love of books and learning remained with him all his life. In this way he was much like Thomas Jefferson. 

Even as a boy, Horace Mann would daydream of the time when he would have a good education. Best of all, he liked to picture himself as doing something for the good of the world.

When Horace was about twenty years old, a traveling school teacher came to Franklin. It was said that he could not tell the time of day by the clock, and that he had never learned the multiplication tables. But there was one thing at which he was very good. He knew a great deal about teaching languages such as 

Latin and Greek. 

Horace Mann went to this strange schoolmaster.

The master soon saw that Horace was a splendid student. He wanted Horace to go to college and offered

OUR AMERICA 

to help him get ready. In six months of hard study 

Horace learned enough to pass the college examinations. That fall Horace was a student at Brown 

University in Providence, Rhode Island. 

It was not always easy for Horace to keep up with his studies and to earn money for his expenses at the same time. His health was poor and he was often sick. He really lost his health before he knew how to care for it. Still he forced himself to keep on with his work. Before long he became known as one of the best students in the university. At the end of three years, in 1819, he graduated at the head of his class. 

Horace made many good friends in college. His room became a gathering place for those who wanted to have a good time. The story is told that one evening there was an especially merry time in Horace's room. 

Then all of a sudden the laughter stopped. The president of the college entered the room. He stood in the door so that none of the boys could get out. 

Before the president could say a word, Horace blew out the candle. He dashed toward the door and crawled out between the president's legs. The president was barely able to keep from falling. He could not catch Horace or any of his friends. Many years later, Horace Mann married the president's daughter.

At another time the students wanted to celebrate the Fourth of July. They asked Horace to make the speech of the day. Horace was willing, but the college

HORACE MANN 

235 officers would not permit the celebration. The students went ahead with their plans, and Horace made. his speech. Horace was lightly punished for his part in the celebration, but he did not mind since everyone had a good time.

The first summer after graduating from college Horace began to study law. In the fall he went back to Brown University as a teacher of Latin and Greek. 

Two years later he gave up teaching and entered a law school. He had decided to become a lawyer. 

The next fifteen years were busy ones for Horace Mann. He soon became a well-known lawyer. He won four out of five of all his cases. One reason for his success was that he would never take a case unless he believed he was on the right side. The people of his town liked him so well that they elected him to the 

Massachusetts state legislature.

For ten years he served in the legislature. During this time he never forgot his wish to make things better. His very first speech defended the people's right to worship God as they pleased. Still later he made a speech in favor of building railroads. He helped pass a law to start a hospital for people who were insane. 

He worked to get a school for the blind started. 

Horace Mann could always be counted on to favor anything that helped the people or the state. 

But happiness and success were soon followed by sorrow. His wife died less than two years after their marriage. This was a blow which he could never for-

OUR AMERICA 

get. A little later his wife's father and his own mother died. Then his brother proved to be dishonest about some money he had borrowed. For three years 

Horace Mann worked to pay his brother's debts. He even slept in his office to save room rent. At last they were paid, and for a time his troubles were over. 

In 1836 he began the work which made him famous all over the world. He became the first secretary of the Massachusetts State Board of Education. Many 

Brown Bros.

Horace Mann

HORACE MANN 

237 of his friends thought he was doing a foolish thing. "The salary is small," they said. "If you take this job, you can no longer be president of the state senate. 

You will even have to give up your law practice.

There is no honor to this job. Why, even its name makes you appear as a servant. Your title should at least be that of president of the board." "It is up to me to bring honor to the job," he answered. "My law books are for sale. My office is for rent. Men are cast iron, but children are wax. My interests are with the boys and girls of today. They are tomorrow's men and women. 

His duties were to get facts about the conditions in the public schools of the state. He had to send out information about the best ways of teaching. He had to attend teachers' meetings in different parts of the state. Then, too, he was supposed to make a report each year to the Board of Education. He had no power to make the schools or teachers do anything. 

He knew that his first task was to get the people interested in their schools. Then, perhaps, he could persuade them to do something to make the schools better. But at that time no one seemed to care about the common schools. Those who had enough money sent their boys and girls to private schools. 

So Horace Mann began to travel about the state. 

He visited schools and held meetings in the towns and cities. At these meetings he made speeches about education. He tried to make the talks as interesting

OUR AMERICA 

as he could so that people would come. Sometimes the crowds were large, but often only a few people came. 

After a visit to one town he wrote, "To get people interested in the schools is like trying to break down a mountain with one's fist. My health is failing, but 

I will not quit." At another time he wrote, "People tell me they are afraid I will become discouraged.

They do not know the stuff of which I am made." 

In many places the people did not know how poor their own schools were. One day he visited a school with large holes in the roof. He asked the teacher if the children were not sometimes drowned out. "We would be," replied the teacher, "except for one thing.

The floor leaks as badly as the roof. The water runs out almost as fast as it comes in."

All of these things made Horace Mann more determined than ever. Slowly but surely he began to see the results of his work. During one year more new school houses were built than in ten years before. 

Other buildings were fixed up so that children would be warm and dry. New seats and desks were put into many schools.

The towns began to raise more money for schools. 

They began to hire better teachers and pay them larger salaries. At that time many teachers were paid less than the common laborers. In some parts of the United States this is still true. Many states do not yet pay their teachers a salary upon which they can live decently.

HORACE MANN 

239 Another important result of Horace Mann's work was the first state normal school. This was a school in which teachers could be trained. When the school opened in 1839 it had only three students. In a few years two more normal schools were started. Before long other states began to set up schools in which their teachers could be trained. 

These normal schools soon began to supply better teachers to the common schools. Both men and women teachers were trained. More women became teachers than ever before. It was not hard for parents to see the difference between a trained teacher and an untrained one. Parents soon began to send their children to the public schools instead of private schools. 

Horace Mann worked as Secretary of the Massachusetts Board of Education for twelve years. Each year he wrote a report for the board. One of them told about the best ways to teach reading. Another showed how important education was for the people of any country. Still another described what he saw in the schools of Europe.

These reports became famous in many parts of the world. The legislature of New York had more than eighteen thousand copies of his fifth report printed. The Government of England printed a large part of his seventh report. The German Government also printed several of his reports. Copies of his yearly reports went to every part of the United

OUR AMERICA 

Brown Bros. 

Study and work are enjoyed in schools today. 

States. They are still looked upon as the best school reports ever written.

Horace Mann has often been called the father of 

America's common schools. Today there are excellent public schools in all parts of the United States. 

There are well-trained teachers who understand boys and girls. There are fine buildings and playgrounds. 

It is wonderful, too, that the public schools are free to the children of all the people. All of this grew out of the movement which Horace Mann started. Is it any wonder that so many schools are named after him? 

When Horace Mann left his job as Secretary of the Board of Education, he became a member of the

HORACE MANN 

241 

Brown Bros. 

A happy lunchroom

United States Congress. Some years later he went to 

Ohio to be president of Antioch College. "The common schools," he wrote at one time, "were my first love. They will be my last."

At another time Horace Mann said, "the common school is the greatest discovery ever made by man." 

He had a faith in them that nothing could destroy. 

The people of America now have almost as great a faith in their schools. Beginning early in 1937, celebrations were held all over the country. They were held in honor of Horace Mann, over one hundred years after he started his work for the common schools. 

A few months before his death in 1850, Horace Mann made a speech. It was a talk to the students of his last graduating class. At the end of his talk he gave them a message. It was really the story of his own life. "Be ashamed to die," he said, "until you have won some victory for man."

XIX 

"He has taught boys to love the outdoors." 

An education comes from what is learned out of school as well as from what is learned in school. 

Daniel Boone and Theodore Roosevelt learned many good lessons and habits of living from their outdoor life.

Another person who learned many of these lessons was born in the same year that Horace Mann died. A long time later this man helped to build a great organization for boys. 

It has taught thousands of American boys good habits of living and a love for the outdoors. This organization is the Boy Scouts of America. The man is Daniel Carter Beard. 

For fifty years one man has been a hero to millions of American boys. He is often called the father of the 

Boy Scouts of America. His name is Daniel Carter 

Beard. He is better known simply as Dan Beard, or even as Uncle Dan.

Many years ago Dan Beard lived in the Daniel Boone country. He lived in Covington, Kentucky, near the Ohio River. It was only a few miles from 242

31 

Courtesy, Boy Scouts of America

Daniel Carter Beard 

Cincinnati, Ohio, where Dan Beard had been born in

June, 1850. The open fields and the dark forests were not far away.

Dan Beard's family had been early settlers in the country of Daniel Boone. His father was an artist.

He was also the best shot in the whole neighborhood. 

The house was filled with interesting things which the early hunters and settlers had once used. His

OUR AMERICA 

father's old hunting coat hung behind the door. Dan liked to look at this coat and especially at the buttons. 

Each button had a different hunting picture on it. 

He heard stories of the early days from his mother and father. He read all of the books in the house from cover to cover. He liked best the stories of explorers and scouts and other great Americans. The story of 

Daniel Boone was one of his favorites. 

So Dan Beard grew to love the great outdoor country. He spent much of his time in the nearby fields and forests. He wanted to know as much about the outdoors as Daniel Boone had known.

Dan and some other boys learned how to camp in the woods. They dug caves in the river bank. They practiced shooting with the bow and arrow. They tried in every way to become like Daniel Boone. Dan 

Beard was the leader. 

They even saved a boy from drowning. One day some boys were playing on the ice on the river. The ice broke and one of the boys fell into the water. Dan 

Beard and his friends knew just what to do. They stretched out on the ice and formed a human chain. 

In a few minutes they had the boy safely on shore. 

Dan liked to build things for himself. Once he cut down a large tree. He started to trim it off and hollow it out. He wanted to make it into a boat. Finally he succeeded. At another time he built a house boat. 

What good times the boys had with this boat!

Once he built a little railroad in his back yard. He

built a house in the tops of some trees. He made models of ships, and kites without tails. He made all kinds of wonderful things. He showed many of the boys how to make things for themselves. 

He fixed up a little zoo in the back yard. In one part of the zoo he built a fish pond. Then he went out to the woods and river. He caught fish and birds and animals for his zoo. He had rabbits, raccoons, squirrels, and foxes. Then there were catbirds, robins, wild ducks, and pigeons. He also had some fish, frogs, turtles, and snakes. In this way he learned a great deal about wild animals. 

When Dan was eleven years old the Civil War started. This was a war between the people in the 

South and the people in the North. Soldiers from both armies fought many battles near Dan's home. For a while the soldiers were on each side of the river near the place where Dan Beard and his friends had a camp. 

The two armies would shoot across the river at each other. Dan Beard's little camp was in a very dangerous place, but he did not mind so very much. 

Almost every week he and some of his friends would crawl past the soldiers. They kept close to the ground so they would not be seen. Bullets whistled through the tall grass and leaves above them. But each time they reached the camp safely. 

One day Dan Beard and his friends bought some bright red trousers. It took all of their savings to get

OUR AMERICA 

them. But soldiers had to have a uniform. Dan and the boys had decided to become soldiers. They put on their red trousers and started out in Dan's boat. They went to see General U. S. Grant of the Northern 

Army. They wanted to join his army. 

The General was very polite. No, he didn't need any more soldiers. He asked the boys about their red trousers and their home-made boat. He thanked them and sent them home. He told them not to try to join the army again. The boys did not seem to be too badly disappointed. After all, they could still wear the red trousers.

When Dan was fourteen years old, he got a job in an army hospital. His job was to wait on the wounded soldiers. At that time many people believed that fruit was not good for a wounded man.

Dan picked some pears and gave them to the soldiers. The men liked the pears very much. Some of the cores fell on the floor. When the doctor saw the cores and found out who had brought the pears, he sent Dan from the hospital. 

Many years later Dan Beard met a blacksmith.

The blacksmith came to do some work on Dan's cabin. 

In the evening Dan told the story of the pears. The blacksmith laughed. "I was one of those soldiers," he said. "I don't remember anything that ever tasted so good as those pears."

Of course, Dan did not spend all of his time outdoors. He went to school and became a very good

student. His father taught him how to draw and paint. Dan liked this almost as well as he did his outdoor life. When he was only eight years old he helped the teacher in his school with her drawing class. 

There were many artists in the Beard family. 

Dan's uncle was a famous painter of animals. His three brothers and his two sisters also were painters. 

His father made paintings of many of the greatest men of that time. Some of them came to his father's home to have their pictures made. This gave Dan 

Beard a chance to meet and talk with many great persons.

After the war, he went to a school where he studied to be an engineer. Several years later he graduated. 

He found a job which paid him four dollars a week. 

He took his first week's pay and spent it all for neckties. "I never had but one home-made necktie at a time," he said later. "I wanted a whole lot of new ties, and I didn't want them to be home-made." 

One of his first jobs was to pound sticks into the ground. He did not think this was very proper work for one who was a trained engineer. He was ashamed of his job. Then, too, it was hard work. But he kept at it. "It was the best training of my life," he wrote one time. "It made my muscles strong. It taught me how to take care of myself. It also taught me to respect men for what they could do."

When Dan was twenty-two years old he found

Dan Beard and the other boys wanted to join the army.

work with a map company. He had to travel about the country and make maps of small towns and villages. This work gave him many chances to be outdoors. He began to draw and paint pictures of the birds and animals which he saw. 

One day Dan Beard was visiting his brother in

New York. He had brought along some of his animal pictures. An editor for a magazine happened to see one of his pictures of a fish. The editor paid Dan 

Beard fifty dollars for it and printed it in the magazine. 

Dan Beard was both pleased and surprised. Drawing pictures was nothing but fun for him. "If I can earn a living by having fun," he said, "I am going to take a vacation." He left his job with the map company. He started to work as an artist. 

He now began to work and travel a part of each year. He would paint and draw until he had some money saved. Then he would quit work and start for the mountains and forests. Each trip made him love the outdoors just that much more. 

Dan Beard traveled in many parts of America. He climbed mountains and hunted wolves in Canada. 

He explored a dangerous part of the Grand Canyon in Arizona. He paddled a canoe down swift streams in the Rocky Mountains. He tracked wild game in the great western forests. He loved all parts of the great outdoor country.

He liked to watch the wild animals and learn their

OUR AMERICA 

habits. Once he said, "I caught many small animals alive. I kept them long enough to study them and draw pictures of them. Then I set them free, even the snakes. I always try to learn the truth about animals, and I always let them live."

On each of these trips he tried new ways of traveling and camping. He learned the best way of making a canoe out of birch bark. He learned how to keep fish and meat fresh for many days. He discovered how to make good shelters of brush, how to cross a stream safely on a log, and how to make a clay oven. 

He learned hundreds of wonderful things about living outdoors. 

One day a friend in New York said to him, “Dan, why don't you write a book and tell how to do all of those things? Then boys all over the country can learn how to do them, too."

Dan Beard began to write the book. It was called 

The American Boy's Handy Book. It was first printed more than fifty years ago, but it is still popular. 

Later, he wrote another book for boys called The Outdoor Handy Book. This, too, was very popular. Still later he wrote a book for girls. 

These books are as interesting as ever. Boys and girls still like to learn how to do things. Dan Beard's books tell them how to play games, how to row or sail boats, how to make a zoo, how to swim, and how to camp. The books also tell how to make kites, tops, canoes, rafts, and dozens of other things.

Courtesy, Boy Scouts of America Cub Scouts learning how to box 

Dan Beard's books made him a famous man. He began to get hundreds of letters from boys all over the country. When a boy wanted to know something about the outdoors, he wrote to Dan Beard. Many boys came to visit him. He finally started a summer camp for boys. Later the Dan Beard Outdoor School was started. Thousands of boys came to spend a part of the summer with Dan Beard in his camp and school.

In 1905, a magazine editor asked him to think of something that would interest boys. Dan Beard remembered his boyhood friends. He decided to start an organization, or club, for boys who wanted to

OUR AMERICA 

learn about the outdoors.

He called it the "Sons of 

Daniel Boone." 

Five years later, in 1910, the "Sons of Daniel Boone" gave way to a new organization. It was called the "Boy Scouts of America." Today there are more than a million members. Boys in every part of the 

United States belong to it. There are also Boy Scout troops in many other countries of the world. 

The Boy Scouts learn about the outdoors. They learn how to make camps and build fires. They learn how to use a knife and an axe. They learn how to give first aid to people who are hurt. All of these things, and many more, Boy Scouts learn by really doing them.

There are special medals and honors for those who learn to do new things. Perhaps a boy wants to learn how to row. When he has learned how to row he takes 

Courtesy, Boy Scouts of America 

Getting ready to start on the Rabbit Hop race

a test. If he passes the test he gets a badge. This shows everyone that he has learned to row. 

The motto of the Boy Scouts is "Be Prepared."

This means they must always be ready to do their duty. When a new member joins the Boy Scouts he promises to help other people at all times. He agrees to do his duty to God and his country and to obey the Scout law. He also promises to keep his body strong and his mind awake.

A Boy Scout does a good turn each day. Sometimes it might be to help a blind man across the street. 

It might be to help an old man or woman in some way. 

Perhaps it might even be to save someone's life. Once a Scout saw a boy drowning. He dived into the water and held the boy up until help came. "That's nothing," he said afterward. "A Scout should always help when he can." Hundreds of Boy

Scouts have received a gold medal for saving someone's life.

Scouts are not allowed to take money for the good turns they do. It is all a part of their day's work. 

Once a boy in a large city carried a heavy bag for an old lady. She gave him some money. In a little while he came back. "My brother told me," he said, "that a good scout doesn't take money for helping people. Here's the money you gave me. I'm sorry I took it." 

From the very first Dan Beard was one of the leaders in the Boy Scouts of America. He is still one of

OUR AMERICA 

the chief officers. He has watched it grow from a little handful of boys to a great organization. He has taught thousands of boys to love the outdoors. 

A number of men were eating together in a hotel one day. One of the men was a famous explorer. Dan 

Beard was there, too. The explorer saw Dan Beard and pointed to him. "There sits the man," he said, "who first taught me to love the outdoors."

Many of those who learned about the outdoors from Dan Beard now have sons of their own. Perhaps most of these boys are already Boy Scouts or will someday become one. The fathers are often as interested as their sons in what the scouts are doing. 

The Boy Scouts have a saying, “Once a Scout, always a Scout."

A national meeting was planned to be held in Washington, D. C., in June and July, 1937, to celebrate the twenty-seventh birthday of the Boy Scout organization. President Franklin D. Roosevelt sent a special invitation to the Boy Scouts. More than twenty-five thousand Boy Scouts said they wanted to attend. 

The plan for this great meeting included a village of tents to be put up by the boys. The tents were to cover more than three hundred acres of ground. The tent village was to have its own telephone system, its own policemen, and even a daily newspaper to be printed by the boys. The camp was to be near the 

White House where the President of the United

Courtesy, Boy Scouts of America 

A happy and healthy Boy Scout troop States lives. Dan Beard was to be chief of the camp. 

Although more than eighty-five years old, Dan 

Beard is as good a scout as ever. Wherever he goes there is a crowd of boys around him. Everyone wants to talk to him and shake his hand. They are all eager to meet the oldest and best scout of them all. 

Dan Beard has always loved boys. Once he wrote, "As a boy I was loyal to boys. As a young man I was loyal to boys. They are still a great joy to me." Dan

Beard is now an old man, but he still gets his greatest happiness from working with boys. And the boys, too, are happy as long as they can have "Uncle Dan."
