Pueblo Indian women. Edgar L. Hewett has always been a true friend of the Indians.

XX 

"He knows how the early people of America lived." 

America is a very old land. The story of the 

Indians who lived in it goes back a long, long time before Leif Ericson and Christopher Columbus.

Most of the first white settlers in America were too busy to care about its early history. Many years went by before a few men became interested in learning something about that history. 

Soon after Jane Addams, Julius Rosenwald, and Theodore Roosevelt began their great work, a young man in New Mexico began to study the Indians of long ago. By the time that Dan Beard helped start the Boy Scouts, Edgar L. Hewett had learned a great deal about the early people of 

America. 

The Norsemen and Christopher Columbus both found people living in America. The Norsemen called them Skraelings. Columbus called them Indians. 

Where did these people come from? How long had they lived in America? Were all of them alike? What kind of lives did they live?

No one will ever know the answer to all of these 257

OUR AMERICA 

questions. America is much too old for that. Its secrets are too well hidden. The story of how the early people lived and fought and died can be told only in part. That story could not be told at all if it were not for the work of a few men. 

One of these men is Edgar L. Hewett. For more than forty years he has been finding out about the early people of America. He has climbed steep mountain sides to visit dark caves. He has walked and ridden across miles of lonely desert to study queer paint- 

Courtesy, Edgar L. Hewett 

Edgar L. Hewett

ings on the side of a rock wall. He has dug into old buried cities and uncovered strange secrets.

Perhaps better than any one else, Edgar L. Hewett knows what one part of America was like long before the white man came. He has learned how the early people, who were all Indians of some kind, built their homes and made a living. He has found out how they fought their enemies and cared for their loved ones. 

He has even learned something about what they believed and thought.

Many years before Edgar L. Hewett began digging strange secrets out of the earth, he was planting things that would grow. He was born on a farm in 

Illinois on November 23, 1865. At that time Julius 

Rosenwald was three years old and Jane Addams was five. They lived in another part of the same state. 

The farm on which Edgar Hewett lived was much like all the other Illinois farms. There was always plenty for a strong, active boy to do. The farm animals had to be fed. The crops had to be planted and harvested. And during the winter there was school for two or three months. 

When he was fourteen years old, he started to go to high school in a nearby town. The school was almost five miles from his home. At first he rode back and forth on a horse each day. For about two years he rode through all kinds of weather. "I learned to take it rough," he said many years later. "I am

OUR AMERICA 

glad I had a chance to live and work on a farm." 

Then he got a job and moved into town. He earned his room and board by doing odd jobs for a doctor and working in his drug store. There was not much time left for anything besides his lessons. But he did not complain because he wanted to get a good education. He graduated from high school when he was eighteen years old.

Later in that same year he began to teach in a little country school in Missouri. After one year, he moved to another school in a small town. Again he taught school for a year, and once more he moved. 

This time he went to Tarkio College in Tarkio, Missouri. Here he spent part of his time studying and part in teaching some classes in history. 

As a young man, Edgar Hewett read all of the books he could get. Best of all, he liked books about history and travel. "I was always interested," he said, "in what men did hundreds and thousands of years ago. I wanted to see those parts of the world in which they had lived and worked. One time I read a book which told about the jungle country in Central America. It gave me an interest in the people of that country which I have never lost."

Before he finished college he became a superintendent of schools. First he worked in Missouri, and then he moved to Colorado. He soon began to hear many stories about the land and the people to the south. The land to the south was known as New

Mexico. It belonged to the United States, but it had not yet become a state.

New Mexico was a very old land. The first white men to explore it were Spaniards. They had come up from the Spanish settlements in Mexico. One of the early explorers was named Coronado. He came to 

New Mexico in 1540. He had heard wonderful tales of seven cities in which almost everything was supposed to be made of gold.

Instead of the seven cities of gold, he found many poor Indian villages. These Indians were called the 

Pueblo Indians. Pueblo is a Spanish word meaning town or village. The Pueblo Indians lived in large houses, sometimes five or six stories high. They made their living by farming. Many of their villages were very old. No one knew how long they had lived there. 

Even though Coronado found no rich cities, many other Spaniards followed him to New Mexico. Some of them came to live in the strange land. The first settlement was started in 1598 at San Juan by Juan de Oñate. This was more than twenty years before the Pilgrims came to Plymouth. The city of Santa

Fe, in which Edgar L. Hewett now lives, was started sometime between 1605 and 1610.

In parts of New Mexico there were many old villages in which the Indians no longer lived. Some of these old villages were up on the flat hill tops. Others were built in the side of a cliff. Many of them were so old that the walls had fallen. Only a pile of stones

OUR AMERICA 

marked the place where hundreds of people had once lived. No one seemed to know anything about them. 

It was all very mysterious. 

The stories of the strange old land of New Mexico were all very interesting to Edgar Hewett. He thought more and more about the dead towns in the cliffs and deserts. What had happened to the people who once lived in them? Had they moved away? Had they been killed by enemies or disease? Was there anything in the old towns which would tell their story? 

Edgar Hewett began to make trips to New Mexico. 

He spent his summer vacations among the cliff ruins northwest of Santa Fe. He came not only to see them but also to study them. He wanted to find out as much as he could about the people who had once lived there. 

Before long he was invited to make some speeches in Las Vegas and Santa Fe, New Mexico. The people in those towns wanted him to talk about the Indian ruins. They had discovered that this young man from 

Colorado knew more about their own country than they did. He went to the New Mexico towns and made his talks. They must have been very good talks, because he was invited to come again and again. 

One day he was asked to become the first president of a new school. It was called the Normal University. So, in 1898, Edgar L. Hewett went to Las 

Vegas, New Mexico, to live. It was the same year

that the United States went to war with Spain to help the people of Cuba. For five years he served as president of the Normal University.

During this time he kept on with his study of the early people. On many trips his only companion was 

Spider, his horse. Together they pushed their way up rough mountain canyons and sandy river valleys. 

Somewhere ahead was an Indian ruin that should be studied. And when the day's work was done, they slept under the clear, starlit, New Mexico sky. "I got Spider when he was a colt," said Mr. Hewett. 

He was a rich bay color. I called him Spider because he had such long legs. He soon became a wise, surefooted horse. He could climb almost anywhere that 

I could. He went with me through thick and thin, and he never seemed to get tired. He was one of the most faithful creatures I ever knew. A horse like that is a wonderful friend to have."

Soon after Edgar L. Hewett came to Las Vegas, he began to get others interested in studying the Indian ruins. People in all parts of New Mexico became interested. They began to see how wonderful was the story of the early people who had lived all about them. This interest grew and grew. Today it is stronger than ever.

He made plans for the Normal University to study a group of cliff-dwellings. Today they are known as the Bandelier National Monument. They are visited by thousands of people each year. He helped start a

OUR AMERICA 

society to study the Indian ruins. At first it had only a few members, but now there is a branch in almost every part of the state.

What was still more important, he made people see how necessary it was to study the ruins in a scientific way. It was not right to dig just any way. People should not be allowed to help themselves to everything they could find. The things which the Indians had left behind were too precious for that. Later he helped to write some laws to protect the ruins. These laws were then passed by the United States Government.

In 1903, Edgar Hewett decided to leave New Mexico. He wanted to go to Europe and study at a famous university in Switzerland. This was one part of the plan he had worked out for his own life. “I knew what I wanted to do," he said years later, “and I have always worked to make that plan come true.” 

Edgar Hewett wanted to study a science called archæology. "Archæology," he said one time, “is the science of things that live. It deals with the things that nothing has been able to destroy. It takes from the earth that which early man left behind him. It is from the tools and weapons and other things he left behind that we know how he lived." 

Before he left he sent Spider to a ranch. He gave orders that Spider should be given the best of care as long as he lived. It was a kind way to treat the faith-

ful horse which had gone with him on so many happy trips. 

The next five years were busy ones for Edgar Hewett. He finished his work at the university in 

Switzerland. Then he began to study how the early people in other countries had lived. This, too, was a part of his plan. He went to Egypt, Palestine, Turkey, Syria, Greece, and Italy. In all of these places he learned from the earth the long-hidden secrets of early man.

He spent one entire year in Mexico. He explored the country on horseback from the north to the south. 

He wanted to see how the wonderful Indian civilizations of Mexico had grown. He made careful records of what he saw and then wrote a report.

Out of this report grew the School of American Research. It was started to study how early man had lived in America. From its very beginning in 1907, he has been the director of the school's work. It is located in Santa Fe, New Mexico. Here Edgar

Hewett has his office in the old Palace of Governors. 

He also went down into Central America. The jungle was so thick that a man had to cut a path in order to walk through it. It was also the old home of the Maya Indians. Later Edgar Hewett and some of his students uncovered one of their wonderful old cities. He dug into hillsides and laid bare some grand staircases. He found their great pyramids, their strange statues, and their old sun temples. They

OUR AMERICA 

were covered with all kinds of beautiful carvings. 

The old Maya pyramids are now among the wonders of the world.

It was during these same busy five years that he did another great piece of work. The United States 

Government asked him to study the cliff-dwellings at Mesa Verde, Colorado. Mesa Verde is a Spanish name meaning green table-land. After he finished his study, the government made a national park out of the cliff-dwellings. It is now called Mesa Verde Na tional Park. 

The cliff-dwellings at Mesa Verde are the most wonderful ever found in America. More than three thousand different ruins have been found. Some of them are small houses in which only a single family lived. Others are towns in which four or five hundred people lived. Some of these cliff towns are three, four, and even five stories high.

One of the largest of these towns is called the Cliff 

Palace. Another is known as the Spruce Tree House.

They were built in large caves in the side of a steep cliff. The walls of the cliff towns were built of stones which had been cut to the right size and shape. The ceilings and floors were made by putting heavy poles across the walls and covering them with sticks and earth.

The Indians who lived in the cliff-houses were peaceful people. They did not like to fight as did their enemies, the Indians who lived on the plains.

Spruce Tree House, Mesa Verde 

That was one reason why the cliff-houses were built. 

In most places, the only way to get to them was by climbing up a steep, narrow trail. When these trails were blocked, the cliff-dwellers were safe. They could stay in their homes as long as the food and water lasted.

After a great many years, the cliff-dwellers began to move away. They did not all go at one time. First, one little family group would move. Then another would leave and go to some other place. Soon only a few people were left, and then finally none at all. 

Many of them may have been killed by their enemies.

OUR AMERICA 

No one is entirely sure of all the reasons why the cliff-dwellers moved away. But the best reason is that the climate changed. A time came when each year there was less and less rain. The crops which they raised in nearby fields began to fail. Before long they had to find a place where they could make a better and easier living. 

It is now believed that many of the cliff-dwellers moved south. Those who stayed in New Mexico are said to be the ancestors of the Pueblo Indians. It is hard to tell how old the cliff-dwellings are. Perhaps soon after Leif Ericson came to America they were filled with happy people. Some of them may even be older. 

One thing is certain. When the white men first began to explore America, the cliff-dwellers had moved. They had gone on to a better land. Only their houses remained, empty, lonely, and mysterious. 

In 1915, a great fair was held in San Diego, California. Edgar Hewett was asked to take charge of the part which showed what man had done through the different ages. One building was called the 

Science of Man Building. It told the story of early man step by step. 

One day President Theodore Roosevelt came to the fair. He could stay only a few hours. The part he chose to see was the Science of Man Building. "Get

Hewett to take me through," he said in a telegram. 

During the next twelve years, Edgar Hewett spent

much of his time in San Diego. For a while he was director of the San Diego Museum and a professor in the San Diego State College. He also spent a part of each year on trips to places where he could study early man.

He kept on with the work among the pueblos and cliff-dwellings of New Mexico. He made trips into the desert countries of Africa. He took his college classes to Mexico, Central America, and South America. Here they studied the old Indian civilizations. 

At other times he made trips to the desert countries of Asia.

He had many interesting experiences on these trips. Once, he and Mrs. Hewett and some students were driving across the desert in Arabia. Suddenly the car in which they were riding ran off a high bank. 

Both Mr. and Mrs. Hewett were hurt, and the car was wrecked. The Arab guide was killed. 

There was nothing to do except to look for help. 

An Arab camp was found half a day's walk away. To this camp Mr. and Mrs. Hewett and the others were taken. They were given the best care that the Arabs knew how to give them. Many days later a Red Cross car was sent to bring them to a French army post. 

When they were ready to leave, the Arabs went through the village and got all the money they could. 

They raised about twenty dollars, all they had in the camp. This they loaned to Edgar Hewett so that he could get back to civilization. They knew that they

OUR AMERICA 

would never see him again, but they also knew that Edgar Hewett could be trusted to repay the loan. 

As soon as Mr. Hewett got back to civilization, he returned to the Arabs what he had borrowed. It was sent by messenger from one town to another, until at last it reached the right place. Many months later, Edgar Hewett received a letter from the head man of the Arab village thanking him for the return of the money.

But Edgar Hewett does not care to talk about his strange and exciting experiences. "I learned early in life to take the world as I found it," he said one time. "I always felt it was a great waste of time to worry about things." He does not like to talk about himself. Only a few of his friends know the story of his life.

He believes that it is much better to do a thing than to talk about doing it. When something goes wrong on one of his trips, he does not let it discourage him. "I think a person can do what he wants to do," he said, "just by sticking to it, by not knowing when he is licked even if he is licked. "Of course, he must have faith in his own ability to do what he wants. He must also have faith in the world, and in the men with whom he works. A person who has the idea that the world is against him, or that he never gets a fair deal, does not find much of a place for himself. "Along with faith, he must have the courage to go

ahead. I think that being afraid is what causes most people to fail. A person who is afraid that he will fail is beaten before he starts."

In his own work, Edgar Hewett has always had to keep faith with others. He has made friends with the people in every country where he has worked. He has lived their life and learned their ways. He has also learned to speak their languages. But what is still more important, he has come to know their ways of thinking.

Among the Pueblo Indians of New Mexico, there are few white men more trusted than Edgar Hewett. 

Pueblo of Taos: North House

OUR AMERICA 

They have taken him to unknown ruins and helped him dig in others. They have told him stories about the early people which had come down to them through the long years. They have even shared their greatest secrets with him.

In a little pueblo north of Santa Fe all of the Indians but one had died. The last man happened to be the old rain priest. For a while he lived all by himself in the lonely pueblo. He stayed until it was again time for him to pray for rain. Then he went through the old, old rain ceremony just as he had done many times before. Once there had been many in his pueblo to watch him. Now there was no one.

A short time later he came to see Edgar Hewett. 

He brought all of the things he used in the rain ceremony. He told how they were used and said he had used them for the last time. He gave them to Dr. 

Hewett for the museum at Santa Fe. In less than a year the old rain priest died. Another Pueblo had passed from the living to the dead. "In my studies of early man, I have always depended upon the help of the Indians," explained Dr. 

Hewett one time. But he has helped them, too. He has sent doctors to care for the sick, or to help a family with a new baby. He has given horses to some of them who needed horses more than anything else. 

He has helped them in every way he could. 

When he visits a pueblo now, there is often sad news waiting for him. A few years ago he went to

the pueblo of Zia. Only one or two old men came to greet him. "Most of my old friends are passing away," said Dr. Hewett sadly. And his heart was heavy as he left the little village.

In 1927 Edgar Hewett became a professor at the 

University of New Mexico in Albuquerque. He was in charge of all its studies about early man. For twenty-five years he has been the director of the 

Museum of New Mexico in Santa Fe. Some of the finest paintings and Indian art work in the United States are kept in this museum. In addition, he is still in charge of the School of American Research in Santa Fe. 

This is the work which he is now doing. It keeps him very busy, but he still finds time to do many other things. "I have not yet lost a full day because of sickness," he said one time. "I have always had a strong body and good health. My outdoor life has helped me to keep fit."

He is now more than seventy years old. He is still carrying out the plans he made for himself. "Many years ago," he explained, "I planned to study the early civilizations of the desert lands in all parts of the world. I must make two more big trips. One will be to northern India. The other will be to the 

Gobi desert in central Asia. Then I will have finished what I started out to do."

In the last few years he has made many short trips. 

He has also written two very fine books. One of them

OUR AMERICA 

tells about early life in the American southwest. The other tells about early life in Mexico and Central America. There will soon be a third book which will tell about early life in Peru. "One part of my work now," he says, "is to tell the story of early man in America. I have spent most of my life finding out about early man, and getting ready to tell that story. I want to tell it so simply and clearly that everyone can understand it." 

But the whole story of how early man lived in America can not yet be told. There is still much that is unknown. No one knows this better than Edgar L. 

Hewett. "I feel that the study of early man in this country has just started," he said. "We still have a great deal to learn."

Each year he has added a little bit more to what is known about early man. In 1936, he wrote a book about the Chaco Canyon ruins. These ruins are in northwestern New Mexico. They are a part of the 

Gran Chaco National Monument. This monument is almost straight south of Mesa Verde National Park. 

The ruins of twelve large towns and many small houses have been found in this place. They are the most important pueblo ruins which have yet been discovered. One of the largest towns is called Pueblo 

Bonito. This means "beautiful town." Another is known as Chetro Ketl. It was built upon the ruins of a still older town.

Chetro Ketl was about six hundred feet, or two

Courtesy, University of New Mexico Press

Chetro Ketl as it may have appeared when Leif Ericson came to America blocks, long. It was at least five stories high. The people who built it had to cut and carry many million blocks of stone. They had to cut and carry thousands of logs and poles from forests many miles away. The same long hard work also went into the building of all the other towns.

Dr. Hewett thinks the people who lived in the 

Chaco towns spoke the same language. At one time there may have been as many as ten thousand of them. They made their living by farming and hunting. They raised corn and beans and squash. They dug long ditches to bring water to their fields just as farmers in dry lands do today.

OUR AMERICA 

Their tools were made of stone and bone. Most of their clothing was made from woven cloth. The cloth was woven out of cotton. They made baskets out of grass and reeds. They did some painting on stone and wood, but their finest work was the pottery. 

Many of their bowls and mugs had beautiful patterns painted on either the inside or the outside. 

The Chaco towns are older than the cliff-dwellings of Mesa Verde. People were living in them at least one hundred years before Leif Ericson came to America. The people of the Chaco towns moved away about the year 1120. The Mesa Verde cliff-dwellings were then only between fifty and one hundred years old.

No one can tell what happened to the Chaco people. They were not killed by disease or destroyed by war-like Indians. They were not forced to move because of poor crops as were the cliff-dwellers. When 

Dr. Hewett uncovered the town of Chetro Ketl, he found that all of the doors and windows had been sealed up.

This could mean only one thing. When the people of Chetro Ketl moved away, they planned to come back some day. But why they moved, or what happened to them, no one knows. When Dr. Hewett dug it up, the old town was still waiting for a people who never came back.

Edgar Hewett's studies of early man have made him famous. He is known in all parts of the world.

Great success has not changed him. He is still the same kindly man, always ready to do his part in any good cause. A poor Indian who comes to visit him is just as welcome as the important and famous men who come from all parts of the world. 

His life is a good example of how a young man can make use of all that this country offers him. "Some people believe that the world owes them a living," he said one time. "The fact is, they owe everything to the world. I have never stopped being thankful to my country for the opportunities it has given me. It owes me nothing. I owe it everything."

Among his friends and visitors are many young men and women. Some of them are still in school. 

He is always glad to see them, and he is never too busy to help or encourage them. That is one of the reasons why he is so well loved by everyone. "I have great faith in the young people of this country," he says, "as long as they have the spirit of adventure, and the courage to strike out, to go ahead and do things."

XXI 

"He has flown across the top and bottom of the world." 

Daniel Boone and Jedediah Smith each explored a large part of this country. A few years later there was no new land left to be explored in the United States.

Today almost every part of the world has been visited by explorers. One of the last places to be carefully explored was the land at the bottom of the world.

The most important trips to this far-away land were made by a man who used a new way of exploring. While Edgar L. Hewett was learning about old civilizations by digging into the ground, Richard E. Byrd was learning about a new country by flying above the ground. 

Eric the Red would have been the most surprised man in the world if he could only have seen what was happening in Greenland in 1925. Almost a thousand years earlier Eric the Red had explored a tiny corner of Greenland. He had walked over the rough, icy country. That was the old way. 278

Brown Bros. 

Richard E. Byrd

Now Richard E. Byrd was exploring a large part of Greenland in a very short time. He flew over the snow-covered land in an airplane. This was the new way.

There were three boys in the Byrd family, Tom, Dick and Harry. They lived in Virginia. Each of them brought honor to his family. Harry, the eldest brother, became the Governor of Virginia and later a United States Senator. Tom, the youngest brother, became an officer in the United States Army.

OUR AMERICA 

Dick, who was born in 1888, became one of the greatest explorers in America today. After exploring in Greenland, he flew to the top of the world and back. Then he flew across the Atlantic Ocean from

New York to France. Next, he flew across the bottom of the world and a few years later he went back on a second trip. Other men have done some of these things. No one else has ever done all of them. 

Like many boys in Virginia, Dick learned to swim and ride and shoot. He studied hard and played hard. 

He formed good habits which helped him all his life. 

Dick's father and mother wanted their boys to have good minds as well as good bodies.

Dick liked to take his dog and walk in the hills. 

Once he heard about a cave. He decided to find it. 

Early the next morning he started out. He took along a lunch for himself and a bone for his dog. That afternoon a storm came up. Dick found a place to wait until the rain stopped. The lightning struck a tree a few feet away. He had to wait a long time before he could go home. The storm did not frighten him, but he was disappointed because he did not find the cave.

One day, when Dick was twelve years old, a letter came to him. It was from Judge Carson, an old friend of the family. He wanted Dick to come and visit with him. Judge Carson lived in the Philippine Islands.

These islands are out in the Pacific Ocean, not far from Asia.

Dick wanted very much to go, but his father and mother did not think it was safe. Dick did not give up hope. He kept on coaxing until his parents agreed to let him go. He took a train to San Francisco. Then he got on a ship. A great storm struck the ship, but it got through safely. Dick thought this was the best part of the whole voyage. 

He had many wonderful adventures in the Philippine Islands. At last it was time for him to go home. 

He did not return the way he had come. Instead, he got on a ship which took him the rest of the way around the world. When his ship reached the United 

States some newspaper reporters met him. They wrote stories about his trip for their papers. Not many boys of his age had traveled around the world alone.

Before long, Dick went to a military school in Virginia. He was one of the youngest and smallest boys at the school. But Dick Byrd had something more precious than age or size. He had courage and spirit and the will to do things. Two years later he went to the University of Virginia. This was the school which Thomas Jefferson had started.

During his first year he made the football team. 

He was still light and small, but he could think fast. 

He was a good quarterback. The quarterback is the player who runs the team during the game. The other players liked their steady, young quarterback because he did not get excited and lose his head.

OUR AMERICA 

The next year found Dick Byrd at a new school. 

It was the United States Naval Academy at Annapolis, Maryland. This is the school where officers are trained for the United States Navy. Dick had been interested in the sea and in ships ever since his trip around the world. He liked the Naval Academy very much even though the rules were strict and the work was hard.

Again Dick went out for football. He was put on the first team during his third year at the school. 

Once more he was the quarterback for the team. In one game he made the winning score. After the game someone called him a hero. "No," he said, "if I am a hero there are ten other heroes, too. There were eleven men on that team. I didn't make that score all by myself."

Later in the season his leg was broken. Several months passed before he could use it again. At another time he was doing some tricks in the gymnasium and slipped and fell to the floor from a great height. The force of the fall broke some bones in his ankle. Again he was taken to the hospital. 

Week after week slipped by and still Dick Byrd was in the hospital. He tried to study his lessons so that he would not get behind. It was not easy to study while he was lying in bed. The school year was almost over when he left the hospital. Soon it would be time to graduate.

Dick was far behind in his classes. His friends

were afraid he could not graduate. But Dick did not give up. He wanted to graduate with the rest of his class. He began to study harder than ever. He took the examinations and finally passed them. When the class of 1912 was graduated that summer, Dick Byrd was one of its members.

He was now an officer in the United States Navy. 

He was sent to serve on one of the warships. During his first year in the navy he saved the lives of two men. One of them he kept from being crushed by some heavy machinery. The other he saved from drowning. Later he received a medal for his heroism. 

One day something happened that changed his whole life. While he was on shore in Cuba he met an officer who flew one of the navy's airplanes. "How would you like to go up with me?" asked the officer. "I'd like nothing better," replied Dick Byrd. That afternoon he had his first ride in an airplane. It was a thrilling ride. He enjoyed every minute of it. Even before he landed he had made an important decision. "I am going to learn to fly," he said to himself.

He went back to his ship. More and more he thought about airplanes and flying. Many ideas came to his mind. Was it possible to fly across the ocean?

Could an airplane be used to explore the country around the North Pole? "Someday," he thought, "maybe I can find out." In the meantime, there was work to be done on the ship. 

His leg began to bother him. Two of the broken

OUR AMERICA 

bones in his ankle had never healed together. One day he fell down some stairs and hurt his leg again. The doctors did their best, but his leg would never be strong. He had to leave his ship and go to work in one of the navy offices. 

His health soon began to fail. He did not like office work. He wanted to fly, but the doctors said, "No." 

One day they told him, "Unless you get better, you will have to stop working altogether. You will have to go home and rest until you get well." "Give me a chance," Dick Byrd replied. "I want to fly. Let me try it for one month. If I don't get better, I will do whatever you say."

The doctors finally agreed. In 1917, Dick Byrd went to Florida to learn how to fly. He went to the navy's training school at Pensacola. In a short time he regained his health. Except for a few short illnesses, he has had good health ever since.

The first day he saw a plane crash. He was on the grounds of the training school. He heard a shout and looked up. A plane was headed downward. He watched it spin while the pilot fought to get it under control. Then it dived straight down into the blue water of the bay. Both men in the plane were killed. 

It was a horrible thing to watch, but it took more than a plane crash to make Dick Byrd give up flying. 

Later that day he went up with an old classmate. His training started at once. He learned to take airplane engines apart and then put them together. He

learned how the different parts of an airplane work. 

He was taught how to handle the controls of a plane. He learned how to read the instruments. 

After six hours of flying with a teacher he made his first trip alone. Three times he went up and three times he came down. He wanted to practice landing his seaplane. He knew that the most important thing about flying was to be able to land safely. 

During the next few weeks he made hundreds of landings. At first, he practiced only with seaplanes. 

Then he learned to handle a landplane. Before long he was given his "wings." This meant that he was now a real airplane pilot.

Flying was not nearly so safe then as it is today. 

There were many accidents. Some pilots were killed. 

Dick Byrd had several narrow escapes. Once his plane was struck by another just as he was taking off. He Ford 

YRD ANTARTIC Publishers' Photo Service 

Byrd's early training with planes was important. Many years later he used the plane in this picture for flying over the South Pole.

OUR AMERICA 

was thrown into the water. Both he and the other pilot were picked up by a boat. They were wet but unhurt. 

Dick Byrd began to study the reasons why planes crash. He found that over half of the accidents were caused by carelessness or poor judgment. He also began to study better ways of navigation. Later, he made some new instruments. These became very useful in flying from one place to another. 

The World War was being fought at that time. 

Byrd was sent to build a small airplane station in Canada. He was supposed to watch for submarines. 

One day news came that a submarine had been seen. 

He flew out over the ocean to look for it. A thick fog came up before he could find it. He got back just in time to make a safe landing.

During the next few years many experiences happened to Dick Byrd. He had never forgotten his idea of flying across the ocean. He talked to some of the officers in the navy about it. They were interested, and at last it was decided to make the trip.

In 1919, three navy planes were sent to Canada. 

They were going to fly from Canada to Europe. Just as the planes were ready to leave, a message came for 

Dick Byrd. It ordered him not to make the trip. So the three planes started without him. Only one of them made the trip safely. It was the first time any ocean had ever been crossed by an airplane. 

Dick Byrd was very disappointed. He still wanted to fly across the ocean. In 1921, he received permis-

sion to go to England. A large airship had been built there for the United States Navy. It was to be flown across the ocean to America. Byrd was going to make the trip back with it. 

Before the airship started across the ocean it had to be tested. He wanted to go along on the test trip, but again he was disappointed. All of the places had been filled. The next day he read in the paper that the big airship had been wrecked. It had exploded in the air and fallen into a river. All but five men were killed. 

His first chance to be an explorer came in 1925. A party of men wanted to explore the unknown country north of Greenland. The navy decided to send Byrd along. His job was to fly over this country and explore it from the air.

Several planes were taken apart and put on board a ship. When the ship reached northern Greenland, the planes were taken off and put together again. 

Some Eskimos came to watch the men. They had never before seen an airplane. 

Byrd told them he was going to fly over the land in one of the planes. They did not believe it was possible. Their eyes almost popped out of their heads when they finally saw a plane climb into the sky. 

They looked at Byrd and said, "He must be a great spirit."

The next few weeks were spent in flying over the unknown country. A man named Floyd Bennett

OUR AMERICA 

went with Byrd on most of these trips. They took pictures of the frozen Arctic land so that all might see what it was like. From their plane they could see many miles of country at one glance. The new way of exploring was much better than the old. 

Byrd and Bennett learned a great deal about flying in the cold northern country. They were sure that the best way to travel in the Arctic was by airplane. One day Dick Byrd had a long talk with Floyd 

Bennett. He told him about his idea of flying over the North Pole. The North Pole is the top of the world. Floyd Bennett liked Byrd's idea.

When Byrd and Bennett returned to America they began at once to get ready for a trip to the North 

Pole. They found a good plane with three motors.

They named it the Josephine Ford. It was named after Henry Ford's granddaughter. 

The plane, together with other supplies, was put on a ship. They took about fifty men with them and sailed for Spitzbergen. Spitzbergen is an island north of Norway. They planned to fly from Spitzbergen to the North Pole.

The little harbor at Spitzbergen was filled with ice. 

The only landing place had already been taken by another ship. There was only one way to get the plane on shore. A large raft was built. The plane was lifted over the side of the ship on to the raft. Then the raft was rowed ashore. Great pieces of floating ice nearly wrecked the raft several times.

When the plane was on shore, the wheels were removed. They were replaced by long, heavy skis. Byrd knew that the plane could not take off in the snow with wheels. That was why he decided to try skis.

The men began to fix a place where the plane could take off. They built a long runway in the snow. 

Three times the plane tried to take off. Each time the skis broke. Once the plane came to a stop in a snowdrift. Stronger skis were made. The fourth time the plane succeeded in getting into the air. It was given a good test before it landed. Then the plane was loaded for the trip to the North Pole. Dick 

Byrd and Floyd Bennett got in and tried to start off.

The load was too heavy. The plane could not get into the air. Again it ran into a snow drift, but no harm was done. The plane was pulled back to the head of the runway. A part of the food and other supplies was taken off to lighten the load. Neither

Byrd nor Bennett had slept for more than thirty-six hours. But still they decided to leave as soon as the plane could be made ready. 

At that time two other explorers were in Spitzbergen. They were Roald Amundsen and Lincoln 

Ellsworth. They were getting ready to fly to the 

North Pole in an airship. It was really a race to see who would get there first. The whole world was watching. Who would win, Byrd or Amundsen?

Finally Byrd and Bennett made a successful take off. Amundsen was not yet ready, but he was a good

OUR AMERICA 

sport. He joined Byrd's men and cheered as the 

Josephine Ford started off toward the North Pole. It was Amundsen who first discovered the South Pole in 1911.

Hour after hour Byrd and Bennett flew through the northern sky. They took turns steering the ship.

They knew that a forced landing would probably mean death. One of the motors began to leak oil. 

Both men were afraid it would stop. But the motor continued to run. At nine o'clock on the morning of 

May 9, 1926, Byrd figured they were over the North Pole.

They took some pictures. Then they circled around several times. In this way they traveled around the top of the world in a very few minutes. Within fifteen minutes they were headed back for Spitzbergen. 

Seven hours later they landed. Amundsen and Ellsworth were among the first to greet them. 

At last, one of Dick Byrd's dreams had come true. 

He was the first man to fly to the North Pole in an airplane. But there was still one other dream. He had not flown across the ocean. On the way back to 

America, Dick Byrd turned to Floyd Bennett. "Now we can fly the Atlantic," he said. They began to make plans.

A great celebration was held in New York when they returned. Byrd and Bennett were given medals. 

Other great honors were also paid them. Later, Congress gave Byrd the title of Commander. They were

national heroes. Calvin Coolidge, President of the 

United States, came to greet them. In Virginia, Dick 

Byrd was greeted by his brother Harry, who was then governor of the state.

The next spring a new plane was built for Byrd. 

It was called the America. A special runway was built for the take off. One day Byrd and Bennett decided to test the plane. There was a crash and the plane rolled over on its back. Byrd's arm was broken. 

Bennett suffered a broken leg and a badly hurt head. 

In May, 1927, Byrd permitted a young flier to use his runway. The young man's name was Charles A. 

Lindbergh. Byrd went down to wish him luck just before he left on his famous trip to Paris. On June 

29th, Byrd and his three friends were ready to try again. Bennett came down from the hospital to see them take off.

Before long the plane ran into a thick fog and a heavy rain. After a while they had clear weather, but soon they flew into another fog. For more than two thousand miles they were unable to see the ocean. 

They tried to climb above the fog, but it was no use. 

Sometimes they got radio messages from ships below them. This helped them to go in the right direction. The weather was fairly good when they reached the coast of France. They started for Paris, but the fog became so very thick they could not see to land. Finally, Byrd decided to land in the ocean near a little French fishing village. The plane was

OUR AMERICA 

wrecked, but none of the four men was badly hurt.

The men swam and waded ashore. It was fortytwo hours since they had left New York. The kindly people of the village gave them food and clean clothes and a place to sleep. The news of their narrow escape was flashed to all parts of the world. Again there were great honors for Byrd, first in France, and then in America.

Two of Dick Byrd's dreams had now come true. 

He had flown over the North Pole, and across the Atlantic Ocean. Most men would have been content. 

But Byrd had a third dream by this time. "I must fly over the South Pole," he said. Again he began to make plans for a trip. One of Byrd's ships in the 

Antarctic Publishers' Photo Service

In December, 1928, Byrd's party reached the Antarctic country. There were about forty men in the party. Many of the men were scientists. They had three planes and supplies enough to last for several years. A camp was built. It was called "Little America." Byrd and his men lived there for more than a year while they explored the unknown land at the bottom of the world. 

Late in November, 1929, Byrd decided to make a trip over the South Pole. The biggest plane was made ready. It was called the Floyd Bennett after the man who had gone with Byrd to the North Pole.

Floyd Bennett had died before Byrd left for the 

South Pole. Three men went with Byrd. One of them was Bernt Balchen. He had flown across the ocean with Byrd in the America.

As the Floyd Bennett took off for the South Pole, the men on the ground waved and cheered. Some of them had been at Spitzbergen when Byrd left for the 

North Pole. Both photographs and moving pictures were taken from the plane as it sped along. A few years later millions of people saw these moving pictures. 

At one place the plane had to climb over some mountains almost two miles high. The plane was heavily loaded. No one knew whether or not they could make it. They came nearer to the mountains. Bernt Balchen was at the controls. He was trying to make the plane go higher. "We must lighten the load or turn back," he

OUR AMERICA 

shouted. Only two things could be done. One was to dump some of the gasoline. The other was to throw some of the food away. Byrd thought for a moment. 

Then he said, "Throw out a bag of food."

This made the plane a little lighter. But still they were not over the mountains. Another bag of food was thrown overboard. Again the plane climbed a little higher. In a few minutes they were over the mountains.

They dropped down a little lower and flew straight on toward the South Pole. In a few hours they reached it. A radio message was sent. It told the world that Byrd was then flying above the South Pole. The plane circled and flew around the bottom of the world. Byrd thought back to a day three years ago. He and Floyd Bennett were then flying around the top of the world at the North Pole.

Byrd picked up a stone which he had carried all the way from America. It was a stone from Floyd Bennett's grave. It was wrapped in an American flag. 

Byrd looked at the stone for a moment. Then he dropped it down to the bottom of the world. The men stood and saluted as the flag and stone fell. Then the plane headed back to Little America. 

Early in 1930, Byrd's party sailed for America. As 

Dick Byrd was leaving Little America, he stopped and looked at the lonely camp. Then he turned to one of his friends. "We'll be back, Bill," he said.

Again there were great honors waiting for Byrd.

He was given some more medals. He was made a 

Rear Admiral in the United States Navy. No other

American explorer had given the world so much new knowledge about unknown lands. But Byrd was restless. There were still so many things he wanted to know about the country at the bottom of the world. "But surely," thought the people, "there is nothing left for Byrd to explore now." They were mistaken. 

They did not know Byrd. He began to make plans for a second trip to the Antarctic. In the fall of 1933 he e. 

A Boy Scout who was a member of the Byrd Antarctic Expedition shows a penguin to some fellow Boy Scouts.

OUR AMERICA 

left with another party. Again many of the men were scientists. They went to study the strange lands about the South Pole.

They headed for Little America again. Everything was just as they had left it. On one table stood a coffee pot and half a loaf of bread. Cans of food were piled in the shelves behind the stove. Someone pushed a button. A bell rang. The telephone still worked. On another table stood a phonograph. A record was in place. One of the men started it. It began to play "The Bells of St. Mary's." Another man pushed a light switch. The lights still burned. 

Many of the men in Byrd's party had been with him on the first trip to Antarctica. What thoughts must have passed through their minds as they came again to Little America. Perhaps no one but Byrd had believed they would ever see it again. But here it was, and here they were. 

During 1934, Byrd and his men explored thousands of miles of new country. Many new discoveries were made. Almost every day radio messages were sent to the outside world. In this way people could read in their papers about something which had happened at the bottom of the world the day before. Millions of people listened to the radio broadcasts from Little America. 

All exploring trips must end sometime. Early in 

1935, they began to get ready for the trip back to the United States. The ships were loaded with every-

thing that could be taken. All of the records and pictures were aboard. Only the two radio towers and the flag showed where Little America was. Then 

Byrd lowered the flag for the last time at Little 

America. 

Admiral Richard E. Byrd now lives quietly at his home in Boston, Massachusetts. He plays with his son and visits with his friends. He reads and studies and thinks. He is still a young man. Perhaps, even now, he is planning another exploring trip to some far corner of the world. Who knows? لم

XXII 

"His newspapers fought for the people." 

Benjamin Franklin's newspaper helped to tell the story of America at that time. The newspapers of today tell the story of what is happening in this country and in other parts of the world. 

Newspapers and newspaper men have done much to build the United States into a great nation. They have often taken the lead in a fight for better and happier ways of living.

One great newspaper man started his first paper about two years after Alexander Bell invented the telephone. That man, who died in the same year 

Richard E. Byrd flew over the North Pole, was

E. W. Scripps. 

When Richard E. Byrd went to the South Pole, the people of America followed him. They followed him through the newspapers. 

That is the way most people now learn about what is happening in the world. The newspapers tell the day-by-day story of everything that is important. 

Perhaps a wonderful invention has been finished. A new way to fight an old disease has been discovered. 298

An important law has been passed. A great man comes to the end of his work. 

All of these things are news. They are a part of the story of America. But the newspapers, too, have a story. It is a story of how they came to be what they are now. It is the story of the men who make newspapers. One of those men was E. W. Scripps. 

Edward Wyllis Scripps was born on a farm near 

Brown Bros.

E. W. Scripps

OUR AMERICA 

Rushville, Illinois. The day of his birth was June 18, 1854. At that time Walter Reed was three years old. Dan Beard was four and Luther Burbank was five. Thomas Edison and Alexander Bell were each about seven. 

Eddie Scripps was almost fifteen years old when

General Dodge finished the Pacific Railroad. Gutzon 

Borglum was then two years old. Edgar Hewett was four, Julius Rosenwald was seven, Jane Addams was nine, and Theodore Roosevelt was eleven. Many years later, Eddie Scripps' newspapers were to tell about the work of each of these people. 

The farm on which the Scripps family lived was a poor one. The family was large. All of the children had to work at an early age. One of Eddie's first jobs was to carry in wood for the fires. Another was to drive the cows in from the pasture. Later, he had to cut the wood and help to milk the cows. "While milking I would try to make up little poems," said E. W. Scripps one day. He was telling some stories about his early life. "The result was that I would do about as much milking in an hour as the others would do in ten or fifteen minutes. "My family and the neighbors looked upon me as a very lazy boy. Sometimes I was called the laziest boy in the whole country. This often made me feel proud, and I would laugh to myself. I knew that instead of being the laziest boy, I was one of the most active. "I liked best to work in the fields. I could plow and

hoe and rake better than any other boy of my age. In working a field, I always liked to divide it up. Each person was given his share. Then I would compare my work with that done by the others. It always made me happy when I did more than the hired men." 

When Eddie Scripps was about fourteen years old, his father became ill. Eddie was left to take care of the farm. He hired some town boys to help him with the summer work. They were each to receive twentyfive cents a day. As usual, Eddie divided the field into an equal number of rows.

The boys started a race. Eddie finished his row first. He was so far ahead that he sat down and waited for the rest to catch up. He joked with them for being so slow. This started an argument. The other boys said Eddie had been training for his work. 

It was not fair for him to be in the race. 

This gave Eddie an idea. He started another contest. The boys voted that he should not take part in it. He was to act as the judge for the others. Away they went, each trying to hoe better and faster than anyone else. And while they worked, Eddie looked on and took it easy. "From that time," he said many years later, "there were always plenty of boys to work on the farm. All of the town boys wanted to get into the game. It was my job to start a contest of some kind. After it had begun, I would sit in the shade and read. I always carried a book with me."

MAURICE 

Eddie Scripps reading in the shade after starting a work contest for the other boys

Eddie Scripps soon learned a great deal about handling people. He found they would work harder when they were trying to beat some one else. He learned that all of them liked to be winners. This understanding of people became very useful to him. 

He never forgot what he learned from the cornfield contests. 

Life on the farm was not all work. "There were many days," he said, "when I could run away into the woods with a book. For hours I would lie on my back below the moving branches of the trees. I would read or dream or think. I could look up and see the clouds floating in the sky."

The long winter evenings gave him more time for reading. Then, too, it was pleasant to walk about and feel the cool night wind. It was fun to look up at the stars and think about all sorts of things. When 

E. W. Scripps was an old man he said, "I have always thought it was my good luck to have lived on a farm." 

That was the way Edgar L. Hewett felt, too. 

When Eddie Scripps was eighteen years old, he moved to Detroit, Michigan. For a while he worked in a drug store. Then he got a job sweeping out a newspaper office. His pay was three dollars a week. 

Eddie's older brother was one of the owners of this newspaper. 

One day the newspaper office burned down. Eddie's brother, James, then started a paper of his own. It was called the Detroit News. Eddie was given a job

OUR AMERICA 

collecting for some of the papers that were sold. He was paid six dollars a week.

Many years before, another boy, Benjamin Franklin, had worked for his brother in a newspaper office. 

Like Benjamin, Eddie was to become much more famous as a newspaper man than his older brother. 

Perhaps Eddie was helped more by his older sister, Ellen, than by anyone else. He never decided on any、 thing important without first asking what she thought.

Eddie's next job was to get people to take the News. He started a route and delivered papers to his customers. After a while he hired a boy to deliver the papers for him. Then he started more paper routes and hired more boys. Before long he was earning about forty dollars a week from his routes. 

Of course, Eddie was the boss. This just suited him. It was like the old days in the cornfield. It gave him time to do other things. He wanted to learn how to write, so he became a reporter on the paper. In a short time he was made the editor of the News. 

After six years in Detroit, E. W. Scripps went on a trip to Europe. He came back with the idea of starting a newspaper of his own. He wanted to start it in Cleveland, Ohio. His brothers in Detroit agreed to help him get the new paper started. 

A few months later the first copy of the Cleveland Penny Press was printed. It came out in November, 1878, about one year before Thomas Edison invented

the electric light. It was called the Penny Press because it sold for one cent a copy. Today it is known simply as the Cleveland Press. It is one of the best newspapers in Cleveland.

The young editor of the Penny Press had some ideas of his own about running a newspaper. He believed that it should give all the news it could get. 

Each news story should be written in an interesting way. The price of the paper should be so low that everyone could afford to buy it. 

He believed that a paper should not belong to any political party. "We have no politics," he wrote in his paper. "We are not Republican, not Democrat. 

We will work for good men and good ideas. We will work against bad men and poor ideas. It makes no difference to which party they belong. "We shall tell no lies about any person or any party. 

A newspaper should simply print all of the facts it can get. The people can decide for themselves what to do about a person or a party." At that time almost every newspaper in America belonged to one party or another. The Penny Press was one of the first to break away from this custom. It was indeed a great step forward.

The Penny Press soon became one of the best liked papers in Cleveland. One reason for its success was that it dared to give news which not every paper would print. Once a rich man killed himself. Some of the papers hid the truth by saying he died from

Part of the front page of the Penny Press which was E. W. Scripps' first newspaper heart failure. The Penny Press told the facts just as if he had been some poor, unknown man.

After a few months as editor of the paper, E. W. 

Scripps hired a man to do this work. He wanted to be free to study and make plans. Then, too, he believed in leaving alone the editors who worked for him. As long as they made the paper interesting he was satisfied. 

When the Penny Press was running smoothly, he began to look for a new job. With the help of his brothers, he bought a paper in Cincinnati, Ohio. Its name was changed to the Cincinnati Post. The new paper began at once to fight a battle for the people of Cincinnati.

The city owned a small railroad. Some dishonest men were trying to sell it for a very low price. E. W.

Scripps and his editors found out about this. The 

Post printed the story of what was taking place. The people voted against the plan to sell the railroad. This kept the city from losing a large sum of money. 

In 1885, E. W. Scripps started a new paper in Covington, Kentucky. Only two years before, he had bought the Cincinnati Post. The new paper was called the Kentucky Post. Covington was the city in which Dan Beard was born. It was here that Dan 

Beard and his friends lived and played long before there were any Boy Scouts. 

That same year E. W. Scripps was married. For the next six years he lived in Ohio. His first three children were born there. Then, in 1891, the family moved to a ranch in southern California. Here three more children were born. One of them was Robert 

P. Scripps. Today he is one of the men in charge of all the Scripps-Howard newspapers. 

In the years that followed, E. W. Scripps bought and started many newspapers. One of them was the 

Sun in San Diego, California. Another was the News in San Francisco. Before long he also had papers in other states. many "I have been interested in more than thirty papers," he wrote one time. Some of these were sold, and a few of them failed. In late years, the men who took his place have added many new papers. The 

Scripps-Howard chain of newspapers now reaches from the Atlantic to the Pacific.

OUR AMERICA 

E. W. Scripps also found time to start three very important services for newspapers. One of them was a company to supply papers with special stories, cartoons, and pictures. It was called the Newspaper 

Enterprise Association. Most people know it simply as the N. E. A. It now serves more than seven hundred American newspapers.

He also started the company now known as the United Press. It gathers news from all parts of the world. Then it sends this news to the papers. In this way a paper can print news which its own reporters could not get. The United Press now serves papers in every large country in the world. 

The third service for newspapers is called Science Service. It gets news about the men of science and the work they are doing. This news is written so that it will be interesting and easy to read. Then it is sent to the newspapers.

All of these services help to make better newspapers. Because of them a paper can now print news from some far-away land almost as soon as it happens. A short time later a picture can be printed to go with the news. Even if E. W. Scripps had done nothing else, these things alone would have made him a great newspaper man.

Whenever E. W. Scripps finished one job, he liked to try something new. One time he started a newspaper which had no advertising in it. This paper was started in Chicago. It was called The Day Book. He

knew that the advertisers sometimes tried to keep out of the papers the news that would hurt their business.

He believed that a newspaper should be honest with its readers. It should print all the news fairly and without fear. Also, when a newspaper prints advertising, it should print only honest advertising. He would not let his papers print advertising for harmful medicines. He ordered them to refuse advertising which made untrue promises. 

As the years passed his papers became more and more powerful. E. W. Scripps wanted them to use their power to help the people. His papers fought for laws to keep children from doing dangerous work. 

They have tried in many ways to make things better for the workingman. 

In 1917, his health began to fail. He had to give up much of his work. He began to make plans for his son, Robert, and another man to take his place. The other man was Roy W. Howard. Since 1920, Roy W.

Howard has been one of the men in charge of the 

Scripps-Howard papers. 

E. W. Scripps spent the next few years resting and traveling. He made several trips to other parts of the world. He died in 1926 while he was on one of these trips. He was buried in the ocean near the coast of Africa.

One evening many years before he had said, "This is the greatest country the world ever knew, because

OUR AMERICA 

it has the greatest people. Some day our country is going to need my newspapers." 

Courtesy, The Indianapolis Star 

A modern newspaper press Hundreds of great newspapers now serve the people in every part of the United States. Some of these are papers which were started by E. W. Scripps. Of course, many other great newspaper men have also done their part. 

America will always need honest and fearless newspapers. But even more than this, it will need men and women who are not afraid to fight for what is right. It takes great men to make great newspapers.

XXIII 

"He stands for law and order." 

In the United States the people choose whom they want to run the government for them. That is the American way of government. 

It is the duty of the government to make wise laws and see that they are enforced for the good of the people. Perhaps the most famous lawenforcing officers today are the Special Agents who work for the United States Government. 

One reason for their success is that they have a good leader. About two years before E. W. 

Scripps died, John Edgar Hoover became the chief of the "G-Men." 

Poor old "Oscar" is dead again!

There he lies, stretched out on the floor. But no one needs to worry. "Oscar" is not a real person.

He is only a wax figure dressed in the clothes of a man. He is just a dummy used in training the Special Agents. 

Perhaps the Special Agents are better known by another name. They are the famous "G-Men." They work for the Federal Bureau of Investigation. This 311

OUR AMERICA 

Courtesy, Federal Bureau of Investigation, U. S. Dept. of Justice John Edgar Hoover 

Bureau is a part of the United States Government. 

The Director of the Bureau and the Chief of the "G-Men" is John Edgar Hoover.

He was born on New Year's Day, 1895. The place was the District of Columbia. This is where the 

United States has its capital city of Washington. It is the home of the United States Government. It was

here that J. Edgar Hoover grew up and went to school.

From the very first, he has worked for the government. He finished high school in 1913 when he was eighteen years old. That same year he got a job as a clerk in the Library of Congress. It was a fairly good job, but J. Edgar Hoover had other ideas.

He wanted to be a lawyer. So he started going to school at night. He went to the law school at George 

Washington University. It was not easy to work during the day and to study at night. But like many other young men and women, he kept at it until he finished.

In 1917, he began to work with the Department of Justice. This, too, is one part of the United States 

Government. At first he was a clerk. He did his work so well that he was soon given a much better job. The same thing happened again. This time he was made the assistant director of the Bureau of Investigation. 

One day in May, 1924, he was asked to be the director of the Bureau. He thought about the matter for a little while. Then he asked one very important question. Would he be allowed to run the Bureau without any politics? The answer was "yes." So J. Edgar 

Hoover agreed to take the job.

Since that time the work of the Bureau has become more and more important. The Special Agents have caught one dangerous lawbreaker after another.

They have succeeded where others have failed. Today

OUR AMERICA 

people everywhere say, "If the G-Men start after someone, he is sure to be caught."

It is not difficult to find the reasons for their great success. Soon after J. Edgar Hoover became director, he made some very important changes in the Bureau. 

He refused to give any Special Agent a better job unless it had been earned by good work. He also began to be much more careful in hiring new Special 

Agents. 

No one can become a G-Man now unless he is just the right kind of person. He must be between twentyfive and thirty-five years of age. He must have good health and be able to pass a great many tests. Then, too, he has to be a citizen of this country. He must be willing to work in any part of the United States. 

Perhaps the most important rule is that he must have been educated or trained in one of three ways. 

The first is to be a graduate from a good law school. 

The second is to be a trained expert who knows how business records and books should be kept. The last is to have been an officer of the law such as a sheriff or policeman.

A man who has been educated in one of these ways knows how to find facts. He knows which facts are good and which are bad. This helps him to work on a case without wasting time. But there is still another reason why J. Edgar Hoover wants only well-educated men.

The Special Agents work on many different kinds

of cases. They must know how to talk with all kinds of people. A G-Man might spend one hour getting facts from some important business man. An hour later he might be trying to get a dangerous criminal to tell the truth. He has to be able to fit in with people wherever he is sent.

About six hundred Special Agents work for the 

Bureau. Most of them have graduated from good colleges or universities. Many of them can speak a language other than English. They have done all kinds of work, and they come from all parts of the United States. These things are very important to the work of the Bureau. 

One time a G-Man saved an airship from being destroyed because he could talk a foreign language. A 

Special Agent who knew music was able to get a job in a night club orchestra. This made it possible for him to get facts about a criminal who often came there. 

Another case was finished by a G-Man who had often helped the cooks in the army. He got a job peeling potatoes in a kitchen where a criminal was working. Before long he got the facts he needed, and the criminal was put in a jail.

The Bureau has field offices in thirty-seven different cities. Each field office takes care of a certain territory or district. Some of these districts are so large they include two entire states. A number of 

Special Agents work out of each office. One of them

The Federal Bureau of Investigation has one field office in each of these cities. is in charge of the others. He reports what his men are doing to the main office in Washington. 

In this way, J. Edgar Hoover can always tell where his Special Agents are. If more G-Men are needed in one part of the country, he can send help from a field office that is not so busy. They do not need to wait for something to happen in their own district. They can work anywhere. This makes it possible for a small number of men to work on a great many cases.

But J. Edgar Hoover does not stop with hiring the best men he can get. He believes they should be very carefully trained. He has set up several training schools as a part of the Bureau. This is another reason why his G-Men have been so successful.

One of these schools trains the new Special Agents. 

They must spend fourteen weeks in the school before they are sent to a field office. During this time they learn a great many things. They are taught by some of the best experts in the country. 

The new G-Men study books and listen to talks. 

They learn not only what to do but also how to do it. 

They watch the experts show them how a thing should be done. They practice doing different kinds of work by themselves. Then they take tests to see how well they have learned. 

One part of their training is to clear up a makebelieve crime. The teacher takes a class of Special 

Agents into a room. The room is torn up as if a fight had taken place. The lifeless form of "Oscar" lies on the floor. The Special Agents have already been told what to look for in this kind of case. This is their chance to show how much they can find out about the murder of "Oscar." 

At another time they are taken to study "Beulah."

This is the name given to an old automobile. Here the 

Special Agents learn how to look for fingerprints which might be left on an automobile. They also learn to look for other signs which might tell by whom a car had been used. 

Later they are led to "The Rogue's Den." This is a room fixed up to look as if criminals had lived in it. 

The teacher tells the G-Men to study everything in the room. They are supposed to find out as much as

OUR AMERICA 

163-196 

Federal Bureau of Investigation, U. S. Dept. of Justice 

The new Special Agents study "Beulah," and learn how to look for fingerprints and other information on an automobile. they can about the persons who used the room. With a little practice they soon learn that everything has a meaning of its own.

A good Special Agent must know how to get people to talk. So the new men are taught how to ask questions. They practice asking each other questions about a make-believe case. Sometimes they are sent out to get facts from persons who are members of the Bureau. Of course, the new G-Man does not know this. He is really being tested all the time. 

The sand table, too, is used in the training work. 

On this they make a small farm, a country home, a

village, or a part of a city. All of the buildings, trees, roads, streets, and even animals are put in their proper places. Then they study how to keep a criminal from getting away from such a place.

After a while the Special Agents are given a test of what they learned from the sand table. They are taken to a real house out in the country. They are told to close in upon it without being seen. Inside the house are some older G-Men. They watch carefully to see how well the new ones have learned their lesson. 

All Special Agents are taught to be good shots.

They practice shooting at both indoor and outdoor targets. They shoot at still targets and at moving ones. They learn to shoot while they are standing, sitting, running, or jumping. They learn to shoot from an automobile which is going over a rough road at high speed.

Special Agents must even learn how to jump from an automobile, get behind cover, and shoot almost at one time. They are taught how to shoot at night as well as in the day. They must know how to use many different kinds of guns, and how to take care of them. 

This training has kept a great many men from being killed.

A part of the new Special Agents' time is spent in the gymnasium. This is also a part of the training school. Here they are taught how to take care of themselves in a fight. They learn something about boxing and jiujitsu. They learn to protect themselves

OUR AMERICA 

so they will not get hurt. They also learn how to take a prisoner without doing him any great harm. 

Moving pictures are used for a part of the training. These show the new G-Men the right and wrong ways of working on a case. They show how fingerprints should be taken, or how to make use of science in fighting the lawbreakers. Sometimes the moving pictures show parts of a real case. 

One time a man who worked for the government had something happen to him. He claimed that he lost his eyesight. He wanted to get money from the government to pay for his blindness. A Special Agent was sent to study the case.

Without showing himself, he took some moving pictures of the man at work around his home. These pictures showed him doing things no blind man could have done. Of course, that was the end of the case. 

The man was just trying to cheat the government. 

The new Special Agents are trained in a great many other ways. Before they leave the training school, each G-Man is put to work on some real cases. 

At first he is put on a case with some of the men in the 

Washington office. They watch and help him. If he does well, he is soon allowed to work on a case by himself.

When the training has been finished, the Special Agents are sent to the different field offices. But J. 

Edgar Hoover has still another training school which they must attend. It is called the Retraining School.

Each G-Man must come to it at least once every eighteen months. This time the training lasts only four weeks, but it gives the men all that is new in their work.

There are still more training schools in the Bureau. 

One of them trains the chief clerks who work in the field offices. Another one is the Police Training 

School. The students in this school are sheriffs and police officers. They come to the school from all parts of the United States.

They are given about the same training as the G-Men. The training course lasts twelve weeks. 

Many of them go home and give the same kind of training to other officers in their city or county. They know that it pays to have well-trained policemen. J. 

Edgar Hoover and his G-Men have proved that. 

The men of science have done many things to help the Bureau. They have invented machines and discovered new ways to get facts. J. Edgar Hoover believes in helping his G-Men to fight crime in every way he can. So more and more he is turning to science. This is another reason for the great success of his Special Agents. 

One part of the Bureau in Washington is used for making scientific studies. There are machines that tell from which gun a bullet was fired. There are special lights which show any kind of hidden writing. 

And most important, there are experts to make studies of almost every kind.
