The secret message in this letter has been discovered by the use of a special light in the Bureau of Investigation. 

Some of them work with the messages sent by criminals. They compare the writing with samples which the Bureau has saved from other cases. From this study they can often tell who wrote the message. 

They also study the paper upon which the message was written. They find out what kind it is, where it was made, and when and where it was sold. With these facts they can often tell the G-Men where to look for a certain person. A boy who wrote letters demanding money from Shirley Temple was caught by G-Men in this way.

Among the experts in the Bureau are those who study footprints, tool marks, and automobile-tire tracks. If a clear footprint or track has been left be-

hind, a cast is made of it. This cast can be kept as long as needed. When a man is caught his footprints can be compared with those in the cast. 

The Bureau keeps a record of all the different tire patterns. By comparing a track with these patterns, the experts can tell the G-Man what kind of tire was used on a car. These facts are very useful to the Special Agents. Many cases have been cleared up by studies of this kind. 

Other experts make studies of ashes, dust, soil, cloth, hairs, blood spots, and a great many other things. The dust or soil on a man's shoes might tell the part of the country in which he had been. The ashes of burned cloth might tell what kind of suit a man was wearing. Even the smallest fact sometimes helps the Special Agent to get his man. 

In one case a man had been arrested for killing another person. The dirt underneath the fingernails of the dead person was studied. It was found to contain some tiny blue threads. It was proved they came from the clothes of the prisoner. Then the dirt underneath the fingernails of the prisoner was tested. It was found to contain little pieces of skin from the face of the dead person.

A few men in the Bureau spend most of their time studying guns and bullets. The Bureau keeps a record of the different kinds of guns which are made. 

When a G-Man sends in a bullet, the expert can soon tell from what kind of gun it was shot. This helps

OUR AMERICA 

the G-Man to look only for a certain kind of gun. 

Science has helped in still another way. It found long ago that no two persons ever have the same fingerprints. In the Bureau at Washington is the largest collection of fingerprints in the world. There are more than six million of them, and new ones are being added every day.

These fingerprint records help, not only the Special Agents, but all other officers of the law. Suppose the police in a city arrest a man whom they do not know. 

They think he is wanted by the police of some other city, but they are not sure. They take his fingerprints and send them to the Bureau. If the Bureau already has his fingerprints, it can tell the police who he is and for what he is wanted. 

In one part of the Bureau are the fingerprints of persons who are not criminals. Many good citizens have sent their fingerprints to the Bureau. This helps to protect them. A person can always prove who he is by his fingerprints.

It is getting harder and harder for the lawbreaker to get away. J. Edgar Hoover and his G-Men have shown others how to fight crime. Out of every hundred men they catch, ninety-four are sent to prison. 

They have not yet failed on a single important case. 

The use of science and well-trained men who are carefully chosen is the secret of their success. 

Many visitors come to the Bureau in Washington. 

J. Edgar Hoover likes to see them come. It shows they

325. are interested in the work of his men. It also shows they are interested in having the laws obeyed. This is much more important.

Every good citizen wants the laws to be enforced. 

He knows that laws are made to protect him and his family. He knows that the police and other officers are helping him. A town or country where the laws are not obeyed is a poor place in which to live. 

J. Edgar Hoover has made people see how important it is that the laws should be obeyed. He has made many of them want to help. Perhaps that is the most important part of his work. One day he got a letter from a twelve-year-old boy. "I want to grow up to serve my country in fighting crime," wrote the boy. "I admire your stand for law and order. I want to train myself to do the same kind of work. I need some help in knowing how to go about it. "Do not think because I am quite young that I will forget about it. I want to plan my life way ahead of time. I want to help my community be a better place in which to live."

If only all the people felt this way, how much easier 

J. Edgar Hoover's job would be!

XXIV 

FRANK E. WEYMOUTH, CHARLES H. 

PURCELL, and JOSEPH B. STRAUSS 

"They are building the things that couldn't be done."

The United States has always been a land of great natural wealth. Even here, however, the people have had to struggle with the forces of nature.

In some parts of the country the climate is dry, and it is difficult to get enough water. In other places there are great bodies of water that keep people from moving about as freely as they would like.

While John Edgar Hoover and the Special 

Agents are winning their fight against the forces of crime, great engineers are bringing some of the forces of nature under control. Three of those engineers are Frank E. Weymouth, Charles H. 

Purcell, and Joseph B. Strauss. 1. 

The pitter-patter of rain drops was a welcome sound to the early people of the pueblos and cliffdwellings. They lived in a land of hot sun and dry 326

wind and thirsty ground. No one knew better how important it was to have water. 

If the rain did not come, their crops would fail.

What was worse, there was so little they could do about it. Some of them could dig ditches to bring water from a nearby river as did the people of the 

Chaco towns. Most of them could only move away as did the cliff-dwellers of Mesa Verde. 

There were also times when the rain poured down as if the clouds had burst. Then a high wall of water would rush down the rivers and creeks. Sometimes the water would spread out over the country in a great flood. The crops and everything else in its path would be washed away. It was too bad this water could not be saved until it was needed. 

Since that time a great deal has been learned about getting and saving water. In some parts of the 

United States the farmer can now have water whenever his crops need it. He does not have to worry about either too much or too little water. All of this has been brought about by the work of engineers and other men of science. 

They have found ways of pumping water out of deep wells. In some places a windmill is used. In other places a gasoline engine or an electric motor does the work. Better ways of running water through pipes and ditches have also been found. Then, too, new ways of farming that waste less water have been discovered.

OUR AMERICA 

Most important are the great dams which have been built. These dams stop the flow of water in a river. A great lake is formed behind the dam. This water can then be let out through gates as it is needed. In this way the farmers below the dam can always be sure of a good supply of water. 

In some parts of the United States, water from a dam has turned a sandy desert into rich farm land. 

One of these places is the Salt River Valley near Phoenix, Arizona. Here the water comes from Roosevelt Dam. It was named after Theodore Roosevelt. 

He was one of the first Presidents to see the need for great dams.

Many large dams have been built in the dry western states. Near Globe, Arizona, is the beautiful 

Coolidge Dam. The Elephant Butte Dam near Hot 

Springs, New Mexico, holds back the waters of the Rio Grande River. In Oregon there is the Owyhee 

Dam. In Wyoming there is the Shoshone Dam on a river of the same name.

All of these dams were built by the United States Government. Still other dams are now being built in many parts of the country. Among them are Norris Dam in Tennessee and Grand Coulee Dam on the 

Columbia River in eastern Washington. But none of these can compare with a dam which was finished in 1936.

In the fall of that year a button was pushed and Boulder Dam was put to work. This great dam

stands between the solid rock walls of Black Canyon. 

One end of the dam is in Nevada. The other end is in 

Arizona. Between the two states runs the wild Colorado River.

This is perhaps the most famous of western rivers. 

For millions of years it has been digging and cutting its way through the mountains. Its waters helped to make the wonderful Grand Canyon in Arizona. Then they rushed on to empty their sand into the great 

Pacific Ocean. 

Almost four hundred years ago Spanish explorers came looking for the seven cities of gold. They sailed up the lower part of the red, muddy Colorado River. 

Many years later, Jed Smith and the mountain men came to the west looking for beaver. They trapped along the upper part of the Colorado River where the water was clear and swift. Neither the Spanish explorers nor the mountain men dreamed that this rough river would ever be tamed.

In 1928, the United States Government agreed to build the dam. Two years later the government engineers decided the best place to put it was in Black Canyon. Here the rock walls were high and solid. 

They were strong enough to hold a dam in place. 

There was also plenty of room up-stream for a large lake.

One of the first jobs was to build roads so that the workers and machines could be brought to Black Canyon. There was nothing but wild desert country on all sides. The nearest large city was Los Angeles, Cali-

OUR AMERICA 

fornia. A short railroad also had to be built so that supplies could be hauled in for the dam. 

Many men were needed to build the dam, but there was no place for them to live. A whole town had to be built before the work could begin. This town was carefully planned by the government engineers. It was built with streets and pleasant homes. There were churches and schools and playgrounds. The government wanted it to be a clean and comfortable place in which to live. It was called Boulder City. 

Today it is one of the finest little towns in the west. 

The next big job was to tame the mighty Colorado River. The engineers knew that they could not build a dam in Black Canyon so long as the river rushed through it. But how could they get rid of the water while the dam was being built? 

The engineers knew the answer. Two long tunnels were dug through the rock walls on each side of the river. One end of each tunnel came out to the river's edge a long way above the dam. The other end of each tunnel was below the place where the dam was to be built.

The river was then blocked with a small dam near the upper end of these tunnels. This made the water run into the tunnels. It came out of the tunnels almost a mile farther downstream. In this way the path of the river was changed. The engineers were then free to begin building Boulder Dam in the old river bed.

First the dirt and stones in the old river bed had to be cleared away. This was done so that the bottom of the dam would rest upon the solid bedrock. In the meantime, other men were setting up machines for mixing concrete.

Concrete is made by mixing together cement, water, and sand or gravel. When the concrete dries it becomes as hard as stone. It is used in almost every large building. It is also used for sidewalks, streets, and roads.

Boulder Dam is a great solid block of concrete. It is the largest dam in the world. At the bottom it is more than six hundred feet thick, or about two city blocks. It is more than seven hundred feet high, or about as high as a sixty-story building. It is almost one-fourth of a mile across the top of the dam. 

More than three thousand men worked on the main part of Boulder Dam. When the work began, the engineers thought it would take about four or five years to finish it. Everything went so well that the dam itself was finished in less than two years. The great power house below the dam was not finished until 1936, more than one year later.

When the dam was ready, the gates to the tunnels were closed. Only a certain amount of water was allowed to flow down the river. The rest of the water was held back by Boulder Dam. A great lake slowly began to form above the dam. 

This lake will be about one hundred and fifteen

OUR AMERICA 

Brown Bros.

Boulder Dam from the down-stream side. The great lake formed by this dam is in the canyon above the dam. miles long when it is full. It will be the largest manmade lake in the world. It will hold so much water that a state the size of New York could be flooded to a depth of one foot. This great body of water will even make the desert country about it a little cooler. 

The water from this lake is already being put to

work. As it comes racing out through gates and tunnels, it is made to turn large machines in the powerhouse below the dam. These machines are generators that make electricity. They are the largest ever to be built anywhere in the world. No other power plant can make even half so much electricity as the one at 

Boulder Dam. 

The electricity from Boulder Dam will furnish light and power to cities, towns, farms, and ranches. 

It will pump water and run machines and light the homes on thousands of farms. It will run great factories and light the busy stores and streets of large cities. Some of these places will be about three hundred miles from the dam.

Los Angeles, about two hundred and sixty miles away, was the first city to buy electricity from

Boulder Dam. It began to use power from Boulder 

Dam in October, 1936. A great celebration was held in the city at that time. The sale of electricity to cities such as Los Angeles will bring in enough money to pay the government for building Boulder Dam. 

After the water passes through the powerhouse it flows on down the river. Many miles below the dam it will be used by farmers to irrigate their fields and orchards. Most of these farms are in the rich Imperial Valley of southern California. Others are in the southwestern part of Arizona. Now that Boulder

Dam has been built these farmers need not worry about having either a flood or too little water.

OUR AMERICA 

Boulder Dam is really a great giant that controls the mighty Colorado River. It holds back the spring floods and saves the water until it is needed. It puts the wild river to work at making electricity. It is making life safer and better and happier in many ways. That was the purpose for which Boulder Dam was built.

It stands as a great monument to the engineers who planned and built it. Among those engineers were Dr. Elwood Mead, R. F. Walter, Walker R. 

Young, and Francis T. Crowe. It is also a monument to the hundreds of men who helped the engineers build the dam. But still more important, Boulder 

Dam stands as a mark of how man is now able to control the forces of nature and make them his friends. 2. 

In a few years a man-made river will cross the state of California. It is now being built over dry desert land, through mountains, and under cities.

When it is finished, it will bring water from the Colorado River to Los Angeles and twelve other cities in southern California.

This man-made river is called the Colorado River Aqueduct. It was started about the same time that work began on Boulder Dam. If everything goes well, it will be finished by the end of 1938. It is even a bigger job than was the building of Boulder Dam.

The great aqueduct is being built because Los Angeles and the other cities want to be sure of a large supply of good water. No city can keep on growing without plenty of water. The cities of southern California have grown very fast indeed. Los Angeles had about three times as many people in 1930 as it had in 1910.

The battle for water is an old, old story to these cities. At first they could get enough from springs and deep wells. As the cities grew, more water was needed. The engineers from the city of Los Angeles began to look for a new water supply. At last they decided upon a plan.

That plan was to bring water to the city from a place called the Owens Valley. This valley is more than two hundred miles north of Los Angeles. In 

1913, the city finished the building of a long aqueduct to Owens Valley. Through the ditches and pipes of this aqueduct flowed a steady stream of clear fresh water.

It flowed down long valleys, over hills, and along the sides of large mountains. It flowed across the hot 

Mohave desert where Jed Smith had suffered from lack of water on his first trip to California. At last the silvery stream of water reached Los Angeles. 

For almost twenty-five years now, water from Owens Valley has been flowing into Los Angeles. 

During all that time the city has grown larger and larger. A few years ago one fact became clear. If

OUR AMERICA 

1000 SANTA 

MORICA 

AL 372 Los Angeles and other nearby cities kept on growing as fast as they had been, a still larger supply of water would soon be needed. 

Where could another supply of water be found?

Again the engineers began to look around. On the other side of the state was the Colorado River. The engineers tested the water and found that it was quite pure. Most of it came from the snow banks high in the mountains of Colorado, Wyoming, and Utah.

That was all very good, but the water in the Colorado was already being used. When the river was low, all of the water was needed by the farmers for their fields. Only during the flood season was there any unused water in the river. If only some way could be found to save the flood waters, there would be enough for everyone.

Then, in 1928, the government decided to build Boulder Dam. This made it possible for the cities to go ahead with their plan to bring water from the Colorado River. The first big job was to decide where the new aqueduct should be built. Engineers were

sent to study the country between Los Angeles and the Colorado River.

They studied hundreds of routes before the best one was chosen. One end of this route is on the Colorado River above Parker, Arizona. The other end is a few miles below Riverside, California. From this place water will be sent to each of the thirteen cities.

The eastern part of this route runs through a desert in which no one lives. This made it necessary for the engineers to do a great deal of work before the aqueduct could be built. They had to dig wells and put in many miles of water lines. They had to build good highways, telephone lines, and electric power lines. They also had to put up comfortable camps in which the men could live.

Most of this work was finished in 1933. In that same year another group of men began work on the longest and hardest part of the aqueduct. They began to dig tunnels through the mountains. The mountains along the way were too high for the aqueduct to go over them. They were too long to go around.

OUR AMERICA 

There was nothing else to do except to dig tunnels through them.

There are twenty-nine tunnels in the main line of the aqueduct. One of these tunnels is thirteen miles long. Another is more than seven miles long. All of the main line tunnels together make ninety-two miles.

The tunnels are so large that powerful machines were used for digging. Each tunnel is sixteen feet wide and sixteen feet high. This is about as high as 

Courtesy, Metropolitan Water District, So. Calif.

An open canal and the mouth of a tunnel on the Colorado River Aqueduct

most schoolrooms. The tunnels are shaped something like a horseshoe. When the rock was dug away, a strong lining of concrete was placed inside the tunnels.

Some parts of the aqueduct are being built by first digging a ditch. Large machines are also used for this work. A concrete pipe is then built in the ditch and covered over with dirt. This pipe is just as high as the tunnels but a little wider. 

In other places the water will run through an open canal. The bottom and sides of the canal are lined with concrete. The canals are twenty feet wide at the bottom and twelve feet deep. They are much wider at the top than at the bottom. 

Along the eastern half of the aqueduct a number of pumping stations are being built. At these places giant pumps will force the water uphill. Electricity from Boulder Dam will be used to run the pumps. 

The pumps are needed because the country west of the Colorado River is much higher than the river itself. The highest part of the aqueduct is about onefourth of a mile above the river. This place is near the middle of the aqueduct. So the first half of the aqueduct runs up hill, and the second half runs down hill. 

At the eastern end of the aqueduct a dam is being built across the Colorado River. This dam is called 

Parker Dam. It will be less than half as high as 

Boulder Dam. It will form a large lake from which water will be pumped into the aqueduct.

OUR AMERICA 

A dam is also being built at the other end of the aqueduct. It is known as the Cajalco Dam. Here, too, a great lake will be formed as the water pours in from the aqueduct. The water stored in this lake will be used by the cities as it is needed. 

Each part of the aqueduct is a big job by itself. 

From the very beginning a large number of men was needed. In 1936, more than sixteen thousand persons were helping in some way to build the great aqueduct. 

Most of these workers came from the cities and towns of southern California.

Of course, many different kinds of workers are needed. There are men who dig tunnels and run machines, and haul supplies. There are office workers, and cooks, and doctors, and engineers. Some of the workers are carefully-trained men of science. Others are men who only know how to work with their hands. But all of them have a part in the building of the aqueduct.

Many of the workers live in the camps that were built along the way. They eat their meals in large dining rooms, or mess halls. They sleep in rooms that are air-cooled. If someone becomes sick or gets hurt, he is taken to a hospital in the camp. Without good living conditions, the men could not do so much work. 

The man who is in charge of the whole job is Frank E. Weymouth. He is the chief engineer and also the general manager. His office is in Los Angeles. For more than forty years now he has been working as an

engineer, but the Colorado River aqueduct is his biggest job.

Frank Weymouth was born in Maine in 1872, three years after another great engineer finished his part of the Pacific Railroad. When Mr. Weymouth was twenty-four years old, he graduated from the University of Maine. Here he was trained to be an engineer.

Since that time he has worked on many jobs in many places. He helped to build a water system for 

Boston and some other cities. He worked for a while in Winnipeg, Canada. Then he went down to Central

America for the United States Government. Here he studied how a canal might be built across the country of Nicaragua. The canal was to join the 

Atlantic Ocean and the Pacific Ocean.

A few years later a canal was built across the country of Panama instead. Theodore Roosevelt was

President when this canal was started. Perhaps some day the United States will build another canal to join the two oceans. If this is done, it will almost be sure to cross the country of Nicaragua. Perhaps it will even follow the route which Mr. Weymouth and others found long ago.

Mr. Weymouth worked with the United States 

Government for a long time. He helped to build some of the great dams in the western states. One of these dams was the Arrowrock Dam in Idaho. He also helped to make the plans for Boulder Dam.

OUR AMERICA 

In 1929, he was hired by the city of Los Angeles. 

He became the chief engineer in charge of the water works. One part of his work was to make plans for the Colorado River Aqueduct. When the cities at last began to build the aqueduct, he was made the chief engineer.

And so the great man-made river is creeping across the desert and through the mountains. Before long the people of Los Angeles will be using water that comes from snow banks hundreds of miles away.

But without the training and ideas of men like Mr. 

Weymouth such wonderful work could not be done. 3.

In another part of California an old, old dream has come true. The waters of beautiful San Francisco Bay have been conquered. A great bridge has at last been built across the famous bay. 

Perhaps all the world now knows about the San Francisco-Oakland Bay Bridge. It joins together the cities of San Francisco and Oakland. San Francisco is on the western side of the bay. Oakland and many other fine cities are on the eastern side. 

The story of the bridge which now joins these cities is a long one. It begins with the place where San 

Francisco was built. The city of San Francisco stands on a narrow neck of land with water on three sides. Only from the south can the city be reached by land.

Courtesy, Calif. Toll Bridge Authority

The San Francisco-Oakland Bay Bridge. Below is the city of 

San Francisco. Across the bay are Oakland and other cities.

OUR AMERICA 

To the west of San Francisco is the mighty Pacific

Ocean, and on the east is the bay. To the north is the famous Golden Gate, the entrance from the ocean into the bay. All of this gives San Francisco one of the best harbors in the world. Ships from near and far sail in and out of this wonderful harbor. 

This trade has helped to make San Francisco a great city. But it did not keep the city from being cut off. Highways and railroads from the north and east could not run into the city. There was no way to get across the great bay except by boat. 

In the old days people often had to walk or ride many miles around the bay to get from San Francisco to some place on the other side. Later small boats began to carry people across. Then as the cities around the bay grew larger and larger, great ferry boats were used. Many of the people who worked in 

San Francisco built their homes in Oakland. 

Back and forth across the bay went the busy ferry boats. They carried thousands of people and automobiles every day, and above each boat flew a flock of white sea gulls. Now and then they would stop following the boats long enough to dive down to the bay for a scrap of food. Soon they would return to their place above the boats.

The ferry boats gave good service, but San Francisco was still cut off from its neighbors. For many years the people of the city had been looking at their

beautiful bay. Then their eyes would lift to the cities and the mainland on the other side. "If only a bridge could be built across the bay," they would say. "Yes, that would be fine," someone would answer, "but it can't be done. The bay is too deep. It is too far across." 

Of course, a great many men thought about a bridge across the bay. One of them was Leland Stanford. He helped to build the western part of the 

Pacific Railroad. About a year before the railroad was finished and the golden spike was driven, he said, "A bridge should be built over the bay. Then San 

Francisco could be at the end of the railroad." 

Nothing came of his idea, and again the years went by. From time to time someone would talk about building a bridge, but no one did anything about it. Most of the people still thought that it could never be done. 

In 1928, the State of California hired a new highway engineer. His name was Charles H. Purcell. For many years he had been building bridges and roads along the Columbia River in Oregon. One day in 

1929, he heard some talk about a bridge over San 

Francisco Bay. "Why don't we go ahead and build that bridge?" he asked.

The cities of San Francisco and Oakland were interested. The state of California was also willing to

OUR AMERICA 

look into the matter. In October, 1929, a group of men were chosen to study the problem of building a bridge over the bay. Charles H. Purcell was made a member of this group or committee. 

One of the first jobs was to find out what the bottom of the bay was like. How deep was the water in those parts of the bay where the bridge might be built? Was the rock on the bottom solid enough to hold the weight of a great bridge?

While men of science were answering these and other questions, Mr. Purcell began to make plans for the bridge. Many different plans were carefully worked out. Each plan took a great deal of time. 

First it had to be worked out in the minds of the engineers. Then it had to be put down on paper. 

Drawings were made of every part of the bridge. 

Not a single thing could be overlooked. The bridge had to be built on paper before it could be built over the bay. It took almost as long to plan the bridge as it took to build it. 

In 1931, the committee adopted the plan which Mr. 

Purcell and his helpers had finally worked out. The state of California decided to go ahead and build the bridge. In 1933, money to pay for the bridge was borrowed from the United States Government. Mr. 

Purcell had already been chosen as the chief engineer. 

When these things happened, the people of San Francisco and Oakland began to breathe a little bit easier. It looked as if the bridge was coming at last.

The people who rode back and forth on the ferry boats began to watch for signs of work. They did not have long to wait. In July, 1933, work on the bridge was started.

As the months went by the engineers won battle after battle with the bay. Perhaps the hardest job was to build the great concrete piers below the water. 

These piers went down through the water and mud until they stood upon solid bedrock. In some places the water and mud was more than two hundred feet deep. Never before had a bridge been built in water so deep as this.

When the piers were finished, giant steel towers began to rise high above the bay. Then, slowly but surely, the rest of the bridge was built from tower to tower. Day after day and night after night the work went on. When one group of men came off the job, another would take its place. Even while the cities were asleep, the bridge was being built. 

More than twelve thousand men worked on the bridge. Hundreds of them took great risks every time they went to work. If they slipped and fell into the bay, they were sure to lose their lives. There were many dangers to face. Sometimes the rain and the fog would make the steel framework of the bridge very slippery.

At other times the sunshine was so bright it was hard for the men to see. Then, too, there were windy days when even the mighty steel towers would sway

OUR AMERICA 

back and forth. It took men with courage and strength to build the bridge. Without such men, the great bay would never have been conquered. 

In November, 1936, the bridge was ready for use.

A steady stream of automobiles began to move across it. Almost three and a half years had passed since the first work on the bridge had been started. It was a short time in which to build so large a bridge. 

The San Francisco-Oakland Bay Bridge is the largest in the world. No other bridge is even half so large. It is more than eight miles long. It crosses

The San Francisco-Oakland Bay Bridge compared with some of the world's largest bridges about four and a half miles of open water. The main part of the bridge is about four times as long as the 

George Washington Bridge over the Hudson River in New York City.

There are two decks on the Bay Bridge. The upper deck will be used only for automobiles. It is large enough for six lanes of cars. The lanes are carefully marked just as they are on a good highway. At night

the bridge is so well lighted that drivers do not need their car lights.

The lower deck will be used for trains and trucks. 

There is room for two railroad or street car tracks and three truck lanes. Special streamlined trains are being built to run over the bridge. They will be run by electric power. The lower deck of the bridge will be ready for use sometime in 1937.

About half way across the bay the bridge passes through a tunnel in a small island. To the north of this island, a man-made island is rising above the waters of the bay. Before long many beautiful buildings will be put up on this island. In 1939, a great world's fair will be held here. When the fair is over, the man-made island will be used as an airport for all kinds of airplanes.

The cost of building the Bay Bridge will be paid by the people who use it. A toll is charged for every car that drives over the bridge. This toll has now been set at fifty cents. When the bridge has been paid for, it will be free to all who wish to use it. 

Great celebrations were held in San Francisco and

Oakland when the bridge was opened for use. There were long parades and cheering crowds in each city. 

There were beautiful displays of fireworks. Some of the warships of the United States Navy came to join in the celebration. 

On November 12, 1936, the governor of California and Charles H. Purcell and many others gathered on

OUR AMERICA 

the Oakland end of the bridge. A silver chain with a golden link was stretched across the bridge. A few speeches were made by important men. Then the governor cut through the golden link in the chain. 

The bridge was open.

When the chain had been cut, more than two hundred airplanes began to fly above the bay. Ships and boats sailed about in the water below the bridge. At each end of the bridge a long stream of cars began to drive across the bay. The dream of many years had at last come true. 4.

A few miles from the San Francisco-Oakland Bay Bridge, another great bridge is being built. It rises high into the sky for all the world to see and admire. 

It is a bridge across the Golden Gate. 

The Golden Gate is a narrow arm of the sea. It is about one mile wide and three miles long. It leads from the Pacific Ocean into the quiet waters of San 

Francisco Bay. Every ship that comes into the bay must pass through the Golden Gate.

For a long time the people of San Francisco have talked about a bridge across the Golden Gate. It would join the city with the beautiful country to the north of the Golden Gate. The famous Redwood Highway could then run straight into San Francisco. This is the highway that passes through the tall Redwood forests on the way to Oregon and Washington.

The story of the Golden Gate Bridge really began about twenty years ago. In many ways it is the story of a man who was a dreamer as well as a great engineer. It is the story of a man who wanted to build the biggest thing in the world. That man is Joseph B. Strauss.

He was born in January, 1870. His father was a painter and a writer. Joseph Strauss grew up in Cincinnati, Ohio, across the river from the place where 

Dan Beard and his friends played. He went to grade school, high school, and then to the University of Cincinnati. 

One day when Joseph Strauss was a student at the university he decided to try out for the football team. 

He was a little fellow and very light. In those days no one but a big man made the football team. The game was much rougher than it is now. At the end of the first practice, Joseph Strauss had to be sent to the hospital. 

Courtesy, Metropolitan Water 

District, So. Calif. 

Frank E. Weymouth 

Courtesy, Calif. Toll

Bridge Authority Charles H. Purcell 

Brown Bros. 

Joseph B. Strauss

OUR AMERICA 

"You're a good sport," he was told, "but you can't play football. You're too little."

So Joseph Strauss began to make plans. He decided to be an engineer and build bridges. Even if he was too little to play football, he could still do big things. Perhaps some day he would build the biggest bridge in the world.

In the years that followed he became one of the best known engineers in the United States. He planned and built all kinds of bridges. Many of these bridges were built in different parts of the United States. Others were built in Russia, in Japan, in 

China, in Egypt, and many other countries. Some of these bridges were very large, but Joseph Strauss still wanted to build one that was bigger than any of them. 

Then one year he made a trip to San Francisco.

He stood on a high hill and looked out over the Golden Gate. He knew that some day the people of San Francisco would need a bridge over that entrance to their great bay. Engineers and others said it "couldn't be done." But Joseph Strauss knew that he could do it.

He talked with the engineer for the city of San 

Francisco. "Yes," said the engineer, "a bridge across the Golden Gate would be a wonderful thing. I have asked many bridge builders to work out a plan for one. They have all given it up as a bad job. They say it can't be done." "Why?" asked Joseph Strauss.

"Well," answered the city engineer, "the Golden

Gate is too wide and too deep. Even if a bridge could be built, it would cost too much money. But if you have a plan, I wish you would go ahead and work it out. I certainly would like to see a bridge over the 

Golden Gate."

So Joseph Strauss began to make plans for a bridge that would cross the Golden Gate. The city of

San Francisco hired men to find out what the bottom of the Golden Gate was like. They found a great shelf of solid rock about twelve hundred feet out from the south shore. At this place the water was only about one hundred feet deep. 

At last Mr. Strauss finished his plans. A concrete pier would be built upon that shelf of solid rock. Then a great steel tower would rise from the pier. Another tower would be built upon the shore on the other side of the Golden Gate. Between the two towers would hang the great bridge.

It was a daring plan, perhaps a little too daring. 

Many people were against it. The distance between the two towers was more than four thousand feet. 

No single bridge span this long had ever been built before. There was not one even half as long. 

Then, too, there was that pier below the water. 

How could anyone build a strong pier way out in the 

Golden Gate? The moving ocean water would surely keep the pier from being built. No, it didn't seem likely that the Golden Gate could be conquered. And

OUR AMERICA 

yet, wasn't this Joseph Strauss a famous engineer who had built dozens of great bridges? 

More than ten years went by before the people decided to build the bridge. In 1930, San Francisco and the counties to the north formed the Golden Gate Bridge and Highway District. A plan was also worked out to raise money for the bridge. Two years later the people of San Francisco voted in favor of the plan. 

At last Joseph Strauss was free to go ahead. In

January, 1933, work was started on both sides of the Golden Gate. The first big job was to build the concrete pier below the water. A long platform was built from the shore out to the place where the pier was to stand. Machinery for use in building the pier was put on this platform.

For a while everything went well. Then one fall day a fierce storm arose. Great waves rolled in through the Golden Gate. They washed away the work that had been done on the pier. Some of the machinery was also swept into the sea. 

The men had to start the work all over. A short time later another storm came up. Great waves again rolled in through the Golden Gate. They swept everything before them. The work on the pier was again destroyed. A large part of the long working platform was also wrecked.

These storms were discouraging, but Joseph Strauss was not willing to quit. A new and stronger

platform was built. Once more work was started on the pier. This time the workers were successful. 

In January, 1935, the great concrete pier was ready. Almost two years had been spent in building it. Now that the battle with the sea had been won, the worst part of the work was over. At last Joseph 

Strauss could go home and catch up on his sleep. 

By this time the steel tower on the north side of the 

Golden Gate had been finished. Before long, another tower was built upon the concrete pier on the south side. The rest of the bridge is now being built between the two towers. The work will be finished sometime in 1937. 

The Golden Gate Bridge is next to the largest bridge in the world. The San Francisco-Oakland Bay

Bridge comes first. But not even the Bay Bridge has so long a span as the Golden Gate Bridge. On no other bridge is the distance from tower to tower so great. 

The Golden Gate Bridge. On no other bridge is the distance from tower to tower so great.

OUR AMERICA 

Like the bridge across the bay, the Golden Gate Bridge will be paid for by those who use it. The money spent for the bridge will be paid back in about thirty-five years. Then it will be free.

One day, very soon now, the bridge will be opened for use. A stream of automobiles will run across the 

Golden Gate. When that happens another great dream will have come true. 

For twenty years Joseph Strauss has dreamed about a bridge over the Golden Gate. During all that time he has fought and worked and worried. When he was beaten, he fought again until he finally won. 

The great bridge will be a monument to the courage and imagination of Joseph Strauss, a little man who wanted to build a big bridge. But it will be even more than this. It will be a monument to all of the men who believe and say, "It can be done."

XXV 

"He has helped to build an air route across the Pacific Ocean."

Many great changes have taken place in America during the last few years. Perhaps one of the most important is the speed with which people can now go from place to place.

It is only a little more than thirty years ago since the airplane was invented. Today great airliners fly over regular routes and cross the country in less than a day.

Even more wonderful is the way in which the airplane has conquered the world's largest ocean. 

While Frank E. Weymouth, Charles H. Purcell, and Joseph B. Strauss were doing their great work, Edwin C. Musick was helping to build a new air route across the Pacific Ocean. 

A fresh wind whipped across San Francisco Bay. 

The afternoon sun still hung high in the sky. Below on the blue-green water, a beautiful flying boat headed into the wind. The hum of its motors changed to a roar.

For a few seconds it raced swiftly through the water, leaving behind a trail of white foam. Then it 357

OUR AMERICA 

rose smoothly into the air and climbed high above the bay. Over the great bridges and out through the 

Golden Gate it flew. Its silver nose was pointed straight into the path of the golden sun.

The famous China Clipper was again on its way across the broad Pacific Ocean. Every week one of the flying Clipper ships leaves San Francisco Bay.

And every week one of them returns from the other side of the world. On each trip they carry a load of passengers and mail. 

Since November, 1935, the flying Clippers have been making regular trips back and forth across the mighty Pacific. They fly between Alameda, California, and the Philippine Islands. Alameda is a city near Oakland on the eastern side of San Francisco Bay. The Philippine Islands are off the coast of Asia near the countries of China and Japan. 

It is only a little more than thirty years since the airplane was invented. One cold December day in 

1903, at Kitty Hawk, North Carolina, two brothers tried out a strange machine. On that day Wilbur and 

Orville Wright made the first successful trip in an airplane. 

Four different times their airplane rose into the air. Once it stayed up almost a minute and flew several hundred feet. From that small beginning grew the great air lines of today. Orville Wright, who is still living, could now ride hour after hour and fly several thousand miles in the giant Clippers that cross the Pacific.

Courtesy, Transcontinental & Western Air, Inc. 

An airplane landing at night at the Kansas City Airport. Today great airliners fly over regular routes and cross the country in less than a day.

OUR AMERICA 

Clyde 

SUNDERLAND 

Courtesy, Pan American Airways 

The Clipper ships fly over the great San Francisco-Oakland

Bay Bridge. Below is the man-made island on which the 

San Francisco World's Fair will be held in 1939.

This new air route across the Pacific Ocean is one of the most wonderful things in aviation today. The story of how it was built is even more wonderful. It is the story of a daring but carefully made plan, and of how it succeeded when many men worked together. And, of course, it is the story of the men themselves, of men such as Captain Edwin C. Musick. 

The airplane was ten years old when Edwin Musick learned to fly. He was only nineteen, but flying was a young man's work then, just as it is now. 

It was filled with danger because airplanes were still small and poorly built. A man risked his life almost every time he went up in one of them.

It took a steady nerve and a great deal of skill to handle the airplanes of that time. Edwin Musick had plenty of both, and so he learned to fly, and to fly well. He was among the very first to learn. Even 

Richard E. Byrd did not learn to fly until 1919, six years later.

In 1917, the United States went into the World War. By this time Edwin Musick had become an expert flyer. During the war years he served first with the army and then with the navy. It was his job to train fliers for the army and navy. Many good aviators have received part of their training from Edwin Musick.

Several years later he became the chief pilot on the first air line between Florida and Cuba. Here he learned a great deal about flying over the ocean. He

OUR AMERICA 

made trip after trip in the flying boats, or seaplanes, that were used on this route. Three of the flying boats were named the Pinta, the Niña, and the Santa Maria. More than four hundred years had passed since ships with these same names had first visited 

Cuba. 

Edwin Musick's next job was with a little air line between Philadelphia and Washington, D. C. After 

Courtesy, Pan American Airways 

Edwin C. Musick

a while he moved back to Florida. He bought some flying boats and went into business for himself. Then, in 1927, he went to work for a new company known as the Pan American Airways. His first job was to fly over the ninety miles of water between Key West, Florida, and Havana, Cuba.

The new company grew rapidly. Before long it had a whole network of air routes between the United States and the countries to the south. From Florida there was a route to South America. From Texas there was a route to Mexico, Central America, and the Panama Canal. There were also routes between many of the countries in South America. 

Edwin Musick liked his new work, and the company liked him. In 1930, he was made the chief pilot. on the routes between Florida and South America.

From the very beginning he had been one of their most trusted men. If there was a fine new ship to try out, he was given the job. If there was a new route to open, he made the first trip.

In 1935, the Pan American Airways began to build an air route across the Pacific Ocean. The news made people open their eyes in wonder. How could any company hope to build an air line over an ocean as large as the Pacific? While people talked and wondered, the Pan American Company went ahead with its plans. And as one part of those plans, Captain 

Edwin C. Musick was sent to the shores of San Francisco Bay.

OUR AMERICA 

It was seven years since he had started flying on an air line over ninety miles of water. Now he was to begin a new air route over nearly nine thousand miles of ocean. During those seven years he had made more than eleven hundred trips between the United States and the countries to the south. Each time he had brought his ship and passengers back safely.

For that matter, Edwin Musick has never had a serious accident in more than twenty years of flying. 

No passenger has ever been hurt in one of his planes. 

It is a record of which he can well be proud. Of course, there is a very good reason for this wonderful record. 

Before Captain Musick starts on a trip he carefully inspects his ship. He goes over it from one end to the other. Everything must be in perfect working order before he is willing to take off. "Ed Musick is a regular old grandmother when it comes to inspecting his ships," said one of his friends one time. "But that's why he never gets into any flying trouble. His ships are always in shape to stand by him and his passengers and crew." 

Like all good pilots, he does not believe in stunt flying or in taking chances. One time he was flying a new Clipper ship on its first trip to South America.

He had thirty important newspaper men on board. 

A great welcome had been planned for them in Rio de Janeiro, the capital city of Brazil.

Before the ship reached the city a heavy fog came up. Captain Musick refused to take the chance of landing in the fog on the busy harbor of Rio de Janeiro. He brought his ship down safely a few miles from the city. He knew that this would spoil the plans for the celebration that evening. But he also knew that the safety of his passengers was more important than getting them to dinner on time. 

The Pan American Company sent many men besides Edwin Musick to the shores of San Francisco Bay. There were engineers, radio men, pilots, and other carefully-trained workers. They began at once to get an airport ready at Alameda, California. This was the first step in the plan for the air line across the Pacific.

The plan was to fly the ocean in five long hops. The first hop was from Alameda to the Hawaiian Islands. 

From Hawaii the flying boats would go to Midway

Island, then to Wake Island, next to Guam, and finally from Guam to the Philippine Islands. Four of these islands belong to the United States. The 

Philippines were once a United States colony, but plans have been made for them to become an independent nation.

This was the route over which the Clipper ships were to fly. But first it was necessary to have a modern airport at each island. This was not much of a problem at Honolulu, Hawaii, or at Manila in the

Philippine Islands. Both of these cities had good harbors that could be used as a seaplane base for the great flying boats. Then, too, these islands were well settled and it was fairly easy to get workers and supplies.

But at the other three islands the airports had to be built up from nothing. Only ten people lived on 

Midway Island, and no one lived on Wake Island. 

Almost everything that was needed for the airports had to be shipped out from the United States. Early in 1935, a steamship was sent from San Francisco with a big load of materials. More than one hundred men were also sent along. 

First they went to Midway Island, then to Wake

Island, and finally to Guam. At each island they fixed up a safe landing place for the flying boats.

Then they put up radio stations, buildings and everything else that was needed for an airport. When the

airports were ready a small crew of men was left behind to take care of them.

Later the steamship made a second trip with another load of materials. This time the men built a complete modern hotel on each of the three lonely islands. The work of building these mid-ocean air bases was finished in less than two years. It was indeed a wonderful piece of work. And yet, it was only one part of the whole large plan of an air route across the Pacific Ocean. Another part of that plan called for the building of some new flying boats. 

As early as 1931, the Pan American Airways had decided what kind of ships it needed. The officers of the company knew that an ordinary airplane would not do. They also knew that flying boats larger than any ever built would be necessary. So they went to talk with the leading airplane builders in the United States. "We need ships that can fly three thousand miles without stopping," they said. "These ships must be able to carry enough gasoline so that they can fly this far even when the wind is against them. It is twentyfour hundred miles between California and Hawaii, and we must be able to cross safely at all times. "We want ships that can carry a large load of passengers and mail. Unless we carry large loads we cannot make expenses. The ships must also be fast. 

We want them to fly at a regular speed of one hundred and fifty miles an hour. Then, too, we want them

OUR AMERICA 

to be seaworthy in case they are forced down while flying over the ocean."

The airplane builders shook their heads. Most of them said it couldn't be done. Only two men were willing to try, and they each agreed to build three giant flying boats. The result of their work was the famous flying Clipper ships which now cross oceans so easily and swiftly. They were given their name in honor of the old "Yankee Clippers" which sailed the 

China Seas almost a hundred years ago.

The flying boats now used on the Pacific route are named the China Clipper, Philippine Clipper, and

Hawaii Clipper. Each of them weighs more than twenty-five tons. They are about ninety feet long, and their wing span is about one hundred thirty feet. 

The ships have four powerful motors, but they can carry a full load with only three of them running. 

They can fly as fast as one hundred and eighty miles an hour, but their regular speed is about thirty miles less. There is room for forty-three day passengers on board in addition to a load of mail. For night trips there are eighteen berths in which passengers can sleep as comfortably as they would on a train. On long ocean trips the Clippers cannot carry a full load of passengers because the gasoline load is so heavy. It takes a crew of six men to fly a Clipper ship.

The China Clipper and its sister ships are the largest flying boats on any airline in the world. One of

Courtesy, Pan American Airways

A comfortable corner in one of the flying Clipper ships the men who helped to plan them was Charles A. 

Lindbergh. The story of how they were built is an exciting one, even though it was only one part of a great plan. Without such ships the airline across the 

Pacific would not have been possible.

While the flying Clippers were being built in the eastern part of the United States, and the airports were being built out in the Pacific, Edwin Musick was busy with his share of work. In the spring of 

1935, he flew to Hawaii and back. Several months later he made a trip to Midway Island and returned to Alameda. Then another pilot made round trips to 

Wake and Guam.

OUR AMERICA 

On these trips Captain Musick and the other pilots explored the new air route. They made charts and maps for each part of the route over which they flew. 

They kept careful records of what the weather was like. They learned about winds, and fog, and storms, and how to make the best of them. They studied the stars and practiced flying by them. 

When one of the new airports was ready, they tested it out. They also tested the instruments in their ship. Sometimes they covered the windows of the cockpit with curtains. For hour upon hour they practiced flying blind with nothing but instruments to guide them. Each time they made a perfect crossing on the long ocean hops.

These exploring trips were made in a ship named the Pan American Clipper, but the crew called it the flying gas-tank. It was a little smaller than the Clipper ships which are now used on the Pacific route. On these first trips the crew was often changed. In this way many different men were given training in flying over the ocean. By the time regular service began three full crews were ready to man the giant flying boats.

The wonderful way in which the Pacific air route was explored and tested brought new fame to Captain Edwin Musick. A few months later he was given the Harmon Trophy for 1935 as the "world's outstanding aviator." This is perhaps the greatest honor that any aviator can receive. Only two other Ameri-

cans have so far been given the Harmon Trophy. 

They were Charles A. Lindbergh and Wiley Post. 

On November 22, 1935, thousands of people gathered at the airport in Alameda. They came to take part in the great celebration that marked the beginning of air mail service across the Pacific. On the western shores of San Francisco Bay another large crowd waited to see the flying Clipper pass out through the Golden Gate. In every part of the world people listened in their homes while the magic of radio brought them the story of what was happening. 

The China Clipper rested gently in the water. It was ready to fly the ocean with its heavy load of mail. 

While the excited crowd watched, the crew of seven men moved along the narrow catwalk and stepped on board. The last man to go on board was Captain 

Edwin C. Musick. In a few seconds he was seated in the cockpit. Over the radio in the cockpit came the voice of the president of the Pan American Airways. "China Clipper, are you ready?" "China Clipper, Captain Musick. Standing by for orders, sir," was the answer.

Then over the radio came reports from each of the island air bases. "Pan American Airways Ocean Air

Base Number One-Honolulu, Hawaii. Standing by for orders,” was the first report. A few seconds later the other airports had flashed the message that they, too, were ready and standing by. As soon as these

OUR AMERICA 

reports were received the president of the company spoke again. "Captain Musick, you have your sailing orders. 

Cast off and depart for Manila."

The engines of the China Clipper began to roar. 

The great ship moved slowly out into the bay. On shore the band began to play the Star Spangled Banner. Before the band had finished playing the flying

Clipper was in the air. Thousands cheered and waved as it flew out through the Golden Gate. In a few minutes it was out of sight among the fleecy clouds. 

The whole world watched that first wonderful flight. Millions of people silently joined in the cheers as the China Clipper was welcomed at one island after another. With the China Clipper, and its fine crew, went the world's wishes for fair flying and happy landings.

Six days later the flying Clipper swooped down over Manila and came to rest upon the bay. It arrived on time almost to the minute. The flying time from 

Alameda to Manila was sixty hours. The fastest ships on the ocean make the crossing in twenty-one days. That swift trip of the China Clipper was indeed something to stir the imagination and make hearts beat a little faster.

In the months that followed the Clipper ships made trip after trip over the Pacific skyway. They flew through driving rain, and heavy fog, and strong

wind. They made each crossing safely, and most of them were on time. The world still wondered a little at the success of the new air line. But to Captain 

Musick and the other pilots each crossing was just another job.

After eleven months of mail service, the Clipper ships began to carry passengers across the Pacific. 

On October 21, 1936, the Hawaii Clipper left Alameda with the first load of passengers. Captain Edwin C. Musick was again in command as the great ship took off for Manila. Once more he blazed a new trail in the history of flying. 

Every few weeks now, a bell rings in the waiting room of the Pan American airport on the shores of 

San Francisco Bay. A voice comes through a loudspeaker in the waiting room. "Members of the crew of the China Clipper will please go aboard."

A little later the bell in the waiting room rings again. The voice from the loud-speaker comes once more. "Passengers for the China Clipper will please go aboard." 

And so they step on board, a little bit nervous, perhaps, and more than a little bit excited. After all, it isn't everyday that one flies the Pacific. Nor is it everyday, or even every year, that something so wonderful as the Pacific air route is added to the story of our America.
